# A-2 Project Executive Summary (max 300 words)

Viet Nam is particularly vulnerable to climate change and already impacted by more irregular and intense climate variability and change. Two of the regions most vulnerable to climate risks affecting smallholder farmers are the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast regions, where women and ethnic minority farmers face challenges in accessing water for agricultural production with longer periods of severe water scarcity during the dry season and increased frequency of extreme weather events such as droughts and floods. These factors have resulted in declines in yields and incomes partially harmful to small-scale farmers vulnerable to reduced water availability on rainfed agriculture.

The objective of this project is to empower vulnerable smallholders in five provinces of the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast regions of Viet Nam – particularly women and ethnic minority farmers - to manage increasing climate risks through implementation of two linked outputs. The first output is to increase access to climate-smart water for vulnerable smallholder farmers for climate-resilient agricultural production in the face of climate-induced variability and change, including droughts and floods. The second output is to provide training on climate information, technologies, and practices for climate-resilient water and agricultural management. While this project will use an integrated approach to address these challenges, it will also build capacity among farmers through the use of GCF and co-financing resources to build the capacities of all farmers in climate vulnerable areas, such as the project will reach not only direct individual beneficiaries but also indirect beneficiaries.

The project was developed as part of an integrated programme funded by multiple sources, as envisaged by the Government of Viet Nam. The programme aims at increasing the resilience of the agricultural sector focusing on Vietnam’s Central Highland and South-Central Coastal Regions. In alignment with this goal, a multi-stakeholder approach to climate-resilient agricultural development is envisioned and supported through an integrated approach to agricultural resilience building. This includes: a) strengthening the capacity of smallholder farmers in five provinces to adopt climate-resilient agricultural practices; b) increasing access to climate-smart water for agricultural production by improving infrastructure, enhancing water security and guaranteeing access; scaling up adoption and application of climate-resilient agricultural technologies and practices through training and extension services; c) strengthening the capacity of smallholder farmers in five provinces to access to market and credit. This approach directly addresses climate risks while also establishing or strengthening institutional capacities for long-term multi-stakeholder support to vulnerable smallholders.

This project will be implemented by the Department of Agriculture and Rural Development as a lead most vulnerable Districts and Communes of the five target provinces of Viet Nam by complementing and enhancing the activities and results of the Government’s NDC. The project will contribute to the achievement of the following objectives: a) improve infrastructure financed through a USD 89.59 million loan from the Asian Development Bank, as well as USD 22.06 million in co-financing resources; b) increase access to climate-smart water for agricultural production by improving infrastructure for poor/low-income smallholders, with a particular focus on ethnic minority and women farmers; and b) to strengthen institutional capacities of Districts and Communes in five provinces through training and extension services and use of agro-ecological information for climate risk management, and multi-stakeholder coordination for climate-resilient value chain development through climate innovation platforms.

This project is aligned with the Government of Viet Nam’s Nationally Determined Contribution (NDCs): These include support livelihoods and production processes that are appropriate under climate change conditions, including increasing access to climate-smart water for agricultural production by improving infrastructure using indigenous knowledge, prioritizing the most vulnerable communities; implement integrated water resources

# B.3 Description of Financial Elements of the Project

## The Government of Vietnam (GoV) is committed to support smallholder farmers – particularly ethnic minority and women farmers – in the most vulnerable provinces of Vietnam to adapt to increasing rainfall variability and frequency, as well as to improve their livelihoods through improved water management practices that enhance climate resilience of investments being made under the Water Efficiency Improving in Drought-Affected Provinces (WEDAP) project. The WEDAP project is implemented by the GoV with co-financing from the World Bank (WB) and Asian Development Bank (ADB), as well as co-financing of US$ 22,000,000 from the Government of Vietnam.

## GCF grant

The GCF grant will be used to overcome barriers to water security undermined by climate change for poor/near poor farmers by enabling them to make the last-mile connection from their farm plots to the WEDAP primary irrigation infrastructure. The GCF grant will also support capacity development of local government and community infrastructure, poor and near-poor farmers, particularly ethnic minority and women farmers, face insurmountable barriers in accessing water resources for agricultural production. This is especially true for smallholder farmers, while better-off farmers will invest in connecting to the WEDAP infrastructure on their own.

## The GCF grant will furthermore be applied to enhance storage water on rain fed lands for seasonal cropping and improve irrigation management services. ### 1.5. GCF financing will cover the incremental costs of adaptation for poor/earner-for-poor farmers in the WEADAP command areas (including post-WEADAP agricultural practices and cropping systems) necessary to increase climate risk mitigation applications of diversified, integrated agricultural practices and cropping systems, including local community-based advisory services.

All farmers in the WEADAP command areas will benefit from the increased coordination and collaboration amongst all stakeholders involved. This will lead to a more efficient use of resources and an overall improvement in the resilience of the WEADAP/WEADAP investment through the GCF resources.

### 1.6. Additional co-financing will be provided by the Government in the form of additional co-financing of US$ 4,437,435 from the central and provincial budgets to support the multi-pronged approach and holistic investment to enhance the water management infrastructure and increase the resilience of the WEADAP/WEADAP investment. The additional co-financing will be applied under the proposed project for investments in: (i) construction of the last-mile connection and supplementary irrigation through pond storage on rain-fed lands (Activity 1.3); and (ii) improved access to credit and markets (sub-activity 2.2).

### 1.7. In-kind co-financing The MARD and DARDA (provincial Departments of Agriculture and Rural Development) will contribute in kind towards the project implementation. These contributions are expected to be in the form of i) US$4,706.50 for contribution of public land for building new shared community ponds; the locations and sizes of these ponds will be determined by the communities with the assistance of the MARD and DARDA in consultation with local authorities. The ownership of the ponds, post construction and during their lifespan, will be given to the communities. ii) US$ 105,328 for contribution towards the cost of land acquisition for the project as administered by the local authorities; and iii) US$ 465,300 for staff time towards project implementation support, and resources towards project management costs.

### 1.8. Finance for Offtake Management Arrangements (OMA): During the six-year project duration, a total of US$ 919,824 towards OMA costs are expected to be incurred for maintenance of installed last-mile connections (Activity 1.2) and supplementary irrigation through ponds (Activity 1.3). The cost of these activities will be covered by co-financing contribution was vetted through field consultations (Refer to Stakeholder Consultations Report, AnnexXII) and will be supported through cash co-financing by the provincial and district authorities during the six-year project implementation period. The total amount for OMA costs is US$ 919,824 of which US$ 750,360 (81%) was committed as co-financing contribution towards maintenance cost of last-mile connections to the WEADAP systems (Activity 1.2), supplementary irrigation through ponds (Activity 1.3.), and on-farm production activities (Activity 1.4). Yearly payments for OMA costs are estimated at US$ 15,328 of which the provincial authorities will contribute US$ 12,960 while cash-and-kind contributions by communities will be at approximately US$ 4,777,732. Of the total project cost (US$ 1,382,125) would be towards their respective in-kind contribution of US$ 4,777,732. The remaining costs for OMA are estimated to be US$ 609,300 for monitoring, repair and maintenance costs of the water collection system; and US$ 1,862,136 towards maintenance cost of last-mile connections (Activity 1.2) and supplementary irrigation through ponds (Activity 1.3). The efficiency equipment, which have been further detailed in the GIM plan for the project. Finally, provision of adequate staff time to support OMA costs is estimated at US$ 405,867. The total amount for OMA costs are estimated total of US$ 4,642,932 as cash and in-kind contributions for OMA over the entire project duration (16 years) managed by the provincial authorities.

### C.1 Strategic Context

Viet Nam is particularly vulnerable to climate change and already impacted by more irregular and intense climate variability. When considering climate change exposure, sensitivity and the capacity to adapt, the country is consistently classified as under "very high risk" or "severe risk." Every year the country is affected by a range of hydro-meteorological hazards including tropical cyclones, heavy rainfall, flooding, droughts, landslides, heat waves, wind storms, coastal, rivers and floods. Heavy rainfall and landslides in June–December and extreme temperatures during July to September are the major causes of damage. The frequency and intensity of these events have been the main cause of long-term increases in economic losses from weather- and climate-related disasters.

Changes in precipitation are leading to hotter and wetter summer seas and hotter and drier sea summers, resulting in periods of drought and water scarcity. These changes also lead to more intense tropical cyclones, which cause severe water scarcity during their life cycle and increased frequency and intensity of droughts. As a consequence, events such as floods, landslides, and droughts are becoming more frequent and intense.

Viet Nam is particularly vulnerable because it has a large population living in poverty, especially the ethnic minorities accounting for 34.6%–39.1% of the region's population. The Central Highlands are susceptible to changes in water availability in the dry season when there is little rain and low river flow. Only 20% of the land area has access to irrigation, with only 5% of this irrigated land being used for rice cultivation.

The Central Highlands region constitutes Vietnam’s largest perennial crop zone, where smallholders produce a wide range of crops including coffee, tea, rubber, cashew nuts, pepper, tobacco, sugarcane, maize and cassava, chiefly for local consumption, especially by the poorest. Farmers in the region currently intercrop rubber with other crops such as coffee, tea, and pepper to reduce risk from extreme weather events.

The Central Highlands are also vulnerable because of their high vulnerability to climate change-induced price fluctuation. However, under increasingly extreme climate-change-induced droughts, farmers’ coping strategies can be exhausted within a few years. The average depth of the soil in the region is 10–25 cm and up to 30 m in depth. Many farms drill three or four wells but are still unable to obtain sufficient water, augmenting their dependency on rainfall.

Around 48% of the people in the South-Central Coast region of Vietnam rely on agriculture for their livelihoods, with ethnic minorities comprising from 57.7% of the population in Khmer Ho province to 23.1% in Ninh Thuan. Sufficient rainfall is critical for rice production and other crops such as maize, cassava, sugarcane, and pepper during the dry season, the lowest rainfall, and a relatively smaller river system. Only around 30% of agricultural land is irrigated, leaving more than half of the region’s population vulnerable to climate change-induced droughts and extreme events.

It is anticipated that many of the poor-in-the-rural are likely to face food insecurity and increasing poverty.

# Detailed Project Description

## National Climate Change Response

The first national program on climate change in Vietnam, the National Target Program to Respond to Climate Change (NTP-RC), was launched in 2008. In response to the need to mainstream climate change resources into social and economic development while pursuing broader sustainable development and contributing to global efforts to address climate change, the NTP-RC was revised and updated as the National Action Plan on Climate Change 2017–2030 (NAPCC). The NAPCC is a comprehensive national strategy that aims at reducing greenhouse gas emissions from energy production and consumption sectors while enhancing Vietnam’s resilience against climate change. It focuses on five key areas: improving disaster risk reduction, building security and agricultural development, human health, natural resources and ecosystem function, and overall sustainable development. The NTP-RC was revised in 2012 to become the National Action Plan on Climate Change 2012–2030 (NAPCC). The NAPCC is a comprehensive national strategy that aims at reducing greenhouse gas emissions from energy production and consumption sectors while enhancing Vietnam’s resilience against climate change. It focuses on five key areas: improving disaster risk reduction, building security and agricultural development, human health, natural resources and ecosystem function, and overall sustainable development.

## Project Objective Against Baseline

### Climate Change in Vietnam

20. **National Climate Change Response**: The first national program on climate change in Vietnam, the National Target Program to Respond to Climate Change (NTP-RC), was launched in 2008. In response to the need to mainstream climate change resources into social and economic development while pursuing broader sustainable development and contributing to global efforts to address climate change, the NTP-RC was revised and updated as the National Action Plan on Climate Change 2017–2030 (NAPCC). The NAPCC is a comprehensive national strategy that aims at reducing greenhouse gas emissions from energy production and consumption sectors while enhancing Vietnam’s resilience against climate change. It focuses on five key areas: improving disaster risk reduction, building security and agricultural development, human health, natural resources and ecosystem function, and overall sustainable development.

21. To achieve Viet Nam's commitments towards the 2015 Paris Climate Agreement and the country's Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC), the GOV has approved a specific implementation plan in October 2016. The plan includes five components: mitigation; adaptation; human, technological and financial resources for implementation; measurement, reporting and verification of greenhouse gas emissions; and international cooperation. Priority activities for climate change adaptation relevant to this project are, among others, support livelihoods and food security through protection, sustainability management and maintaining agricultural land; restructuring of crop and livestock systems; adopt technology for sustainable agriculture production and the sustainable use of water resources in agriculture; and improve the efficiency of irrigation works.

22. MARD is currently developing a mid-term and long-term strategy for the improvement and restructuring of the irrigation sector in order to optimize the efficiency of irrigation works, contribute to implement National Target Program on Climate Change (NTP-RC) and NAPCC, and support the implementation of climate change services, which integrate approaches to a "smart response to climate change" agriculture following commercial or organic agricultural production. The project will contribute to improving food security through protection, sustainability management and maintaining agricultural land; restructuring of crop and livestock systems; adopt technology for sustainable agriculture production and the sustainable use of water resources in agriculture.

23. **Climate Change Scenarios**: Climate change scenarios are projections of future climate conditions based on various assumptions about greenhouse gas emissions and other factors that influence climate patterns. These scenarios provide a range of possible outcomes, allowing decision-makers to plan for different potential futures. The project will contribute to improving food security through protection, sustainability management and maintaining agricultural land; restructuring of crop and livestock systems; adopt technology for sustainable agriculture production and the sustainable use of water resources in agriculture.

### Climate Change Impacts on South-Central Coast

#### Current Observed Changes in Climate and Weather-related Events in the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast, Based on Available Data for the Period 1960-2015

The following trends and patterns are observed:

**Temperature:**

* In the Central Highlands, there is a significant trend of about 0.04 to 0.3°C temperature increase per decade.
* Minimum temperatures increased by approximately 0.18°C per decade in Da Lat (South-Central Highlands) to 0.65°C per decade in Dalat City.
* Maximum daily temperature has increased significantly, with a trend of about 0.08 to 1.6°C increase per decade.

**Precipitation:**

* In the Central Highlands there is an overall slight increase in annual rainfall but with variation per location, some showing significant increases while others show little change.
* There are, however, increased extreme precipitation events, with a trend of about 20 to 45 percent respectively. The number of very wet days has also increased significantly by up to 2 days per decade.

**Drought and Risk:**

* For the entire Central Highlands region, the most frequent disaster events have been heavy rainfall, floods, storms, and landslides.
* The South-Central Coast is more exposed than before due to sea level rise and erosion.
* Factors causing droughts in this region are reduced dry season rainfall and a longer but usual dry season.

**Drought Affected Provinces:**

* Drought events have been food, drought, heat waves, strong winds.

# Detailed Project Description

## Future climate scenarios for the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast indicate the following:

### Temperature increase
29. In the Central Highlands, an increase in overall temperatures is expected of about 1.2 to 2.6°C by mid-century and 2.2 to 4.5°C by end-of-century for the higher (RCP8.5) greenhouse gas scenarios. Average temperature increase will be less than this in the lower lying parts of the region, except in the coastal areas where average temperatures above 35°C is projected to increase by 15 to 20 days a year.

### Changing precipitation patterns
30. In the Central Highlands, an 8 to 20 percent increase in rainfall in the wet season - which will start earlier and end later - and 10 percent decrease and more irregular rainfall in the dry season are predicted. Larger variations between seasons of rainfall intensity are expected for the southern parts of the Central Highlands but little change in the northern parts.

### Drought and flood risk
31. The magnitude and frequency of floods during wet seasons are expected to increase in the Central Highlands due to increased precipitation, while the number of very strong typhoons is projected to become more severe due to rising temperatures and rainfall deficits in the dry season, particularly in the northern part of the region.

### Climate change impacts on water availability
32. Key impacts of climate change on the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast relate to water availability and its effect on agricultural productivity. Water resources will be impacted adversely due to increased evaporation, reduced rainfall intensity during wet seasons, and more irregular rainfall in dry seasons, leading to damage to existing infrastructure.

### Coastal areas
33. Coastal areas are expected to face increased salinity intrusion.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROGRAM | PAGE 1 OF 102

### Climate Change and Its Impacts on Agriculture

#### Groundwater Extraction and Water Availability

Groundwater extraction is expected to increase, leading to a decline in water availability for rain-fed crops in upland areas with limited storage options. This will further exacerbate the situation during extreme droughts and in the hottest scenario when rainfall declines and evaporation increases.

#### Crop Productivity and Vulnerability

Crop productivity will be most impacted by reduced water availability, especially in regions where irrigation is not feasible or affordable. The main crops currently cultivated in both regions, vulnerable to climate change impacts, are rice (mainly paddy rice) and maize. Rice production is projected to decline from 40% to 70 percent less water available for crops, which will particularly affect perennial crops such as coffee and pepper, and annual crops such as rice and maize.

#### Vulnerability of Indigenous Ethnic Minorities

Indigenous ethnic minorities in South-Central Coastal provinces are mainly Cham, Rade, and Chu Ro. Those in the Mekong Delta are mostly Khmer, Thai, and Vietnamese. Immigrants from other regions on the target area are mainly Tay, Nung, Thu Tu, Mong, H’Mông, Kinh, and Chu Ru.

#### Small-Scale Farmers and Their Vulnerability

Small-scale farmers—particularly poor, ethnic minorities and women farmers with plots of less than 1 ha—are most vulnerable to climate change. They have limited access to irrigation infrastructure or water sources, making up the majority of manual labor on these small farms. Since men increasingly migrate to urban areas in search of work, women are left behind to manage their households and farm land. Women also spend more time to spend more time and energy to source water not only for irrigation but also for domestic consumption. Various intergenerational differences exist between men and women regarding the ability to adapt to climate change.

#### Barriers to Adaptation

Major barriers preventing women from adopting or influencing household and community adaptation strategies include lack of education, limited access to credit and other financial resources, and caregiving work as well as production; lower recognition of women’s work, an imbalance towards lower-valued farming work, and gender-based discrimination. Women are also less likely than men to be involved in decision-making on the production side of the value chain, limited decision-making power in the community and at governance levels, with fewer opportunities for leadership roles. These barriers are compounded by lack of access to information and other marginalized groups in terms of education, extension, training, and lower participation of women compared to men in communities and households. Women also face greater transaction costs when accessing services such as market and climate information, technical support, agricultural inputs, production tools, and climate-smart technologies.

### 36. Baseline Efforts and Investments

Over the past two decades, GoV has channelled a significant portion of 75% of public agricultural investment into irrigation with a focus mainly on water intensive rice systems aimed at ensuring long-term food security for the population. This has led to an increase in the area under rice cultivation (from 10% in 2003 to 40% in 2016) and other perennial crops. These include the Phan–Phra Thiam Irrigation project (2005 to 2007) in Boen Chum district, the Pha Thong Irrigation project (2008 to 2014) in Kandal province, and the Prey Lang Irrigation project (2013 to 2016) funded through a US$ 1.5 million loan provided by the Italian Government. The program ‘Harnessing water resources for food security’ (2008–2017), implemented in collaboration with the World Bank and the European Union, also included the Pha Thong Irrigation project. The project ‘Introducing Low-cost Micro Irrigation Technology for Poor Farmers in South Central Cambodia’ (2014 to 2016) was funded by the Swiss Agency for Development and Cooperation (SDC). This is a research project in Ninh Tham district. Linking increased in water use efficiency for food production at the farm scale to global climate change.

### While all districts

While in all districts both the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast regions have benefited from past investments in medium to large-scale rural infrastructure, including irrigation systems, rural water supply, and drainage systems, these are not sufficient to meet current needs. These systems were designed for a different era of climate change and do not necessarily reach the most vulnerable. A number of investments have been planned or implemented with the aim of addressing this issue but have yet to be started/piloted starting July 2018, as per the newly issued Irrigation Law to incentivize water use efficiency.

### The GoV has invested heavily in reservoir development

The GoV has invested heavily in reservoir development to retain rainfall in the upper catchments for delivery to the lower catchments. These projects are designed with a focus on flood control and irrigation, but without any risk from low water productivity and the impacts of climate change. The design and management of these large systems does not include analysis of climate information influencing water availability, and as a result, supply from reservoirs is often insufficient to meet demand in dry seasons. These projects are also designed with the aim to modernize reservoirs, dams, sluices and other large water and irrigation infrastructure. These include large scale loan programs such as the World Bank’s Water Sector Development Project (2013–2017) and the USAID-funded project ‘Modernizing Reservoirs in Cambodia’ (2014 to 2018). The GoV has also implemented a number of planned ADB-financed Water Efficiency Improvement in Drought Affected Provinces (WEIDAP) project, which will serve as a model for future projects. These include the Phnom Penh Reservoir Rehabilitation Project (2015 to 2019), as well as the project ‘Irrigation development in sustainable coffee areas’ in Dak Lak (2015 to 2020), under PPC Reconstruction and Development Program.

### Government, development partners

Government, development partners and private sector support to conventional agricultural modernization and value chain development is concentrated on large-scale commercial crops and areas with good infrastructure. These include the USAID-supported project ‘Support to the Agricultural Sector in the New Rural Development’ (2014 to 2017), the project ‘Productive Rural Infrastructure Sector Project in the Central Highlands’ (2014 to 2019), as well as the World Bank’s Water Sector Development Project (2013–2017). These projects include the WEIDAP project ‘Enhancing Agricultural Competitiveness in Vien Nam’ (2017 – 2020) in Khmer Chau, Can Tho and The Binh, with a focus on water use efficiency. Other projects include the World Bank’s Water Sector Development Project ‘Transformation Project’ or WEIDAP project (2015 to 2020), mainly funded through a US$ 238 million loan from the World Bank. This project is designed to improve water supply and sanitation services in rural areas, with funding from the Government of Canada; and the Agriculture, Farmers and Rural Areas Support Project (2011 – 2017) in Kampong Speu province. The WEIDAP project ‘Irrigation Development in Sustainable Coffee Areas’ in Dak Lak is estimated US $1.5 million as beneficiary contribution. However, farming systems and strategies remain more focused on conventional agriculture with little attention to the development of sustainable agricultural practices or long-term application results in water and soil degradation. The shift to more resilient and sustainable agricultural production systems remains a challenge for many farmers, particularly those living in remote areas where access is a particular challenge. Two initiatives with this aim are the ‘Sustainable Economic Empowerment of Ethnic Minorities’

### Detailed Project Description

#### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 17 OF 102

**45. Adaptation Solution:** With continued growth of climate variability and change, the current strategies of small farmers are no longer sufficient to address these challenges. Smallholder agriculture is increasingly vulnerable due to increasingly ineffective coping mechanisms and requires transformational adaptation investments to achieve lasting climate resilience among smallholders. To ensure that smallholder agriculture can adapt effectively to climate change, including rainfall variability and drought, the GoM must adopt a paradigm shift in the way smallholder agricultural development is envisioned and supported. This will require increased public support for smallholder farmers through access to improved agricultural technologies and cropping systems, and creating climate risk-informed and climate resilient value chains to support diversified agricultural production and income generation among smallholders.

**46. For smallholder farmers:** Smallholder farmers are increasingly vulnerable in their ability to adapt sustainably and build the resilience of their agroecosystems in the face of increasing rainfall variability and drought, they will need to access sufficient water for irrigation and rain fed land, and smallholders will need to be able to plan and manage cropping systems that can withstand climate change. Smallholder farmers are increasingly vulnerable due to lack of access to information on climate risks and agricultural vulnerabilities, identify and implement management measures to build resilience of crop, soil and livestock production in the face of increasing rainfall variability and drought. Smallholder farmers require timely access to climate information and agricultural advisories so they can understand climate risks and agroecosystem vulnerabilities, identify and implement management measures to build resilience of crop, soil and livestock production in the face of increasing rainfall variability and drought. Smallholder farmers need access to diversified agricultural production over time. Access to value-chains, markets and finance is fundamental to sustain the transition towards more resilient smallholder agriculture.

**47. Therefore,** the proposed adaptation solution hinges on two mutually reinforcing elements: (i) a supply-side intervention that provides smallholder farmers with an effective package of climate-resilient agricultural technologies through large-scale and supplementary irrigation and water storage for rainfed farmers, and (ii) a demand-side intervention to promote adoption of climate-resilient agriculture practices, enabled through climate-risk informed planning, financing, and market development. To achieve this adaptation solution, however, several barriers must be overcome.

**Goals and Objectives**

**48. Limited financial capacity among smallholder farmers and government investment in additional sources of water to cope with climate-induced change rainfall variability and drought**

Most smallholder farmers in the proposed project area live in regions that have limited access to reliable sources of water for agricultural production under conditions of increasing climate-driven rainfall variability and drought. During severe and prolonged droughts, smallholder farmers are unable to access sufficient water to maintain agricultural productivity. In addition, many smallholder farmers do not have access to irrigation infrastructure or water, and alternative sources such as rivers, streams, ponds, and wells are easily depleted or insufficient. While government investment in additional sources of water for rainfed agriculture is a critical component of the project area, fiscal constraints impede investments in the additional costs of ensuring smallholder connectivity to this infrastructure. The proposed project will address these challenges by providing an integrated approach that addresses baseline investments that have occurred do not address current or future climate impacts in their design. For poor and vulnerable smallholders, access to water for irrigation is a critical component of adaptation; however, investment is limited given their weak financial capacities, which are further undermined by climate change.

**50. Lack of access:** Smallholder farmers face significant constraints on their ability to increase production due to additional costs of water storage infrastructure or lack of access to irrigation as they are caught in the vicious cycle of productivity loss resulting in insufficient income, leading to inadequate investments in water security and resilient agricultural practices. The limited financial capacity among smallholder farmers and government investment in rainfed agriculture has been hindered by a lack of awareness, technical knowledge and financing, including difficulties accessing credit.

**51. Lack of awareness among smallholders and weak technical capacities across extension support to scale up adoption of climate-resilient agriculture

# Detailed Project Description

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 108 OF 102

### Agricultural Policy and Climate Change Adaptation

In both target regions, agricultural policy has been focused on producing higher yields while neglecting longer term adaptation of agriculture to climate change. Most farmers in these regions are aware of changing temperature and precipitation patterns and increasing intensity and frequency of extreme weather events such as droughts. Although farmers have some experience with drought management, these are progressively less and less effective in coping with the growing variability in rainfall and other climatic conditions. Many farmers are also aware that climate change is leading to more frequent and severe floods, which pose a threat to their livelihoods.

### Agricultural Vulnerability

The majority of smallholder farms in both target regions are characterized by low levels of farm diversification, with many more climate-vulnerable monocultures, and traditional knowledge of such climate-resilient farming systems as intercropping and crop rotation is often lost. Many farmers have not received training on how to adapt their agricultural practices to changing climatic conditions.

### Limited Extension Services

Although demand exists, most farmers have never received or participated in a training event on climate risk and accessible methodologies for agro-ecosystem vulnerability analysis, farm resource planning and management, weather forecasting, and other related topics. The limited capacity of the government extension service and agricultural extension agencies to support climate-risk management and resilient agriculture is primarily due to problems with recruitment, retention, and training of staff in rural areas.

### Limited Extension Capacity

The capacity of provincial, district and commune meteorological and agricultural staff to generate and disseminate localized, easily understandable information on climate variability and extreme weather events is limited. The need for adequate and timely information to help limit their risks is high.

### Technical Capacities

Technical capacities among institutions to generate and disseminate timely, integrated, and actionable climate information are also limited in both target regions. Limited access to credit and market information to sustain investment in resilient water and agricultural systems for agricultural production in light of evolving climate risks.

### Enhanced Climate Information Products

Enhanced climate information products and services must be sustained over time and adapted to the inevitable evolution of climate variability over the coming years and decades, and must be tailored to meet the needs of smallholder farmers. This will require a continued investment in climate-resilient agro-ecosystems. Smallholders, particularly the poorest farmers, require financial resources from access to credit to diversify towards climate-resilient inputs, technologies, and crops and build the resilience of their livelihoods.

59. The incremental costs to poor-poor smallholders of start-up investments in water security and resilient agricultural practices are substantial, and the benefits of these investments in terms of increased yields and agroecosystems obtained, they must be sustained, and farmers must be able to cover the additional costs of resilience measures over time. In the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast regions of Vietnam, for example, only 25% of poor farmers in both regions have taken out loans to invest in farm inputs through the formal system, with most of these loans being used to purchase seeds and fertilizers.

In addition, poor-poor smallholders are often excluded from training programs; it is also important that financial intermediaries have the knowledge and capacities to assess climate risks and adopt appropriate credit instruments.

The objective of this project is to support poor-poor smallholder farmers in the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast regions of Vietnam – particularly women and ethnic minority farmers - to manage increasing climate risks to agroecosystems, increase yields and incomes through resilient agricultural practices, and strengthening access to actionable agro-climate information, credit and markets.

63. The project advances a paradigm shift away from short-term, stop-gap measures to more integrated, multi-dimensioned solutions that address the root causes of climate change induced water scarcity and variability.

### C3 Project Description

#### Rationale and methodology for targeting of the project areas and populations

The starting point for geographical targeting of the proposed project was the targeting (and design) of project interventions in conjunction with the WEADAP project common areas, informed by the analysis of climate vulnerability social vulnerability and exposure to climate risks. The project will focus on vulnerable communities living within the South in the South-Central Coast, particularly in terms of seasonal rainfall variability and drought and flood risk. Eight sites have been selected for this project: two coastal sites (Phuoc Anh and Phuoc Thanh), two upland sites (Dong Van and Nam Dinh), one urban site (Hue City), and three riverine sites (Thua Thien Hue, Quang Tri, and Khanh Hoa). These sites are exposed to climate risks, with the exception of Hue city. The project will work in close collaboration with the WEADAP project areas as a starting point; an intersecting approach should be used to guide the geographical targeting of the proposed project area to the GOF, with the following filters applied: as informed by the climate risk analysis in previous chapters.

The project builds on lessons learned and best practices from a number of successful initiatives involving irrigation innovations, Farmer Field Schools, voucher systems, and smaller farmer credit access and financial management. A number of these initiatives have been implemented specifically to address the needs of smallholder farmers and other vulnerable groups. The following projects are particularly relevant: Forecast-based Financing for Drought Preparedness in South-Central Vietnam (2013-2015) supported by the World Bank, Women Empowerment in Agriculture (WEA) Project in South-Central Vietnam (2014-2017), Women and Save the Children; Sustainable Economic Empowerment of Ethnic Minorities in Dak Nong supported by IFAD, The Climate Smart Agriculture Project (CSAP) in Quang Nam Province (2015-2018) supported by IFC, Microfinance for Smallholder Farmers’ Access to Agricultural Development - project supported by the IFC and the Government of Canada; and Introducing Low-cost Microirrigation Systems into Smallholder Farming Systems in Vietnam (2016-2017) supported by IFAD. These projects have been effective at increasing smallholder farmers' access to water resources, improving their livelihoods, and making them more resilient to climate change.

The project is designed to help secure water resources and improve soil and water management and more efficient water use in the target areas. The project will produce a set of tools and technologies to reduce flood and drought risk mitigation (DUR2), providing the foundation for increased yields. To increase incomes, surplus yields of climate-resilient crops will be sold at market prices or through cooperatives. A network of local actors will be established comprised of representative producers, input suppliers, technical assistance providers, buyers and others to enhance value chain linkages in the target areas. The project will also support the development of a regional platform where partnerships will address production problems and bottlenecks, negotiate contractual arrangements, and smooth access to inputs and markets for smallholder farmers. As a result, smallholder farmers and other vulnerable groups will be able to market surplus yields and, with the increased incomes, increase their resilience to climate change.

The project will work in close collaboration with the WEADAP project areas as a starting point; an intersecting approach should be used to guide the geographical targeting of the proposed project area to the GOF, with the following filters applied: as informed by the climate risk analysis in previous chapters.

### Areas that are prone to identified climate risks: seasonal variability and drought risk.

#### (A) Areas that are most affected by major climate change impacts on water and agriculture, currently and as projected:
- Increasing imbalance in surface and ground water availability for production; with longer periods of severe dry spells and reduced rainfall intensity.
- Reduced crop yields and increased vulnerability to pests and diseases, leading to lower productivity in terms of yields, incomes.

#### (B) Combined areas of rainfed and irrigated areas: with upland and lowland areas with rain-fed cultivation most vulnerable to wetter wet seasons and drier dry seasons and an increased risk of extreme-droughts, while irrigated areas are more vulnerable to water scarcity during the dry season.
- Areas with high social vulnerability factors or density of at-risk populations, such as ethnic minority population, poor and near-poor, and number of women-headed households.

#### (C) Overlying these filters in the five selected provinces results in a priority geographical selection of 14 districts encompassing 60 communes that are most vulnerable to climate risks under the GCC project.
- The majority of the 60 communes (including all communes served by WEIDAP) are a mosaic of irrigated and rainfed cropping systems, with 17 communes having more than half their area in rainfed agriculture. These areas have been identified as high risk sections in line 1 of the FRS; namely areas are more vulnerable to the identified climate risks than irrigated areas, so the focus is on these areas.

### The proposed project, therefore, targets communities in and around WEIDAP sub-project boundaries (the command areas) most affected by major climate change impacts on water and agriculture, both current and projected, including increased imbalance in surface and ground water availability for production; with longer periods of severe dry spells during the dry season, increased intensity of droughts, and reduced crop productivity impacting both yield and income. **C**

73. Before 2016, the GoV used an income-based national poverty line for targeting of beneficiaries in poverty reduction programs. However, since then the GoV has adopted a “rights-based multi-dimensional poverty approach” for targeting of beneficiaries in social protection and other development programs. This new approach combines measures that are a combination of deprivation indices together with other indicators on access to basic services. Programs now target poor households, including women-headed households, single women, and women in families with high dependency rates/disabled children.

**Project Description:**

74. To achieve its objective, the project addresses climate-induced water stress through a two-pronged approach:

(i) It aims at improving access to the provision of water-efficient irrigation infrastructure and increasing water storage capacity to address the risk of water scarcity; (ii) from the demand-side, through introduction of climate-resilient crop diversification and improved agricultural practices that are adapted to climate change impacts on agricultural production. As such, the project invests in enabling smallholders, particularly poor/non-poor, ethnic minority and semi-nomadic communities’ access to increasing climate-driven rainfall variability and drought through implementation of two inter-linked Outputs.

**Output 1: Enhanced water security for agriculture production for smallholder farmers in the face of climate-induced water stress**

75. This Output will overcome barriers to water security for climate-resilient production through investment in irrigation systems and technologies, including storage and water-efficient equipment. Modernization and expansion of irrigation systems will be implemented by rehabilitating existing infrastructure and constructing new ones where necessary, particularly cropping systems. Under this Output, the project directly complements the loan scope provided by ADB to GoV™ to establish a Water Infrastructure Development Program (WIDP) in the two target provinces.

76. While this baseline investment will make primary irrigation infrastructure available to all smallholders within the eight project districts, it is estimated that only 50% of these farmers have access to this infrastructure by those farmers whose vulnerability to climate change impacts on agricultural production are compounded by their socio-economic status, distance from water sources, and lack of land tenure security. The project will therefore focus its investments in areas with better access to secure sources of water for crop production but which currently lack the ability to connect to this infrastructure or modernize it.

77. The ADB-financed WIDP project carried out a thorough analysis of water sources to be used for irrigation in the eight project districts. This analysis identified 12 major water sources that can be tapped into by smallholders. The project improves and connects canals and pipe systems to these sources. Irrigation water under Output 1, Activity 1.2, will be provided through a combination of the WIDP for GoV™ and the existing irrigation system under Activity 1.3 below is rainfall and ensuring surface flow in the river surrounding the ponds used as the ultimate site for the harvested water.

**The project’s investments in these resources will finance the incremental costs of achieving last-mile connectivity for the targeted poor/non-poor smallholder farmers within the WIDP command areas by linking their plots to the WIDP irrigation infrastructure. The project will also provide a combination of climate-resilient crop diversification and improved agricultural practices that are adapted to climate change impacts on agricultural production. This Output will address water deficiencies due to climate-induced rainfall variability and droughts. This Output will combine GCF and provincial government resources to support supplementary irrigation from localized sources, as well as the rehabilitation and expansion of existing infrastructure for rainfed agriculture and supplemental irrigation. These investments will work in tandem with the resilient agricultural practices promoted under Output 2 to augment the resilience of diversified cropping systems, for stability, drought tolerance/agroforestry, etc.**

**Activity 1.1: Establish large-scale irrigation infrastructure to bring irrigation water to eight farming areas across the target regions**

## Project Implementation

86. Proposed last-mile connectivity support to be provided with GCF funding will include shared/pirate pumps and pipelines to connect to the improved canal systems' manifolds, water meters, control valves, transit tanks, shared/private pipelines, and gravity branched new ring main pipeline system.

75. To ensure ownership, benefiting households will be required to provide in-kind contributions of labor and use of small locally owned assets (e.g., a hand pump) for the installation of last-mile connectivity support packages. The last-mile connectivity support packages will be provided – combined with technical guidance manuals and mentors using GCF funding – to help households better understand how to adapt their systems to climate change, including adapting to climate-driven water insecurity. To not do so would leave them at a higher risk of water insecurity compared to those who have received the support.

85. In terms of CDM of the last-mile connectivity support packages, the connection systems require some small maintenance and operation costs that will be covered by the project's own resources (e.g., through GCF funding assistance). For shared systems, a water-user group will be set up or an existing farmer group engaged. In line with good practice on irrigation management in Viet Nam (see section 5.1 of the Feasibility Study), these groups will be self-managed and will have access to technical guidance manuals and mentors provided by the project team and local language GCM guidance notes and manuals will be developed and disseminated.

84. The DARDs' (provincial Department of Agriculture and Rural Development) irrigation management units, as well as the provincial and IMC's technical staff will provide on-going technical support to all households with private and shared systems installed under this project.

# DETAILED PROJECT DESCRIPTION

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 28 OF 102

### Activity 1.3: Enhance supplementary irrigation for rain fed smallholders to cope with rainfall variability and drought

This activity will support poor/hear-poor, ethnic minority- and women farmers to cope with the climate change-driven impacts on water availability on their rain-fed agricultural systems.

One of the pillars of an effective adaptation solution, the project will support construction or enhancement of existing supplementary water storage systems to enable smallholders to store water for use during dry seasons. Investments in water storage are at risk as these investments are not made with a climate change risk management approach and may be lost if rainfall variability increases or changes in climate conditions lead to increased evaporation and/or soil erosion.

These investments could serve as the groundwork for potential phased expansion of either the WEIDAP project or other similar projects, including those that focus on water harvesting and storage. These systems will be constructed using locally available materials and techniques with minimal costs and labour.

To determine the extent to which water resources can be extracted and utilized in each project area, as well as the impacts of climate change on water sources and water use in rain-fed production areas, the availability of water resources for extraction and utilization is determined by a combination of rainfall, soil moisture, and groundwater preparation by modeling water balances for key crops, soils and climate conditions to 2050. Please see Chapter 5 of the Project Proposal for further details.

The GCF and Oxfam will jointly finance on-farm water storage systems for collecting rainwater or surface runoff. These systems will be designed to store water in the wet season for use during dry seasons, as much as possible using existing infrastructure. The design of these storage systems will be combined with training on climate-resilient water resources management.

MARD, in consultation with stakeholders and communities, will select sites based on the following criteria:

(i) options for upland, midland or flatland area;

(ii) in reach of most vulnerable people in the Project Area based on the official Vietnamese poverty line;

(iii) arranged in accordance with the overall layout of the existing water collection system of the Project Area;

(iv) having a high potential to increase water catchment within all climate scenarios;

(v) increasing the ability of gravity irrigation;

(vi) not disrupting farming activities.

MARD will finalize the design of ponds in accordance with the following eligibility criteria:

(i) ensuring sufficient supply of water for crops in the dry season;

(ii) simplicity and low cost (design) and maintenance (doable by beneficiaries themselves with minimal additional costs and labour) as further described in the Feasibility Study submitted together with this Funding Proposal;

(iii) limited evaporation and permeability to avoid loss of stored water;

(iv) include a water distribution system;

(v) integration with existing surface water collection systems if possible;

(vi) preventing sedimentation;

(vii) adaptability to changing water and climate conditions;

(viii) based on bioengineering principles; and,

(ix) suitability for ethnic minorities and women, for example not creating additional workload.

### Design details of the water harvesting including dimensions are available in Annex II of the feasibility study, and further details are available in Annex III - Sub-assessment on Water storage and irrigation.

34. An area meeting these criteria have been proposed in targeted provinces in the Project Area as depicted in the F5 & Figure 45 showing maps with the pond locations. These include 1,159 climate resilient ponds (upgrading 848 ponds, construction of 490 new ponds and 185 shared ponds).

### Implementation

36. Indicators for project success will be based on a water balance analysis and water resources assessment to corroborate the most appropriate design and management for each pond and each location. The water balance modeling will use comprehensive data including rainfall, evaporation, temperature, soil moisture content, groundwater levels, river and rainwater sources, current status of existing ponds, soil conditions, local cropping systems and tree portfolio, climate risk analysis, vulnerability assessment, socio-economic and effective design for water resources sustainability under the different climate scenarios.

### Design and Implementation

96. Based on the water balance modeling results, the initial design of the rainwater harvesting ponds constructed for this study will be based on a combination of pond construction, pond management, soil conservation, land preparation, and storage; water collection system; treatment of pond bottom losses; treatment of pond surface losses; water distribution system; and monitoring and evaluation.

97. External technical expertise is required for the design and landscaping of bioengineered climate-resilient ponds, including the training and mentoring of DARD irrigation Department and other staff. These bioengineering techniques have been developed by the GCF-funded project in collaboration with the International Centre for Research on Agroforestry (ICRAF) and are based on a combination of soil conservation, land preparation, and storage; water collection system; treatment of pond bottom losses; treatment of pond surface losses; water distribution system; and monitoring and evaluation.

98. O&M of the shared ponds will be assured through the establishment of farmer-led 'pond management groups', comprised of gender-balanced and inclusive representation of the households benefiting from the pond. The group shall be responsible for the construction, maintenance, operation, rehabilitation (if required), and/or upgrading (if required) of the pond, use and management process. This includes monitoring and evaluating the construction phase; Pond management group members will be trained in water balance modeling techniques to ensure that they understand these mechanisms; to attract active community members and build on existing farmer-to-farmer networks. Rules and regulations for the ponds will be developed by the group with the support of the project team, which will monitor themselves, through majority agreement and ensuring gender equality and inclusiveness.

99. The establishment of the pond management groups shall be supported by a facilitator who is responsible for providing technical guidance to the community DARD technical staff as Responsible Parties. Please see Annex II(a) Sub-Assignment on Water Storage Management Group established in the Feasibility Study.

**Women farmers with affordable climate resilient on-farm water efficiency technologies:** This will lower net water demand, increase crop water productivity, and enable cropping systems to withstand drought and precipitation shocks and stresses. The project will target the most vulnerable farmers who unlike the better-off farmers cannot afford existing water-saving technologies or are not able to apply them due to lack of knowledge and skills.

100. **The approach builds on farmers’ knowledge of actual climate conditions, production systems and household technologies as needed:** It will adapt and operate water-saving irrigation systems to ensure effective agricultural production in the face of increasing rainfall variability and drought. 2. The technology developed will be flexible and applicable to the crops grown by the poor and near-poor, cost-effective for small-scale farmers, and based on locally available materials. It will be simple enough that it can be built with locally available materials and be easily maintained. It will increase water efficiency, reduce agricultural input costs and ensure a more stable income stream for women farmers in the long term.

## Output 2: Increased resilience of smallholder farmer livelihoods through climate-resilient agriculture and access to climate information services

While the main objective of Output 1 aims to increase availability of water, as well as improve water use efficiency, Output 2 will enable poor/no-poor farmers to manage climate risks to their agro-ecosystems by applying climate-resilient agricultural practices and accessing climate information services.

The interventions in Output 2 are primarily oriented toward reducing the risk from drought because this is the most damaging climate-related hazard for smallholder farmers. The project will also address the risk of prolonged residence of flood waters in the project areas with flood waters dispersing relatively rapidly after extreme rainfall events. This will be achieved by:

1. Leveraging the use of a range of tools and services, including weather forecasts, early warning systems, timely and more detailed/downscaled climate and weather advisories will help smallholder farmers manage their crops and livestock in response to changing climatic conditions.
2. Addressing the risk from flooding, relatively low water, will be addressed in Farmer Field Schools as a standard part of learning to manage climate risks.

The Output 2 will enable poor/no-poor smallholder farmers across the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast regions to overcome information, skills, knowledge and financial barriers limiting their ability to produce climate-resilient crops. The project will also provide access to credit markets and co-financing leverage to help smallholder farmers acquire the skills and knowledge needed to enhance the resilience of their livelihoods.

Through Farmer Field Schools implemented at scale across the two regions, this Output will facilitate widespread adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices by poor/no-poor smallholder farmers. Smallholders will build on traditional knowledge and contemporary science to adapt their cropping systems to climate change impacts while also improving soil health, increasing crop yields, reducing water use, and enhancing agro-ecosystem vulnerability. Farmers will be trained in resilience-enhancing crop diversification, as a climate-risk reduction strategy that can help them cope with the changing climatic conditions. This will include growing leguminous matter, and biodiversity, improving soil structure, and limiting soil erosion. The project specifically aims to support smallholder farmers in producing crops such as soybeans, maize, rice, and cassava, which are more resilient than other crops that also require less water. Project design is in line with Government efforts to promote watershed-based water management approaches and to support sound water governance through a safe, clean and resilient water system.


---
**Page 36**
---

110. For farmers to successfully market their climate-resilient production and continue to adapt to changing climate risks, this Outlook will facilitate value-chain and market linkages through innovative multi-stakeholder Climate Innovation Platforms (CIPs). Private sector engagement in value chains is not presently coordinated with producers, except in rare cases where the private sector has a strong interest in the product. The CIPs will bring together farmers and technical assistance providers all participate in value chains in an ad hoc manner. This lack of coordination results in a fragmented approach to climate change adaptation that does not fully leverage the potential for public-private and non-project partners to collaboratively discuss the challenges of climate change and its impact on water resources, and to develop and implement innovative solutions towards resilient agricultural systems. Each platform will develop a common vision of how to achieve application of climate-resilient practices in their respective regions. The CIPs will be led by public sector institutions that identify, develop and promote straightforward and integrated strategies for developing climate-resilient and inclusive value chains. The CIPs will also provide a forum for the exchange of information on best practices from projects and thereby facilitate coordination and synergies, information exchange and scaling of good practice from the CGIAR Research Program on Climate Change, Agriculture and Food Security (CCAFS) and other platforms for exchange and collaboration. The CIPs will focus on improving the agricultural systems towards pro-poor, environment-friendly and climate-resilient practices in a manner that is inclusive of all stakeholders. This will be achieved not only by the leadership of the state-run institutions directly sustained by GoV resources; farmers, private entities, NGOs and other civil society organizations are also expected to participate in these platforms. The development of such strategies is the primary mechanism for galvanizing and maintaining their interest, ownership and commitment. As markets are accessed and revenues increase from sales, interest in CIP participation is expected to be sustained.

110a. To ensure that climate-resilient agricultural systems can adapt effectively to changing conditions under conditions of evolving climate variability, the project will leverage government co-financing to build the capacities of public sector institutions to provide technical assistance for the development and implementation of climate-smart practices in the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast regions. With systematic reception and interpretation of climate information, farmers are better able to make informed decisions about their farming activities under conditions of evolving climate variability. As climate change increasingly challenges traditional local knowledge of key farming cycles (by, for example, increasing the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as droughts), farmers are increasingly in need of access to actionable weather and climate information that can help them cope with these changes.

110b. Under the Outlook, CGIAR funding supports smallholders in the 45 WEIP/DAP project communities connected to the irrigation grid and an additional 15 communes (i.e. a total 60% of the Project Area). This project enhances the climate-resilience of agricultural systems by supporting the development and implementation of climate-smart agricultural practices (under activity 2.1) co-development and use of agri-climate information for climate risk management (under activity 2.3), and improving access to markets and other services that support smallholder farmers' coordination for climate-resilient value chain development through climate innovation platforms (under activity 2.2).

Activity 2.1: Investments in inputs and capacities to scale up climate-resilient cropping systems and practices (such crop varieties, seeds, fertilizers, irrigation equipment, etc.) and livestock management practices.

114. This activity will focus on empowering farmers with the skills and capacities, as well as continued access to information, to enable them to choose suitable options to increase the resilience of their farms and income streams. Analysis of climate vulnerability in each cluster of communes was carried out as part of project preparation. By analyzing climate vulnerability, identifying existing cropping patterns, missing linkages between farmers and markets, and other constraints, a set of climate-resilient agricultural systems were defined for each cluster of communes. These models include shifting to more resilient crops or crop varieties, diversification of income sources, improved access to inputs and services, and better management practices.

115. The rate of adoption by each farmer of resilience-enhancing crops and varieties is driven by his or her perception of risk based on an appreciation of higher own capacities, the degree of climate risk; the availability of technical and financial assistance, and incentives, access to markets for production, and other factors. This project will assist

farmers to build their technical and managerial capacities, including business planning; provide financial incentives through vouchers for production inputs and materials; provide climate risk advisors; build the capacities of relevant institutions to provide support and access to markets and credit. The rate of adoption will depend on individual farmer’s willingness to adopt new technologies and practices, as well as the availability of resources for adopting unfaltering climate-resilient production practices and systems. ## Strengthening the resilience and productivity of cropping systems and increasing access to information, technical support and other resources and services supporting climate resilient agriculture (CRA), the project will implement community-based participatory approaches in 100 villages across the three target districts of the project area. Each program will consist of a number of FFS linked to a particular Climate Innovation Platform located at agricultural extension service centers. The FFS will be implemented by DARD staff and will focus on CRA climate zone. Specific attention will be paid to reach women farmers and ethnic minorities, for example by setting up working groups with women and ethnic minority farmers. The FFS will be implemented in the field through a mix of location of trainings and using visual materials and interactive formats.

### 17.2 FFS will be implemented in three CRA climate zones, encompassing the 60 participating communities depending on agroecological zone, agroforestry system to be established, and social and economic factors (e.g., local ethnic composition, gender, income). FFS are not actual physical installations but rather flexible arrangements whereby farmers can access information and training in a variety of formats. The FFS will be linked with the particular curriculum demands. By establishing these FFS, it is expected that all farmers will participate and no farmer will be left behind. The FFS will be implemented by DARD staff and will focus on CRA climate zone. It will draw from existing resources and capacities in the project area to ensure that the FFS are sustainable and accessible FFS within easy transit distance of all participating farmers. Each FFS will host up to 25 farmers on average, drawn from a mix of communities and social groups. The FFS will be implemented through a mix of location of trainings and using visual materials and interactive formats.

### 17.3 DARDs (the RPs for the FFS implementation).

The FFS program will test and promote the production of packets of crops, practices, and inputs customized by each agricultural extension service center (CRA). These CRA packages have been developed during project preparation based on an in-depth analysis of current and projected climate risks and impacts on water and agricultural productivity, baseline perceptions of farmers regarding climate change, and other relevant factors. The CRA package will be aligned with Government priorities, ensuring fair gender practices and considering gender and ethnic minority considerations.

The CRA package will be designed to ensure that the most vulnerable groups (e.g., women, youth, children, poor and non-poor smallholders) and local authorities, discussed and refined, with cropping system priorities identified by each CRA. The CRA package will also include a climate-resilient agriculture for a table detailing the correspondence between communities, crops and climate-resilient alternatives.

### 17.4 Farm-level champions will be selected from the participating farmers in the target region using simple methods of analyzing local agroecosystems and vulnerability using traditional knowledge, scientific information, evaluate and confirm appropriate climate risk mitigation measures, refine and adapt the CRA models for local conditions, and ensure that the CRA package is tailored to meet the needs of each community. To ensure ongoing, iterative adaptation to continuing climate change, the selection of champion farmers is monitored and confirmed by DARDs in consultation with concerned communities and confirmation by the DARDs in accordance with the following eligibility criteria:

(i) their local recognition as leaders among their adopters;

(ii) their demonstrated leadership in the development of the Project Area;

(iii) willingness to engage and invest time in mentoring other farmers; and

(iv) basic technical understanding of the proposed technologies and practices promoted by this project.

The selection of the champion farmers will occur as part of sub-activity 2.1.2. After "graduation" from FFS, these farmers will be responsible for training neighboring farmers on climate-resilient agricultural practices and systems with supervision and support from government and NGOs. By utilizing existing farmer extension services but strengthening them to ensure that they are more responsive to the needs of smallholder farmers, this project will reach approximately 55,603 small farmer households, at least 50% of whom will be poor/non-poor farmers.

While some of these inputs may be available with suppliers, some may be obtained by suppliers when participating in training sessions or workshops conducted under the FFS program. The voucher system at the FFS on-farm and development of a simple business plan, a farmer’s participation in the voucher system will be phased out. Compared to other voucher schemes or in-kind support, the voucher system is cost-effective for farmers, more flexible and tailored based on their needs, provides access to local markets and private sector growth, limits the risk of crowding out private sector, and empowers farmers to make their own informed decisions about inputs and services. ### 125. The voucher system will be developed by the Executing Entity, with support from experts who will be selected by the Executing Entity (MARID), and implemented in accordance with the following steps, replicating global good practices for performance-based vouchers as well as lessons learned from the pilot phase of the FFS:

i) Orientation of lead facilitators on the objectives, process and components of the voucher program, with the facilitators selected from the group of FFS core facilitators and lead farmers (see above).

ii) Conducting a market mapping exercise to identify the key suppliers of inputs for the project, ensuring buy-in. If required, implementation or operational regulations should be devised by the province/authorities.

iii) Participatory market mapping and assessment, including a mapping and assessment of the local suppliers' products and services, their capacity to supply the inputs needed in the FFS, their willingness to participate, household financial analysis, and market prices and trend prices, with involvement of the lead facilitators. (See above).

iv) Development of a list of potential suppliers based on the results of the participatory mapping exercise of the CRA packages with the results of the local market assessment. The list should be long and diverse enough to meet the needs of the FFS.

v) Participatory beneficiary selection, conducted by the lead facilitators through village meetings, and based on the following criteria: willingness to participate in the voucher system; ability to pay for inputs; willingness to participate in the complete FFS.

vi) Development of a list of beneficiaries ensuring a diverse number of small and medium size suppliers operating in proximity to the farmers. Suppliers should be selected through an open-bidding process managed by the district administration, which can consist from the external partner. Contracts between local authorities and suppliers should include some key contract-specific aspects such as commodity specification, trader payment terms, quality inspection and acceptance.

#### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 38 OF 102

**vii) Project design of vouchers in terms of monetary value, serial numbers, colors, tags, date of validity etc.:**
The value of the vouchers can be different per sub-project and should depend on the CRA designs proposed and the cost of the required agricultural inputs (per the results of the market assessment). Farmers should not receive the full amount of the voucher as a loan but only 80% to cover the costs of the inputs, for example a minimum of twenty percent. The value should represent only a portion of the costs to offset the farmer's perceived risk and provide an incentive to adopt climate-resilient practices. Vouchers should be designed to ensure appropriate use by ethnic groups, illiterate farmers and other marginalized groups. The design should be tested in pilot projects.

**vi) Set up of beneficiary accountability mechanisms:**
The mechanism should be established to allow farmers and input suppliers to report any issues with the vouchers or the voucher system itself. This will help identify any issues that might arise:

* **Farmers:** Farmers should be able to report on the use of vouchers, distribution and redemption system.
* **Suppliers:** Suppliers should be able to report on the use of vouchers, distribution and redemption system.

**vii) Voucher distribution, linked to participation in FFS and the development of a simple business plan:**
With one voucher per farmer, farmers will be encouraged to participate in FFS and develop a simple business plan. This will help them understand how they can use their resources more efficiently and accumulate them to buy more expensive inputs. Farmer group members should also be allowed to pool all their vouchers together.

**viii) Voucher redemption, reconciliation and reimbursement:**
For farmers, agricultural input suppliers and district (DAMD)/AEC:

* **Redemption:** Use of vouchers and market prices.
* **Reconciliation:** Facilitators based on information from the farmers as well as the suppliers. Progress on the use of the vouchers should be reported and discussed in the sub-project CPs, with a focus on the progress made by smallholder farmers.

**126. The district DAMD AEC will be the Responsible Parties for developing, implementing and monitoring the voucher system under the supervision of the project team, including the Project Manager, the Technical Advisor, the UNO and the WWU. It will be implemented at sub-project CIP level and linked to the FFS program and CRA packages. Technical expertise and experience from UN organizations such as FAO and IFAD will also be drawn upon during the implementation of Farmer Field School support, as part of the coordinated One UN approach in Viet Nam.**
