### CIP workshops and events and 57% of the credit management workshops for small holder farmers, GCF will cover the costs of establishing the CIPs for crops where there are climate risks to value chains, planning for climate resilient agricultural production and communication, technical assistance and 43% of the workshops for smallholders.

#### The project will establish a provincial level coordination mechanism at the provincial level. This will be in the form of Climate Innovation Platforms (CIPs) that bring together representatives of the key stakeholders in specific value chains. These platforms will be established with the participation of government, private sector and civil society associations; input providers; buyers; lending institutions; GoV and NGO technical assistance organizations; key climate and agricultural experts and other relevant actors. The CIPs will be led by provincial level DARDs who will enable resource flow between value chain stakeholders and collaborators to discuss the challenges of climate change and its impact on water resources and agricultural productivity in their locality and to discuss and promote innovative solutions for adaptation and mitigation. At least 10% of the total project budget will be allocated towards development and commercialization of at least one particular value-chain; stakeholders will commit to collaborating in the implementation of this project.

#### Establishment of CIPs, along with technical assistance to enhance access to markets and finance, is intended to leverage private sector investment. The provincial level DARDs will provide leadership for the establishment of CIPs that ensure fund development, communication and discussion of any new climate vulnerability assessments and agro-climatic information. This will be done through a series of workshops and events at the provincial level. Private sector financing will be leveraged through agreements between poor-poor farmer and farmers who are participating in Climate Innovation Platforms. The proposed project will also enable smallerholder producers' groups to access finance for climate resilient agricultural production.

The CIPs will be created at two levels that will be linked to enhance information flow and coordination:

- Five provincial CIPs, as policy dialogue, information sharing and scaling platforms.
- 1 District CIP per province, as technical assistance, capacity building, information sharing and monitoring platforms.

#### The provincial level CIPs will be chaired by the provincial Responsible Parties (DARDs) and bring together decision-makers, climate experts and technical advisors from different governmental organizations. A primary focus of these CIPs will be on improving the capacities of and support to poor-poor, ethnic minority and women for their adaptation and mitigation efforts in the face of climate change.

The District CIPs will be established at the district level with a focus on specific crops (key annual and perennial cropping systems, soil types and rainfed/irrigated areas). This method results in 14 proposed district CIPs. The District CIPs will be led by the district DARDs.

The district CIPs will be held on a six-monthly or annual basis and focus on the development of climate-resilient value chains, including productive and sustainable cropping systems, increased market access, and enabling farmers to obtain credit.

#### The specific objectives of the district CIPs are:

- Implementation of solutions to challenges and bottlenecks for achieving climate resilient value chains, based on on-site visits and lessons learned.
- Establishment and nurturing of partnerships, including contractual relationships;
- Capacity building and technical assistance in areas such as climate-resilient crop variety building, access to inputs and services, local policies for promoting climate resilient agriculture options; linkages to market and engagement with potential private sector;
- Periodic exchange of experiences and knowledge to promote learning and refine activities among the stakeholders.
- Scaling up best practices and lessons from the projects sites through meetings, fairs and other networks.

#### The district CIPs will be chaired by the district DARD and involve similar organizations as at provincial level. The DARD champions and a representative number of district CIP members as required will also participate in the provincial CIPs. At the start of each five-year cycle, the district CIPs will develop an action plan for achieving climate resilient value chains, in line with the vision of the provincial CIPs. And incorporating activities as planned through the GCF-chosen project WEADAP project and from other GoV and non-Government projects and programs. The work plan will be monitored and

# Detailed Project Description

## Activity 3.2 Development and use of localized agro-climate advisories by smallholders to enhance climate-resilient agricultural production

### 139. Meeting a high demand for more localized and actionable agro-climate information so farmers – and agricultural planners – can better identify climate risks and potential impacts, plan their crop calendars and take timely measures to reduce the risk of yield loss or other negative consequences.

This activity will develop and disseminate downscaled forecasts and advisory systems towards a more farmer-needs-driven agro-climate information service. This activity will develop and disseminate downscaled forecasts and advisory systems that are tailored for specific crops, regions, and socio-economic conditions. This will be done by replicating Participatory Scenario Planning, a proven multi-stakeholder approach to developing climate-resilient agricultural plans.

### 140. Linking seasonal and ten-to-fifteen-day agro-climate advisories

The project team will assemble a technical group in charge of the co-development of seasonal and ten-to-fifteen-day agro-climate advisories, called the Agro-Climate Information Working Group (ACIWG). The ACIWG will be comprised of provincial hydro-meteorological services staff, district agricultural staff, mass organizations and representatives from farmers’ associations, women’s groups, youth groups, indigenous peoples, civil society organization or NGO experienced in developing agro-climate advisories through Participatory Scenario Planning processes. Sub-groups will be formed as required to produce tailored advisories for different cropping systems within the sub-project.

### 141. These groups will meet and discuss agricultural and climate information, trends and patterns at pre-season, post-season and during the season. They will also draw on data from provincial hydro-meteorological services, weather stations, farmer champions, and other sources to develop and package easily accessible agro-climate advisories.

Agro-climate advisories will be developed for all 14 districts in the project and will enhance models that can support further replication at low cost.

### 142. The seasonal agro-climate advisories will be developed as follows, with steps repeated as needed to enhance iterative learning:

- Training of provincial hydro-meteorological staff on generating and interpreting downscaled weather forecasts and climate information.
- Agro-ecological zoning to support improved downscaling and advisory development.
- Formation of AGCS technical group at the sub-project CIP level.
- Training of district agricultural officers in participatory scenario planning for developing agro-climate advisories, community facilitation and engagement with women and ethnic minorities.
- Co-development of seasonal and ten-to-fifteen-day agro-climate advisories through Scenario Planning workshops.
- Advisory dissemination or communication, through paper bulletins, village notice boards, radio, television, SMS and other appropriate channels.

### 143. Advisors produced by multi-stakeholder platforms will be disseminated through traditional local administrative systems via written notifications, loudspeakers, TV and radio, through farmer champions from each platform disseminating advisories to their peers, and through mobile phone applications. The project will also train an SMS operator to communicate via SMS and mobile phone applications. Where appropriate, transition to ethnic minority language dissemination channels may be considered for the project, and ensure that local languages are also trained in the system and will encourage sharing with the government in other districts to promote potential replication through government systems.

Key sub-activities include:

#### 2.3.1 Train 50 hydromet and DARD staff on generating and interpreting down-scaled forecasts for use in agricultural planning (eight training over four years for 50 participants)

### C.4 Background Information on Project Sponsor (Executing Entity)

104. The Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development (MARD) is the project sponsor, or called as Executing Entity (according to GCF terminology), or as implementing Partner (IP).

105. MARD is a government agency responsible for agriculture in the fields of agriculture, forestry, salt production, fishery, irrigation/water services and rural development nationwide, including state management functions with regard to land use planning, water resources, and environmental protection. It has been recognized by laws and authorities based on Decree No 120/2017/NĐ-CP dated 3 December 2017 issued by the government.

106. Following the National Implementation Modality (NIM) guidelines which is indicated in HIPMID document and UNDP POP, MARD has been actively involved in the implementation of various projects funded by international donors including the monitoring and evaluation of project interventions, achieving project outputs, and for the effective use of credit lines.

107. Under MARD, the Central Project Office for Water Resources (CPO) will be delegated to coordinate the project.

108. CPO is a department under MARD, serving as focal point with donors to implement irrigation development projects according to the national strategy and policy on water resources management. The CPO is responsible for managing, monitor and consolidate information on projects according to laws and other international COA regulations. CPO has been actively involved in the implementation of donor-funded projects including the successful implementation of donor-funded projects. CPO has wide experience managing foreign funded projects and has successfully implemented various irrigation development projects under the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development Agency (MARD) projects in the irrigation sector, CPO is therefore very knowledgeable about consultant recruitment and project management. The CPO has also been actively involved in the implementation of donor-funded projects implementation. CPO already works closely with ministries including the Ministry of Planning and Investment, the Ministry of Finance, and other relevant agencies at both national level and provincial level.

109. At the country level, specifically through ADB WEAD project, MARD, through CPO will also tap into on-going partnerships with development partners (e.g., GIZ, JICA, World Bank, ADE, etc.), CGI, NGOs and private sectors to mobilize resources for irrigation development projects.

110. UNDP provides the required financial resources to CPO to carry out project activities during the annual cycle. These arrangements are made through a Transfer Agreement (TA) or a Letter of Credit (LoC), or a combination of both. The TA/Transfer (HATC), of which three cash transfer modalities are available: direct cash transfer, direct payment, and remittance to CPO bank account. The TA/Transfer is subject to the approval of the UNDP Country Director as approved.

115. Five DARDs as 'Responsible Parties' are directly accountable to MARD. MARD works with Responsible Parties in order to take advantage of their specialized skills, to mitigate risk and to relieve administrative burdens.


### C.5 Market Overview

101. The new Irrigation Law that came into effect on 1 July 2018 is expected to create private investment into secondary irrigation schemes through economic incentives, including the possibility in government investment/refinancing in large-scale irrigation truck systems. The re-application of irrigation pricing as regulated by the law will be based on the cost of production and payment for these services as part of the cost of production, thereby reinforcing demand-side efficiency. In addition, the new law also provides a framework to establish Irrigation Management Companies (IMCs) expected to ensure not only the quantity but also the quality of water provision for the development of irrigation schemes. The law is expected to provide an enabling environment for IMCs to be set up and pursued. Domestic and export markets are reasonably well-developed, and in the longer run, the gradual removal of subsidies for irrigation services will stimulate different market players to jump in.

### C.6 Regulation, Taxation and Insurance (if applicable)

#### Land Contribution

No prior permissions are required for land contributions for ponds. In the case of shared community ponds, public land contributed by the commune/district authorities is governed by the provincial level land use plan that has been approved by the People's Committee of the province. The land will be transferred to the commune/district and then to the district followed by the commune authority. Only in the case of conversion or change of purpose/land use permitted by the provincial level land use plan, the land will be transferred directly from the commune/district to the contributing household would retain ownership of the lands/ponds, and agreement shall be reached and signed among the parties involved (the contributing household, the commune/district, and the pond owner). There is no need for an ownership agreement, but there are agreements that pertain to terms and conditions on how households share the water in the ponds.

#### VAT Management Under UN-Supported Projects in Viet Nam

There are no applicable licenses or permits for the implementation of the project. In addition, there are no tax implications or regulations applicable for water security interventions i.e., the risk management, flood control, water storage systems, and other related activities under the UNDP-supported projects. CRA packages will have access to climate information, finance and markets proposed in the project. As the five provincial Departments of Agriculture and Rural Development (DARD) are responsible for the implementation of the project, they will be required to comply with Government of Viet Nam’s (GVN) rules and regulations. Following Public Procurement Regulation of the GVN, all procurement activities under the project will be conducted in accordance with the regulation. UNDP has VAT and Tax exemptions which will be applicable for the part of the procurement plan implemented by UNDP.

#### Insurance

There are no specific insurance policies relevant for the project activities; however, insurance shall be applicable for all minor or high-value project assets including project vehicles, ICT equipment and staff recruited for the project in accordance with UNDP rules and procedures.

### C.7 Institutional / Implementation Arrangements

The project will be implemented under the National Project Implementation Model (NPM) according to the Strategic Partnership Agreement between UNDP and the Government of Viet Nam, the One UN Plan (2017-2021) in Viet Nam, and the policies and procedures outlined in the UNDP POP (see below). The national project implementation model is a framework for UNDP’s country programme that outlines how UNDP will implement the project in compliance with UNDP rules and regulations, policies and procedures (including the NIM Guidelines), as well as other relevant laws and regulations. The NPM includes the following components: Strategic Partnership Agreement (SBA), together with a UNDP project document**, which will be signed by the Implementing Partner and UNDP Country Director. A Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) between the Government of Viet Nam (GVN) and UNDP was signed in 1978. The SBA was signed as per GVN Law No. 20/1996.

The project will be implemented under the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development (MARD). MARD is accountable to UNDP for managing the project, including the monitoring and evaluation of project interventions, achieving project outcomes, and for the effective use of UNDP resources. UNDP, in agreement with the Government of Viet Nam, will provide technical assistance to MARD on the implementation of the project. The MoU between MARD (on behalf of the GVN) and UNDP will also provide oversight through UNDP Country

### UNDP's Role
158. UNDP provides a three-tier oversight and quality assurance role involving UNDP staff in Country Offices and at regional level, as well as independent external auditors. This ensures that project activities are carried out with objective and independent project oversight and monitoring functions. This role ensures appropriate project management and accountability for the project. The Project Steering Committee is responsible for the Project Management function; the Project Steering Committee cannot delegate any of its quality assurance responsibilities to UNDP or any other entity. UNDP will provide day-to-day supervision, as well as oversight in accordance with the GCF. As an Accredited Entity to the GCF, UNDP is required to deliver GCF-specific oversight and quality assurance functions including: (i) Day-to-day oversight supervision, (ii) Oversight of the project completion, (iii) Oversight of project reporting.

160. **Governance Arrangements**: The project will be governed by a Project Steering Committee. The Project Steering Committee (PSC) is co-chaired by MARD and UNDP, and comprises the following agency members: CPO, MARD, and UNDP. The PSC will meet at least once every six months in Dak Lak province, Hanoi city, North Thuan and Binh Thuan province. The PSC is responsible for making, consensus, management decisions. PSC decisions will provide direction to the project implementation team and ensure that the project operates efficiently and effectively under competitive conditions.

161. **The PSC will be comprised of**:
- An Executive (role represented by National Implementing Partner) who will chair the project ownership and co-chairs - The PSC.
- A Senior Supplier representative who will provide guidance regarding the technical feasibility of the project, compliance with the DARs (in collaboration with PPCs) of the five provinces and UNDP to manage and implement the overall Universe of Projects.
- Senior Beneficiary representatives from five DARS as Dak Lak, Dak Nong, Khanh Hoa, North Thuan and Binh Thuan who will provide guidance on how to measure the realization of project benefits from the perspective of project beneficiaries; PPCs will also be represented.

### Implementation and Management Arrangements

162. **CPO**: The CPO is responsible for overall planning and budgeting, collaborating with other ministries, sectors and government entities to implement the project performance management system, bidding for the provision of project consultancy services; coordination of the implementation of project activities; preparation of project progress reports (quarterly); and the implementation of project audit, operation of auditors, monitoring and preparation of overall project reports (annual). The CPO will work closely with the Provincial Project Management Units (PPMUs) on a quarterly basis. PPMU is responsible for supervising and expediting the implementation progress of the RP's activities.

# RATIONALE FOR GCF INVOLVEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 47 OF 102

### D.1 Value Added for GCF Involvement

#### 167. Vietnam is experiencing a crucial financing gap to cover the incremental costs of adapting to climate change.

According to Vietnam's NDC, the national budget will only be able to meet one-third of the financing required to implement adaptation activities. The World Bank estimates that to finance the implementation of its climate change adaptation activities will cost approximately US$47.1 million annually. External funding support is needed for the implementation of these activities in order to ensure their effectiveness and efficiency, particularly in high priority sectors such as agriculture, where the majority of the poor and near-poor are employed.

#### 168. Between 2012-2016, Vietnam's debt burden increased significantly as a percentage of GDP (to an effort to support large infrastructure projects). The World Bank has approved US$35 million in financing for two new projects that have only approved for large infrastructure projects, many of which have been in the pipeline for years. New projects, or those that are not yet in the pipeline, will be difficult to finance given Vietnam's debt burden.

Vietnam continues to invest in baseline infrastructure like WEIDAP, requiring to bring water for irrigation to the affected areas in the Mekong Delta and the Red River Delta. The project also includes a component on climate change adaptation of ensuring water security in a changing climate. For poor and near-poor smallholders in the two regions, investment is crucial as they are particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts given their weak financial capacities, which are further undermined by climate change.

#### 169. GCF resources will be used to empower the poor and near-poor farmer population, which has experienced an increased vulnerability due to climate change. In water-intensive crops such as rice, farmers in this group have limited access to market and lending institutions. Investing in the connectivity of poor and near-poor farms to WEIDAP infrastructure will enable them to access irrigation water for their crops.

Investing in the connectivity of poor and near-poor farms to WEIDAP infrastructure will also increase vulnerability by allowing for the production and sale of marketable climate-resilient crops as well as increased participation in value chains. This is particularly important given that many smallholder farmers are not able to access credit or other financial services, which would enable them to invest in irrigation equipment.

#### 170. In the absence of GCF support, the Government of Vietnam will be unable to invest sufficiently in building the capacities of poor and near-poor farmers to adapt and manage their rain-fed and irrigated farmlands in the face of growing climate change impacts. Without sufficient investment in these areas, they may not be able to increase production, augment water storage for rain fed lands, providing climate information for more informed decision making, or access markets for their crops. In addition, poor and near-poor farmers will continue to experience vulnerability by allowing for the production and sale of marketable climate-resilient crops as well as increased participation in value chains.

#### 171. As long as poor and near-poor farmers are unable to access sufficient credit or other financial services to invest in incremental finance in the form of grant resources to deliver direct resilience benefits to vulnerable poor/near-poor farmers, thereby increasing the effectiveness of the larger GVIDAC/WEIDAP loan investment, particularly in regard to smallholder farmers. ### D.2. Exit Strategy

The strategy towards long-term financial sustainability is phased into immediate, medium and long term objectives for a project that will be implemented in phases. The first phase focuses on improving water infrastructure for climate resilience; poor and near-poor farmers have the capacities to apply climate-enhancing agricultural practices and technologies. The second phase aims at scaling up these practices through public-private partnerships.

The phases show increasing project scope consistent with greater financing from both private and public sources, including contributions from fiscal and multilateral finance institutions. See table below, for a succinct overview of the phased strategy.


| Investment in improved water infrastructure | Immediate term | Medium term | Long term |
|---|---|---|---|
| WEDIFAP/GCF investment plan increases access | WEDIFAP/GCF investment plan increases access | National adaptation plan as project as model for replication | NDC targets become law, new standards required to meet improved climate standards for water infrastructure |
| GCF supported advisories and farmer field schools | Approach included in Gov 2030 investment plan and replicated nationally | Next phase FAD farmers group proposal scales up knowledge to new areas | National scale up of approach through NDC/ NAP focus on agricultural transformation. |
| GCF investment enables poor/near-poor apply improved practices | Previously 'too-poor' farmers now finance from IFC/Social bank if required | Public private co-finance for climate info services | National extension services to replicate to other provinces |

### Government commitment and institutional capacities

This project has strong governmental commitment as it builds on the Vietnamese government's long held policy of support to agricultural development and smallholder farmers through extension and technical assistance. Infrastructure improvements will be supported by a cadre of DARD agricultural extensionists to support farmers in agroecosystem vulnerability analysis, application of

### RATIONALE FOR GCF INVOLVEMENT

#### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 49 OF 102

The project will build the capacities of key mass organizations—the Farmers Union and the Women’s Union—to support smallholder farmers in developing their own climate-resilient agricultural practices.

The project will bring state banks—the Agricultural Development Bank and the Social Policy Bank—as well as other lenders, including the LionPeaBank and the Women Union (microfinance), to FFS to engage directly with the farmers. The project will also work with the government to develop a national framework for climate plans. The project will work with lending institutions to develop products and financial instruments specific to the conditions of smallholder and poor-near-smallholder farmers, as well as train their staff in credit and development of small-scale business plans.

The government of Vietnam will be responsible for oversight and management of the maintenance irrigation infrastructure. The WEADAP irrigation systems will be managed over the long-term with adequate maintenance of infrastructure and equipment. The WEADAP mainline infrastructure will be managed by the Gov’t, and local farmers will be responsible for the operation and maintenance of the distribution lines. Water pricing is currently being studied as part of the WEADAP project and is expected to result in a proposal for a system of water charges that will be based on volume used and will be paid directly by the farmers themselves.

Finally, lessons learned from project experience will be identified and codified by GAFD staff for publication and dissemination to policy makers, as well as other institutions and organizations, with the primary aim of developing the policy and programmatic frameworks for upscaling best practice more widely.


#### Capacity Building

Poor-near-smallholders will contribute their labor, as well as materials and potentially cash, to the assembly, operations and maintenance of irrigation equipment and the relatively modest infrastructural elements connecting that equipment to the mainline. The project will provide training in the use of these systems, secure ownership and commitment to the ongoing operations and maintenance of these connecting systems, as well as of the new infrastructure. The project will also train farmers on how to operate and maintain their own line connections and on-farm systems will be maintained by the farmers themselves either individually or, for larger connections, in groups. The project will provide training in the use of water-efficient technologies and operations and maintenance by the project. The selection of water efficient technologies to be applied will be based in part on the cost of installation and operation, as well as their ability to meet the needs of smallholder farmers at a reasonable cost. To ensure sustainability of farmer connections, farmers will be trained in planning and expansion of irrigation systems, including the use of local materials and labor. The project will also assist them build their organizational capacities to program and manage irrigation, monitor usage, and develop O&M funding mechanisms for any new infrastructure that they may wish to add.

Ongoing smallholder application of climate-resilient production practices and sustain will be sustained with the support of DARD staff started to provide technical assistance and training in farmers to FFS, as well as mentoring and coaching by GAFD staff. The project will also work with local organizations to develop a knowledge base based on their successful experience with crop production and thoroughly trained by the project to extend their knowledge to other smallholder farmers. The project will also support the development of an advisory service that works lead farmers in adapting traditional agro-ecosystems and their vulnerability to climate change using indigenous knowledge, as well as through training and mentoring. ### Access to credit and markets

1. Effective value chains provide a fundamental incentive to drive and support the adoption of climate-resilient crops and practices. However, poor producer participation in these value chains requires access to credit as well as markets.
2. This project will support smallholders to access credit and participate in markets with leveraged co-financing from GCF. The project will also provide technical assistance for the development of a business approach to climate-resilient cropping systems. GCF will also support the establishment of Climate Innovation Platforms bringing together key stakeholders such as input providers, buyers, etc., to coordinate their activities in support of climate-resilient value chain development.
3. The project aims to galvanize a sustainable private sector dynamic among poor and near-poor farmers by affording them access to credit and markets through the establishment of Climate Innovation Platforms (CIPs). These CIPs will generate the revenues for reinvestment in climate resilient production systems to meet the ongoing demands of evolving market conditions. The project will also provide technical assistance for the development of a business approach to climate-resilient crop production and thus to build their capacities to access credit as well as markets.
4. Agricultural inputs, soil and water management improvements, and QM of irrigation systems and equipment all require continuous financing so that smallholders can enhance or maintain the climate-resiliency of their agro-economic systems. The project will provide technical assistance for the development of business plans for their small-scale production systems in order to access markets for their crops. Participating farmers will receive training and mentoring on how to develop straightforward business plans and how to access markets for their climate-resilient products. They will also be provided with information on how to manage their finances, including how to track their crop and other assets, as well as market information and advice to allow them to plan, store, transport and market their crops.

### Private sector engagement and stakeholder platforms for sustained resilient investments

5. Sustaining access by small farmers to markets is enabled or facilitated through partnerships with private sector entities. These partnerships will be crop-specific and require coordination up and down the value chain to ensure quality of supply, as well as to provide technical assistance to producers, credit and input suppliers, buyers, credit providers, and technical assistance purveyors, each with a vested interest in ensuring the success of the project. The project will also support the development of Climate Innovation Platforms (CIPs) that come together on Climate Innovation Platforms to develop commodity-specific strategies that encompass all aspects from production through to marketing. These CIPs will provide technical assistance and training for smallholder farmers, vulnerability assessments and agricultural market information, and establish partnerships to facilitate producer access to markets. The project will also support the development of a network of climate-resilient stakeholders in a particular value chain, including farmers, private sector buyers, government technical assistance agencies, credit providers, input suppliers, and other relevant actors. CIPs will convene these stakeholders with research institutions and others, as necessary. CIPs will discuss climate-resilient agricultural production and marketing strategies, share best practices, provide training on climate-resilient crop production systems, evolve their business models to ensure the sustainability of their value chain development initiatives, and dialogue with authorities and policy makers. The participating farmers will be provided with technical assistance for the development of a business approach to climate-resilient production, communicate and exchange information, and dialogue with authorities and policy makers. The participating farmers will also receive training on how to develop straightforward business plans as they evolve towards more structured contractual relationships with successful marketing and revenue generation.
6. The CIPs will be hosted and led by DARD district or provincial staff and linked directly to the Farmer Field Schools in order to ensure that smallholder farmers are part of the value chain development initiatives. DARDs will continue to host and facilitate CIPs after the formal project ends.

# RATIONALE FOR GCF INVOLVEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 51 OF 102

### Operations and Maintenance post-project O&M

#### 187. O&M of project-established infrastructure and technologies will be carried out through a community-centered system, with support from Gov't. O&M will be focused on the three primary aspects of Output 1: Enhanced water security for smallholder farmers in the WEDAP region. These include last-mile connections (sub-activity 1.2), rainwater harvesting and storage facilities (sub-activity 1.3) and irrigation systems (sub-activity 1.4). The O&M system comprises a series of relevant tasks, operations and technologies (please see Annex XIII for detailed O&M activities).

#### 188. Project beneficiaries will be fully responsible for maintaining tools and equipment provided by the project, with technical assistance and training from local DARD staff. Smallholder investments in O&M will be enabled through enhanced water security and increased access to credit. The project will provide a range of services to support access to credit and increased water security. For the rainwater harvesting and storage infrastructure, the project will provide tools and equipment for maintenance and repair, as well as technical assistance and training for stakeholders using shared ponds and equipment. Farmers learn upkeep and maintenance of water harvesting systems through training provided by DARD staff in each district. Community and Gov't financing will be fully responsible for post-project O&M. The project will ensure that the community has a clear understanding of their role in ownership and management to maximize the likelihood of continued operational and financial viability of project investments.

#### 189. O&M of last mile connections to the WEDAP systems follow a similar pattern in that farmers will be primarily responsible for O&M of these connection and where connections are shared, farmers will organize upkeep and maintenance through community-based organizations. The project will provide technical assistance and make straightforward repairs on the irrigation equipment and related simple infrastructure. All beneficiaries will be equipped with the necessary skills to maintain their own systems. The provincial government has committed USD 3,972,659 for a period of 10 years after the five-year project implementation period. Of this amount, the provincial and district authorities will commit USD 1,204,906 while smallholder contributions are calculated at approximately USD 8,000 per farm. The project will provide technical assistance to farmers in maintaining their connections to the WEDAP infrastructure. USD 2,224,906 towards on-farm water storage facilities including rainwater harvesting and storage systems. USD 153,750 for maintenance of irrigation equipment and related simple infrastructure. Communities are familiar with the commitments in time and effort to maintain water harvesting and irrigation systems and, based on consultations and established practice, have agreed to support O&M of project-produced infrastructure and equipment.

### E.1 Impact Potential

#### E.1.1 Mitigation / adaptation impact potential

The project directly contributes to GCF Fund Level impact areas of (1.0) increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods in climate-vulnerable communities, and regions and (2.0) increased resilience of health and well-being, and food and water security.

The GCF Results Framework outcomes that this project fits under are:

- Strengthened capacity for use of weather and climate information services in the Mekong Delta and South-Central Coast regions of Vietnam,
- Strengthened use of climate information, and
- Strengthened awareness of climate change impacts on agriculture.

#### 190. The project will reduce climate-change induced water stress through a two-pronged approach: (i) from the supply-side with provision of water efficient irrigation infrastructure and increasing water storage capacity to address the risk of droughts; and (ii) from the demand side by providing information, training, access to credit markets, and agricultural practices that reduce water input requirements for food production agriculture.

The project will also contribute to GCF Fund Level impact areas of (1.0) increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods in climate-vulnerable communities, and regions and (2.0) increased resilience of health and well-being, and food and water security by:

- Strengthening the capacity of farmers to use weather and climate information services for improved climate-resilient agriculture and climate-risk informed agricultural planning,
- Adopt climate-smart agricultural practices,
- Access credit markets, as well as climate-resilient agriculture information for improved risk management,
- Vulnerable poor/neo-poor, ethnic minority and women farmers will not only increase their income but also enhance their food security.

Overall, the project will benefit over 139,416 households as direct and indirect beneficiaries in climate-vulnerable areas of five provinces in Vietnam. Specifically:

- Over 21,228 poverty-poor farmer households will have obtained direct access to water for irrigated agricultural production, reducing the impact of climate-related rainfall variability and drought.
- 55,063 small-scale farming households (with farms of less than one hectare) will have the capacity to apply climate-resilient agricultural practices through improved irrigation systems.
- 139,418 households in the 60 communes will receive agri-climate information, market advisories and knowledge on how to adapt their livelihoods to changing climatic conditions.
- 3,600 agricultural extension workers and staff of the Farmers’ and Women’s Unions will benefit from increased skills in training and technical assistance and improved capacities for climate-risk informed planning and implementation.
- Increasing access of poverty-poor farmer households to irrigation and/or improved water storage options enables them to use their land more efficiently. This, coupled with the provision of climate-resilient seeds empowers them to replicate climate resilient agriculture practices currently available only to better off farms.

**The project's support to poor-poor, ethnic minority and women farmers for climate-resilient agricultural production is estimated to yield an incremental increase of 14% in income benefits. A potential increase of even 10% in income benefits would have a significant impact on the economic well-being of their current poverty status over the life of the project and further increase their participation in the formal economy.**

The project outcome will strengthen the adaptive capacity of vulnerable smallholder farmers, especially ethnic minority and women-headed households, to climate change by increasing access to information, credit and market information for improved climate risk management. The project will strengthen institutional capacities at all levels to support the development and adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices and cropping systems as well as irrigation planning and management in response to rainfall variability and drought.

The project has significant impact on the vulnerable populations whose agricultural production has been most affected by climate change, including poor-poor ethnic minority and women-headed households. The project will promote the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices and cropping systems – including greater access to credit and more efficient use of water resources through the development and promotion of water-efficient irrigation technologies.

Community-based and multi-stakeholder interventions across climate-resilient value chains promote economies of scale, reduce transaction costs, and increase market access for poor-poor ethnic minority and women-headed households by affording them the knowledge, information, capacity and investments to reduce their climate vulnerability. The project will support the development of a robust institutional framework at all levels to meet the changing demands of evolving climate change. As such, the project will enable poor-poor farmers to build their capacities to access credit as well as markets.

The project also invests in risk reduction strategies for the targeted households by increasing productivity and sustainability of their production assets primarily land, water and crops. Application of water-efficient irrigation techniques and

## E.2 Paradigm Shift Potential

### Degree to which this proposed activity can catalyze impact beyond a one-off project investment

#### E.2.1 Potential for scaling up and replication

The Theory of Change for the project is provided above in section C.2: Project Objective against Baseline. A paradigm shift is expected as a result of the project’s activities, which will be achieved by empowering smallholder farmers in the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast regions to implement an integrated, multi-stakeholder approach to empowering vulnerable smallholder farmers (i.e., those with low adaptive capacity) through climate information services and advisory services to manage climate risks to both water availability (supply side) and agricultural production (demand side). The adaptation pathway will be achieved by: (i) increasing the resilience of smallholder farmers in the Central Highlands to sufficient water for agricultural production including through both large-scale and supplementary irrigation and (ii) improving the adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers in the South-Central Coast regions. This will empower smallholders in the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast regions to build and sustain the resilience of their agroecosystems, increase their productivity, and improve their livelihoods. They will be able to diversify crops for crop production through irrigation, where possible, and through improved soil and water management on rain-fed lands, and smallholders will need to be able to plan and manage cropping systems that make efficient use of water. However, in order to achieve this, smallholder farmers must have access to climate information services in light of water insecurity, the smallholders need access to climate information and agricultural advice so they can adapt their farming practices accordingly. The project will provide these services through a combination of direct access to climate information, including co-development of agri-climate advisories and training, smallholders can enhance their understanding of how to use this information in decision-making processes. This will enable them to adopt water management measures, and carry out climate-informed planning and decision-making to sustain climate-resilient agricultural practices. The project will also provide technical assistance for the development of irrigation systems towards climate-resilient production by enabling farmers to develop small farm enterprises and then co-invest the ensuing profits in the construction of new irrigation infrastructure.

An important element of this paradigm shift is the potential for increased climate adaptation benefits in the future through broad scale replication expected by expiration of this project’s GCF-funded activities with the WEAD/IDAF investment. The project will provide a replicable model that can be scaled up and replicated to benefit all farmers involved in WEAD/IDAP regardless of socio-economic status. This project is co-financed with WEAD/IDAF, which will continue to support smallholder farmers through climate information services and the irrigation infrastructure while strengthening their technical and financial capacities to build and maintain market-oriented, climate-resilient agricultural systems. This, in turn, paves the way for future expansion of water and

205. The potential for replication at scale is high given the possibilities of expansion of the WEADAP irrigation system, the availability of land suitable for irrigation development, and the existence of a strong institutional framework. As an evaluation of project performance, the GoV may wish to expand the WEADAP irrigation system using loan financing or fiscal incentives. The project has demonstrated that it is possible to use public funds as leverage for private sector incentives such as increased and more stable yields and more diversified sources of income, resulting from the successful implementation of climate-smart agriculture practices. This model of public-private partnership and best practice may be adapted to similar socio-economic and ecological conditions elsewhere in the country. The proposed project will build on this experience by scaling up the WEADAP irrigation system, strengthening extension services, improved climate information, and - through multi-stakeholder Climate Innovation Platforms - private sector actors. This will enable the replication of the model at scale, with public funds enabling CVP chain strategies and cooperation will provide ongoing incentives to smallholders for climate-smart agriculture practices, while also engaging other private sector and government actors.

206. Pathways for future replication and scale include the following:

The successful experience with connecting poor and near-poor farms to WEADAP systems will be used to implement further replication more efficiently.
- As a result of the project's efforts to improve water management for irrigation purposes, as well as for rainwater harvesting and storage, the model can be replicated in other areas where similar conditions exist. The MARD and DAIRDs through standard house training and information dissemination channels. The MARD and Farmers Union trainers. These facilitators will train 1,800 lead farmers in the five target provinces. Two lead farmers will, in turn, train approximately 250 other farmers per province. This will result in a total of 9,000 farmers trained. An additional 193,417 poor and near-poor households in the five project provinces; and an additional 2,845,325 poor and near-poor households in the Mekong Delta. The FFS methodology will be evaluated by MARD and maintained for future use throughout Vietnam.

The Climate Innovation Platform (CIP) is a multi-stakeholder platform that brings together 12 districts of the five provinces through digital and print media as well as through mobile text messaging reaching a total population of approximately 3.7 million people in the five target provinces. The CIP has covered 100% of the total farm population of 1,378,450 households in the five target provinces in central Vietnam. The methodology of advisory production will be evaluated and lessons learned codified for analysis and replication.

The Climate Innovation Platform will continue to meet and coordinate beyond GCF financing as they will be key to scaling up climate-smart agriculture practices and engaging other private sector actors. Value chain stakeholders, organized in CIP, will continue to assess strengths and

E.2.2 Potential for knowledge and learning


---
**Page 63**
---

207 This project will systematize project experience at all levels to create knowledge from lessons learned during project implementation and dissemination of best practices. The project will generate knowledge on climate-smart agricultural practices and disseminate it widely to farmers, as well as other GoV institutions and NGOs in the five largest provinces in particular, as well as elsewhere in Vietnam. The project will generate knowledge of water-efficient irrigation management, including drip irrigation, sprinkler irrigation, rainwater harvesting, use of weather stations and early warning systems, multi-cropping, crop diversification and the use of new climate-resilient crop varieties. Local knowledge of weather patterns and seasonal variations will be combined with scientific research to develop a comprehensive body of codified scientific knowledge as part of co-creation of technical advisories to support farmer adaptation to climate change. In particular, the project will generate knowledge on how to improve water use efficiency in irrigation systems through codified. Knowledge products will include manuals, case studies, and protocols for climate-resilient agricultural production practices, including crop diversification, multi-cropping, and intercropping; improved soil management techniques that manage climate risks and permit smallholders to identify and revise locally suited adaptation measures on an ongoing basis. The project will also generate knowledge of how to improve the capacity of farmers and other relevant government agencies to operate and maintain irrigation technologies and sustain climate-resilient agricultural practices over time.

208 The Climate Innovation Platforms established under this project will be in close contact with Farmer Field Schools and supportive institutions and organizations throughout project duration and will work with them to identify lessons from previous projects. These platforms will also provide a forum for the exchange of knowledge between farmers, FFS staff will apply a gender sensitive analysis to farmer and FFS experience with climate smart agricultural practices, water management techniques, and other adaptation measures. The project will ensure that dissemination is conducted with language accessibility a key factor for ethnic minorities, as well as educational level overall. Dissemination of these products will be done through the Farmer Field Schools, Community Radio, and other communication channels. These platforms will also provide a forum for the exchange of knowledge between farmers, FFS staff will apply a gender sensitive analysis to farmer and FFS experience with climate smart agricultural practices, water management techniques, and other adaptation measures.

The M&E plan (Section H2) will include monitoring the generation of lessons learned and dissemination reports, publication of manuals and case studies, and knowledge products for various media; not only to support project management but also to inform learning across national/sub-national/community levels within Vietnam. 210 Knowledge products will be made available to smallholder farmers in a variety of ways. While print materials (posters, leaflets, booklets) are the most common means of dissemination, they may not reach all target groups (e.g., workers, commune and provincial authorities, etc.). The project will also take advantage of increasing connectivity and digitalization within Vietnam to disseminate knowledge products through a variety of channels. These can be accessed through inexpensive handheld devices. Conventional communications channels – TV, radio, booklets, posters, leaflets, etc. – are still widely used in Vietnam. However, the use of these channels decreases relative saturation of the smallholder audience. Knowledge products will be used in FFS training, with many materials provided to participants to take back to their homes and villages.

### E2.3 Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

#### Enabling effective and sustainable participation of women and ethnic minority farmers in climate-resilient agricultural production beyond the life of the project if the financial risks associated with climate-resilient production continue to remain low and the benefits of such production are clearly communicated to them.

1. **Financial risk mitigation:** The financial risks associated with climate-resilient production will be mitigated first and foremost by a reliable supply of irrigation water, whether conveyed through improved infrastructure or increased access to existing infrastructure. This will also ensure that women and ethnic minority farmers have access to the benefits of rain-on-rainet lands coupled with water efficiency application technologies.
2. **Co-financing for WEDAP:** Co-financing from WEDAP will be used to support the development of irrigation systems and systems for potential use in dense settlements, and GCF financing will support training and technical assistance to ensure that women and ethnic minority farmers have access to efficient technologies to maximize water productivity and climate-resilience.
3. **Investing in enhancing and maintaining the resilience of their production assets:** The project will increase the capacity to manage climate risks, invest in enhancing and maintaining the climate-resilience of their production assets, and generate sufficient revenue from their crops to ensure that they can continue to invest in these assets over time. This will be achieved by providing training on the necessary production and management innovations over time to continually enhance climate resilience and productivity.
4. **Enhancing access to information:** The project will provide training for women and ethnic minority farmers, including agro-climate advisors; improve their ability to assess their climate vulnerability and identify innovations; enhance their agricultural knowledge and skills through training of DARD staff to train farmers in climate-resilient agricultural practices and cropping systems, including the use of water efficient technologies. Hydromet staff will also be trained on how to provide information services to women and ethnic minority farmers.
5. **Collaboration with lead farmers:** The project will support the establishment of a network of lead farmers and in collaborating with lead farmers and DARD staff in producing actionable agro-climate advisors. Co-financing created through the Climate Innovation Platforms (CIP) will be used for capacity building, engagement conditions for increased investment in water storage, technical assistance and information services.
6. **Ensuring continuous investment:** The project will ensure that women and ethnic minority farmers are able to continuously invest on their abilities to generate revenues from sales of their products, a primary enabling indicator of climate-resilience. This will be achieved by providing training for women and ethnic minority farmers for enabling access to reliable markets on a sustained basis.
7. **Establishment of Climate Innovation Platforms (CIP):** The project will establish CIPs in collaboration with the Women’s Federation, Farmers’ Union, as well as cooperatives and other associations, input providers, buyers, technology providers, service providers, and others. Each CIP will be responsible for providing a strategy for climate-resilient production and continuous investment by women and ethnic minority farmers. The CIPs are expected to collaborate in the implementation of the strategy they develop.

#### Innovation, market development and transformation:

1. **Resilience enhancing technologies:** Water efficient irrigation and storage technologies will be introduced and adopted through an inclusive participatory process co-developed. The technologies to be developed will be flexible and applicable to the crops grown by the poor and near-poor cost-effective for one hectares or less of farming land suitable for women and ethnic minorities to apply, low labor-intensive, use locally available materials.
2. **Ensuring continuous investment:** The most vulnerable smallholders in the five provinces are marginalized farmers, particularly poor women and minority women farmers. The project will ensure that they have access to irrigation water through a pilot project out of which it is motivated by the prospect of increased more stable yields leading to greater food security and increased income. Stakeholders in the pilot project will be trained on how to provide information services to marginalized smallholder farmers, particularly women and ethnic minorities to ensure that their conditions are fully enabled to participate in the IFAS or other projects actively. Monitoring of IFAS participation and active engagement by marginalized smallholder farmers is a key indicator of climate-resilience.

available materials and be easily maintained. They will increase water efficiency, reduce farming input costs and ensure resilience to identified climate risks. 216. Farmer Field School methodologies will build a multi-tier training system that builds on farmer experience and institutional agronomic science to ensure adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices. Training of trainers and farmer-to-farmer learning will be supported by the use of mobile technology, such as smartphones, to share knowledge and experience and increased institutional capacities for agriculture extension. New agroforestry systems adapted to specific crops and regions will be developed through participatory research and development processes led by participating poor/near-poor farmers who will receive initial input support through an innovative voucher system (see section 217). These systems will be disseminated through farmer-to-farmer learning activities with multinational partners combining traditional knowledge with contemporary scientific assessments.

217. Climate Innovation Platforms comprised of producers, buyers, input providers, market managers, exporters, technology developers, and other stakeholders will be established to support climate-resilient value chain development, focusing on specific crops. The purpose of these strategies will be to strengthen key value chain stakeholder capacities for the production, marketing, and export of climate-resilient crops while developing sustainable markets for climate-resilient crops. With stronger stakeholder cooperation and coordination in developing countries, both domestic producers and internal markets can be assured of the quality and volume needed to meet and grow market demand.

## The project is aligned with national climate, agricultural and development policies as well as Vietnam's Intended Nationally Determined Contributions (INDCs) under the Paris Agreement. GCF funding will ensure that smallholders have access to sufficient, reliable water supply to overcome rainfall variability and drought by connecting them to eight large-scale irrigation systems being built to supply the Water for Life project in the Central Highlands of Vietnam. The total cost of this project is USD 124.26 million.

### E.3 Sustainable Development Potential

#### Environmental, social and economic co-benefits, including gender-sensitive development impact

##### Environmental co-benefits

The target project areas—particularly rain-fed lands—are subject to significant land degradation processes that are exacerbated by rainfall variability and extreme events. Incidence of extreme rainfall has resulted in accelerated erosion as intense rain overcomes the ability of the soil to absorb it, causing soil to be carried rapidly to rivers and streams. This results in sedimentation in waterways and loss of topsoil, which is critical for maintaining structural properties. To reduce land degradation processes, a key strategy of this project will be to control

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROGRAM | PAGE E3 OF 102

### Reducing direct impact of rainfall on soils will be managed through cropping systems that maintain soil cover for extended periods.

- **Cropping Systems**: Will include agroforestry, multi-cropping, and other cover crops that provide a barrier against runoff. These systems will also incorporate climate-resilient crop varieties adapted to the region.
  
### At the same time, the use of crop residues on farm fields will reduce raindrop impact and velocity.

- **Crop Residues**: On-farm fields will be managed with residue management practices such as conservation tillage, rainwater harvesting and storage, grassed-lane buffers, gullies, plugs etc. Farmers in the target area will be trained to use these techniques effectively.
  
### Environmental benefits will also accrue from diversification of production and irrigation.

- **Diversification**: Smallholder farmers will diversify their crops and livestock systems by adopting a variety of farming methods such as mixed cropping, which will improve soil cover, strengthen nutrient cycling, enhance rainfall infiltration and improve water retention. This will lead to greater biodiversity in the ecosystem.
  
### Project interventions are explicitly designed to contribute to a paradigm shift that will provide poor-income farmers.

- **Project Interventions**: Will be specifically targeted at women and ethnic minority farmers who have historically been marginalized from access to resources, knowledge, and decision-making power. By supplying these farmers with the necessary means to access water for irrigation, the resulting increase in yields can lead to greater food security and improved livelihoods.
  
### Improving poor farmers' and especially ethnic minority and women farmers' access to climate information as well as their ability to adapt to changing climatic conditions.

- **Climate Information**: The project will provide these farmers with access to up-to-date weather forecasts, crop advisories, and other relevant information. This will help them make informed decisions about when to plant or harvest crops.
  
### Over 95% of poor and near-poor farmers in the project's target area are women.

- **Gender Focus**: The project aims to address all dimensions of gender inequality (her own aspirations/empowerment; her family's aspirations/empowerment through which she negotiates her socio-economic development); and structure (the environment that surrounds and conditions her).
  
### Agency will be addressed by:

1. Providing practical and user-friendly information to women and men equally on climate change adaptation.
2. Facilitating the exchange of knowledge, skills, and experiences among farmers taking adaptation measures.
3. Through the women-only FFS and the ACIS technical groups, providing a safe and trusted platform or environment for group mentoring, social learning, building confidence, self-image and self-esteem.

E.4. Needs of the Recipient

### Vulnerability of the beneficiary country and population (Adaptation only)

237. Climate change is resulting in increasing deficits in surface and ground water availability in Vietnam for agricultural production with longer periods of severe water scarcity during the dry season and increased intensity of droughts. Agricultural productivity will be negatively affected by climate change, particularly in the Central Highlands where it is already low. This will make small-scale farmers vulnerable to reduced water availability on rain-fed lands, within this group, poor and near-poor farmers are most vulnerable. The vulnerability of these groups is further exacerbated as they rely heavily on cash crops, particularly cash crops, which will significantly reduce household and national level revenues, income and employment. The financial impact of climate change will be felt across the country with a greater impact in rural areas where there will be a significant loss of farm jobs, wages, and net farm incomes, which finance various social needs (e.g. health, education).

238. Two of the regions most vulnerable to climate risks are the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast. For both regions, climate change is expected to severely impact agricultural productivity, primarily through reduced water availability for irrigation. The population in these two regions is predominantly rural and poor. The most vulnerable population is composed of poor/peasant farmers, particularly women and ethnic minority farmers, growing subsistence crops on small plots of land. These groups are also more likely to be small-scale or subsistence smallholders cultivating one or two crops in lowlands but with limited access to irrigation and dependent on streamflow for water supply. The vulnerability of these groups is further exacerbated by their status as an ethnic minority membership, and gender inequality; thus prioritization for adaptation support is urgent. As stated in the World Bank’s 2013 report “The Social Dimensions of Adaptation to Climate Change in Vietnam”, poverty, lack of access to land, limited education, low levels of social inclusion, and poor health are all factors that influence vulnerability. Economic inequalities and differences in access to livelihoods or land, and other factors determine vulnerabilities of households.

239. A World Bank study identifies the main social vulnerabilities for both regions. For the Central Highlands, these include high numbers of ethnic minorities, migrants to the region, and farmers depending on rain-fed and subsistence agriculture. The vulnerability is further exacerbated by poverty, particularly among ethnic minority groups, and a dependence on rain-fed agriculture in many areas. Ethnic minorities are less likely to be represented in key socio-economic development indicators. Ethnic minority women are less likely to be represented in community decision-making processes. In the South-Central Coast region, there are high numbers of ethnic minorities across the five provinces of the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast regions with the majority in the Central Highlands. The study identifies that ethnic minorities are more vulnerable than other groups due to poverty (50% of the population lives below the poverty line), a lack of access to land, and a smaller number in the South-Central Coast Provinces of Khan Hoa and Binh Thuan (37.7% and 74.7% of the total population respectively). Indigenous ethnic minorities in the South-Central Coast provinces are mostly from the Rade, Chay, and Roong ethnic groups. The study also identifies that women and children are more vulnerable socio-economically; groups most vulnerable to climate change impacts: pregnant and nursing women, children, the poor, and those living on small plots of land in lowland or upland areas. Govt statistics confirm the higher than average vulnerabilities of these groups in the target provinces.

241. This project is focused precisely on assisting the most vulnerable farmers—the poor/peasant farmers, particularly women and ethnic minority farmers—in the two target regions to build the climate resilience of their primary agriculture

production assets: water, land and crops. These farmers will receive direct support to guarantee their access to water provided by an ADB-financed irrigation infrastructure program, as well as through improved water storage on rain fed lands, coupled with water efficient irrigation equipment to maximize water productivity. At the same time, while many of these farmers are already adopting climate-resilient agricultural practices and cropping systems, only the most vulnerable farmers will receive initial input support for adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices and cropping systems in their agroecosystems and farms. Farmers will also participate in analyses of agroecosystem vulnerability through co-development of location-specific adaptation plans with local government officials and other stakeholders at provincial level.

To ensure that poor/near-poor farmers can generate sufficient capital to continue to invest in the climate-resilient agricultural practices, they receive specific training in small business planning, including assistance in accessing credit and linking to markets.

The project will invest in adaptive capacities of farmers in five provinces of southern Vietnam, focusing on equal access for women and poor/near-poor households. The project will provide input support for improving their assets, skills, knowledge, and capacities for adaptive agricultural planning and implementation; the project will enhance the capacity of local government officials to develop location-specific adaptation plans, strengthen market linkages for their climate-resilient products, and enhance their incomes so that they can continue to invest in resilience of their agro-ecosystems.

#### E.4.2 Financial, economic social and institutional needs

**E.4.2.1 Institutional needs:** The impacts of climate variability on the targeted provinces are aggravated by insufficient institutional capacities for extension of climate-resilience knowledge and technical skills, and provincial and local authorities’ lack of capacity to plan and implement adaptation strategies. In addition, there is a need to reallocate budget allocations to the relevant Ministries and other institutions responsible for climate change adaptation coordination and implementation. The current institutional arrangements are not adequate in terms of water management and irrigation development are inadequate given the emerging and projected costs of meeting climate change adaptation needs. Furthermore, while there has been some progress in building capacity to meet the challenges of climate change is required, resources have been insufficient to meet this need. This project will build on existing efforts by providing additional support for training and extension services for smallholder entrepreneurs and train trainers in climate-resilient water management and agricultural practices and systems.

**E.4.2.2 Economic needs:** The effects of rainfall variability and drought on agricultural production are particularly egregious for household economies of the poorest poor, minority women and farmers, especially those farming rainfed crops. Many of these households have limited access to credit or other financial services; therefore, outmoded cropping strategies have resulted in a poverty trap for those without access to water, who lack the skills of knowledge to adopt climate-resilient agricultural practices, or do not receive actionable agro-climate information for climate risk management.

The project will stimulate the private sector initiative of poor and near-poor farmers by providing them with the knowledge and skills needed to adopt climate-resilient agricultural practices. The project will also provide the required resources for investment in climate resilient production systems to meet the ongoing demands of climate change adaptation, while ensuring that these investments are sustainable. Farmers will be supported to market their products at a business approach to climate-resilient crop production and thus build their capacities.

The project will increase productivity and stabilize agricultural production of poor and near-poor farmers by mitigating the climate vulnerability of their production assets, primarily land, water and crops. Application of water efficient irrigation equipment, coupled with improved irrigation infrastructure and rainwater harvesting systems, will increase, stabilize and sustain yields while enhancing the climate-resilience of their agroecosystems. Softening the impact of extreme weather events on poor/near-poor farmers by reducing their exposure to climate vulnerability by improving, maintaining and sustaining the climate resilience of their production assets.

and flash floods; heavy rainfall and landslides and extreme temperatures (cold and heat waves). Increased exposure of people and economic assets has been the major cause of long-term increases in economic losses from weather- and climate-related disasters.13,14 The year 2016 was particularly severe, with an acute El Niño-induced drought that caused widespread crop failures, reduced livestock production, increased food insecurity, and depressed prices; heavy rainfall events causing flooding in the North and South-Central Coast and Central Highlands regions of Vietnam; and a tropical cyclone (Typhoon Damrey) in November 2017. The year 2016 alone, more than 2.2 million people were affected, 330 people died, and estimated US$ 1.7 billion of damage and loss occurred, or approximately 0.83 percent of the country’s Gross Domestic Product.

According to Vietnam’s NDC, the national budget will only be able to cover one-third of the financing that will be required between 2021 and 2030 to implement crucial adaptation measures. Just between 2017 and 2020, the government has committed US$ 45 million for climate change adaptation activities. Between 2012-2016, Vietnam’s debt burden increased significantly as a percentage of GDP, and at its peak in 2013, it reached 89 percent of GDP. This is due to the large number of infrastructure projects. While Government is continuing to invest in crucial basic infrastructure like WEDAP, required to bring water from the Red River to Ho Chi Minh City, this is not enough for Vietnam’s climate change adaptation needs. The government will require to ensure water security for smallholders, who are thus required to obtain their own financing. For poor and rural households, access to credit for agricultural production equipment is prohibitive given farmers’ lenient financial capacities, further weakened by climate change.

Private sector actors will sustain their effective participation in climate-resilient agricultural production if the following conditions are met: (1) a conducive policy environment; (2) a stable and predictable market price that remains higher. The primary finance risk to farmers, particularly poor/non-poor women and ethnic minority farmers, will have increased due to climate change. This is because of reduced yields from climate-related disasters, lack of infrastructure and last-mile connections or through improved capturistorage of rainwater on rain-fed lands, coupled with the need for more investment in irrigation systems. The government has already invested in the viability of water monitoring mechanisms and systems for potential use in demand-side management, and GCF financed projects will be able to provide additional support. However, there is a need to ensure that these investments are made in a way that maximizes their impact on increasing access to safe drinking water productivity and climate-resilience. Financial risks to farmers will have been further diminished by their increased capacities to manage climate risk, invest in enhancing and maintaining the climate-resilience of their productive assets, and diversify into other income-generating activities or alternative agricultural products.
