E.5.1 National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP) consistency and coherence with existing plans and policies, including NAMAs, NAPs and NAPs

251. The proposed project is aligned with current Vietnamese Government policies and programmes to foster adaptive capacity building in the agriculture sector excluding Vietnam’s priority actions under its Nationally Determined Contribution to Paris Agreement 2015; Action Plan Framework for Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation in the agriculture and rural development sector period 2011 - 2015 and vision to 2030 (MAPCC); Direction on Integrating Climate Change into National Socio-Economic Development Strategy and Plans of the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development sector period: 2011-2015 and Vision to 2030; MARD GHG emission reduction in the agriculture and rural development sector period: 2012 - 2020 and vision to 2030; National Strategy on climate change period 2012 – 2020 and visioning 2030; and the MARD Agricultural Restructuring Plan, Green Growth Action Plan in Agriculture and Rural Development to 2020 focusing on promoting water efficiency use and improve sustainability of agricultural production systems. The project also aligns with the National Strategy for climate-resilient crops and practices, the irrigation Law (which was issued in June 2017) which aims to modernize irrigation infrastructure and promote water-saving technologies, the Water Resources Management Plan, PPP in irrigation and promoting water efficiency technology.

252. National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP) for Adaptation and Mitigation: In 2007, the Prime Minister issued the main disaster risk management strategy, developed by MARD as the coordinating ministry for disaster prevention, response and mitigation in order to minimize the losses of human life and properties, the damage of natural resources and cultural heritages, and the degradation of ecosystems. The NCCAP is a strategic document that ensures the coordination among all relevant sectors and levels. It contains nine priority areas such as:
- Develop hazard risk maps as a decision-support tool to inform planning on flood protection and control systems, water supply and drainage systems.
- Modernize early warning and forecasting capacities and systems for all regions in Viet Nam.
- Strengthen disaster management capacity of local authorities and safety-injury control. Integrate solutions to increase run-off and underground water in dry season.
- Transform the country’s response to natural disaster impacts and make full use of the total natural resources for socio-economic development.
- Community awareness raising and capacity building.

253. National Target Program on Climate Change (NTPC) issued in 2008 stressed the need for mainstreaming climate change responses into social and economic development, while pursuing broader sustainable development goals. The NTPC was developed by MARD with the support of the World Bank (2010) focused on scientific analysis and initial planning and resulted in the first version of the Vietnam Climate Change Strategy (VCCS). The VCCS is a strategic document that sets out the priorities for climate change planning, capacity building and development of sector and provincial action plans. Once the National Climate Change Action Plan was developed, the NTPC RCC concluded and merged into the National Action Plan to Respond to Climate Change 2010-2030.

254. National Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan: As the current most important strategy, the National Climate Change Strategy (VCCS) is a strategic document that sets out the priorities for climate change planning, capacity building changing significant risks to food security and agricultural development, human health, natural resources and ecosystems. The VCCS focuses on three main areas of action: reducing greenhouse gas emission reduction and a shift towards a low-carbon economy but prioritizes action in the early years of Vietnam’s NAMC’s socio-economic conditions and development.

255. The NCCAS and NCCAP Action Plan on Climate Change 2011-2020 outline a large number of priority actions, including among other:
- Upgrading of the hydro-meteorological forecasting and early warning system;

### National Action Program on Reduced Deforestation and Forest Degradation: The National Action Program on Reduced Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (REDD+) in Vietnam (Phase I: 2010-2015) focused on the establishment of policies and systems for REDD+ roll-out, awareness raising and capacity building. Phase II (2016-2020) focuses on the actual implementation at scale of various REDD+ activities.

### Action Plan in Response to Climate Change in Agriculture and Rural Development: In 2016, MARD issued an updated action plan on addressing climate change in the agricultural sector for the period 2016-2020 and with a vision towards 2030. The plan focuses on increasing the resilience of agriculture against climate change; building capacities; adaptation and mitigation actions within the agricultural sector; and prevention and mitigation of disasters.

### Communist Party Resolution on climate change and environmental management: The 2013 Party Resolution 24- NQ/TW "Active Response to Climate Change" is a key policy document that sets out Vietnam's response to climate change as a key national priority issue, with responsibilities shared by the Government, organizations, private sector, and citizens. It calls for the state under the leadership of the Party to play an active role in promoting economic development while ensuring environmental protection.

### Vietnam Green Growth Strategy and Action Plan: The Vietnam Green Growth Strategy (VGGS), approved in 2012 and updated in 2016, is a comprehensive strategy that sets out how Vietnam can achieve sustainable restructuring in order to use natural resources efficiently, reduce greenhouse gas emissions through research and application of new technologies, and improve the quality of life for all Vietnamese citizens. The strategy aims at climate change, contribute to poverty reduction, and drive economic growth in a sustainable manner. Through the National Green Growth Strategy 2011-2020, Viet Nam seeks to achieve a low carbon economy and to enrich natural resources while ensuring environmental protection and social equity. This will be achieved through the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions and increased capacity to absorb greenhouse gas are gradually becoming recognized as key components in Vietnam's national development strategy.

### The Green Growth Action Plan was issued in 2014 and provides more detail for three of VGGS strategic tasks: Water management, energy efficiency, and renewable energy. While the VGGS is a comprehensive strategy that sets out how Vietnam can achieve sustainable restructuring in order to use natural resources efficiently, reduce greenhouse gas emissions through research and application of new technologies, and improve the quality of life for all Vietnamese citizens.

### The National Strategy on Climate Change 2011-2020 seeks to proactively cope with natural disasters and monitor climate. ## National Strategy on Environment Protection to 2020 with Vision to 2030 details solutions to recover and regenerate natural ecosystems, especially mangroves, as well as solutions to increase forest coverage and improve the quality of forests and afforestation, including to turn bare lands and hills green. To ensure recovery of representative natural ecosystems, especially mangroves, then design the planning schemes for their recovery - to coordinate with other sectors and localities in order to mobilize all available resources from domestic and foreign sources on economic sectors and organizations at home and abroad for recovering natural ecosystems, increasing forest coverage, improving quality of forests and afforestation. The strategy also proposes solutions towards boosting recovery, regeneration and protection of natural ecosystems - to localize and protect natural forests and mangroves in the coastal zone and other areas with high biodiversity value; to establish a system of real-time preventing deforestation and illegal exploitation - to continue afforestation and forest protection while securing a sustainable supply of timber products for domestic consumption.

### Plan for Implementation of the Paris Agreement: To advance Viet Nam's commitments towards the 2015 Paris Climate Agreement and the country's Intended Nationally Determined Contribution (INDC), the Govt has approved the National Strategy on Environment Protection to 2020 with Vision to 2030 (hereafter phase) and 2021-2030 ('Implementation' phase) on five components: mitigation; adaptation; human resources development; institutional implementation, measurement, reporting, and verification systems; and the institutional and policy framework.

## E.5.2 Capacity of accredited entities and executing entities to deliver

Since it began operations in Viet Nam in 1977, UNDP has contributed significantly to environmental protection and sustainable development by providing technical assistance for the formulation of policies, strategies, laws, coordination, and information sharing. UNDP possesses a qualified team of experts including international and national experts, helping Viet Nam to implement its commitments under the Paris Agreement.

UNDP is a long-term partner of the Viet Nam Government. UNDP has supported various related policy formulation processes in Viet Nam, including:

- National Strategy on How to Respond to Climate Change (NTP-RCC 2008)
- Climate Change Scenarios (2008; 2016)
- National Strategy for Green Growth Action Plans (2014)

UNDP has built strong relationships with decision-makers and proven its strengths as an impartial provider of technical assistance. UNDP's work in Viet Nam is based on the principle that capacity development is the foundation for UNDP action on policy support. UNDP also plays an advisory role in the process of preparation and approval of regulations and relevant policies, including those related to climate change adaptation and mitigation, environmental assessment and capacity building, bringing a long-term and institutional and people-centred focus to capacity development.

With its central role in the UN system, UNDP facilitates a multi-sectoral approach to help the government respond to climate change. UNDP provides technical assistance for policy formulation and implementation through direct support and inputs to the preparation and consultations for a number of key legal frameworks, including Biodiversity Law; Forest Law; Water Resources Management Law; Environmental Protection Law; Climate Change Control and 2012, Law on Environmental Protection 2014 which includes a chapter on climate change and green growth; and Draft Law on Environment and Natural Resources Tax; the Royalties Law concerning natural resources management.

With the characteristics of multilateral organization, UNDP can provide the dissemination of international norms and standards in environmental protection and climate change practice. In Viet Nam, UNDP has demonstrated its long-term commitment to the provision of technical assistance to affect and sustain the

### Adequacy of the financial structure

284. The government of Vietnam is unable to meet the additional costs of adaptation to evolving climate change, as according to Vision 2050, the national budget can only meet one-third of the financing required to implement adaptation measures in the 2021–2030 period. Between 2017 and 2020, the government estimates that financing priority will be given to climate change adaptation projects with a total cost of US$54 million. This is insufficient to meet the incremental costs of managing climate change risks and impacts is therefore critical, particularly in high priority areas such as irrigation infrastructure. The project proposes to finance 70% of the incremental costs for improving irrigation infrastructure will be financed by the Government of Vietnam across the five provinces with a US$100 million loan from the GCF. This will cover approximately two-thirds of the incremental costs of constructing poor, ethnic minority and women farmers to this infrastructure.

285. GCF grant resources are required to overcome a set of financial, institutional, and market barriers that, although not unique to Vietnam, are particularly strong in the context of the vulnerability and fragility of their agro-ecosystems to increasing climate risk. These include the inability of smallholder farmers to access sufficient inputs for climate-resilient agricultural production; the lack of credit facilities to address the financial risks faced by farmers to the productivity and yield stability of rain fed agro-ecosystems, the incapacity of farmers to access insurance products that would provide them with adequate risk management tools; inadequate water management and agricultural planning; and the inability of farmers to sustain continued investment in the climate-resilient crop varieties and practices they have adopted as a result of this project.

286. Revenues generated by farmers empowered as a result of this project will improve agricultural incomes, as well as finance the purchase of vital inputs, material and equipment to sustain climate-resilient agricultural production and operations. The revenues generated from the sale of surplus produce in the target areas, if any exist, reflects back to the government or the GCF would be extremely challenging; therefore, maximum concessional support is required.

287. The project targets poor-poor farmers, particularly women and ethnic minority farmers who possess limited access to productive resources and services. These groups are among the most vulnerable in Vietnam nevertheless, through their participation in the training and other activities of this project, they will build their organizational capacity and skills for climate-resilient agricultural production. They will also have access to inputs, equipment and materials required for climate-resilient agricultural production. There is very little likelihood of private sector investment in poor-poor farmers’ irrigation infrastructure without the risk reductions to be achieved by this project. The vulnerability of these groups and their agro-ecosystems are beyond the scope of the government with its debt burden.

### Cost-effectiveness

288. The anticipated stability and increases in production from farmers' small irrigated plots during droughts and dry spells, as analyzed and documented in the Feasibility Study, have been the corresponding effects of crop diversification. The resulting production of a surplus will allow smallholders to generate the revenue needed to purchase inputs for climate-resilient agricultural production. This is expected to lead to an improvement of their agroecosystems beyond the project's lifetime. It is expected that increased production of climate-resilient crops and livestock in poor-poor farmers' households will be complemented by improved water management, complementing and building on WEADAP investments in irrigation systems. This integration with the WEADAP

project will ensure efficiency and effectiveness of implementation given common implementation arrangements, which will result in more pronounced and sustainable impacts. In addition to the above-mentioned activities, the project will provide the financial resources to maintain and enhance climate-resilience of agroecosystems beyond project duration through the establishment of a revolving fund for climate-smart agriculture (CSA) demonstration farms, training of trainers, and wider dissemination of agro-climate advisories, which will improve the efficiency and effectiveness of CSA adoption.

289. Design of project investments was based on lessons learned from multiple sources and experiences in Vietnam and elsewhere. The solutions identified during the Feasibility Study and process of analysis and design render a high degree of confidence that the proposed project activities are feasible and sustainable. The project has been designed with an analysis of best practices detailed in Chapter 5 of the Feasibility Study, which are also described in section 208.

290. The selection of project activities was done after detailed consultations and assessments with a multitude of stakeholders regarding agro-ecological conditions and constraints; climate change trends, patterns and expected impacts on agricultural productivity; availability of climate-resilient crops, market linkages; and potential for public-private partnerships to enable access to inputs, finance, and markets. The project activities are designed in such a way that the adoption of CSA practices to mitigate climate change by adopting climate-resilient agricultural practices and cropping systems transforms not only individual farmers but also entire communities. This is because the project will address the underlying social capital of the target provinces and thus their overall ability to face increasing climate hazards sustainably. The project will also ensure increased access to markets for smallholder, particularly women and minority farmers and increased and more effective partnerships for market access and support to value chain integration.

291. The nineteen lessons listed in section 5.3 of the Feasibility Study are a mix of design considerations, best pedagogical practices and training recommendations. These lessons have formed the basis for the selection of FFS as the primary means of project implementation. The lessons include: the importance of being simple and cost-effective way of building on farmers' experiential knowledge to ensure uptake and ownership, as well as farmer empowerment; the need to build on existing community structures and institutions that have a high degree of constituency for this project is the population of marginalized poor and near-poor smallholders, with particular attention given to women and ethnic minorities. The lessons also highlight the importance of engaging in participatory, experiential approaches, who are sensitive to gender and ethnic minority concerns; and possess the leadership skills necessary to lead CSA adoption. The lessons further emphasize the need for a flexible approach around cropping cycles and in convenient locations and at times that are not expensive or disruptive to smallholder farmers' livelihoods. Finally, the lessons highlight the importance of building on existing knowledge over the years there has been an ongoing accumulation of knowledge and best practice in their design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation, which will help gauge escalation of the project component.

## Water storage and management

### Water harvesting technologies

In addition to household rainwater harvesting systems, water storage tanks and wells, a number of bioengineered options for improved water storage have been successfully tested across Viet Nam including compacted clay-boulder dams, bamboo check dams, small-scale earth dams, and the use of plastic bags filled with sand or gravel made of bamboo and plastic sheeting.17 In and verve grass application to combat bank erosion.18 Small-size, easily constructed earthen dams can be used for water storage in areas where there is a high risk of flooding. These structures are also useful as temporary barriers against soil erosion during the wet season.

### Water efficiency technologies

Water-efficiency technologies, for example drip irrigation, micro-sprinklers, overhead sprinklers or precision irrigation, have also been promoted by local authorities and the private sector, but with a very high investment cost and limited availability.19 The CGIAR-ICRAF project (2014) in collaboration with the Vietnamese firm NRD International Development Enterprises, that promoted a low-cost technology option affordable for the poor and not requiring irrigation expertise, was one of the few projects to have been selected as a best practice (see Box 3.5 of the Feasibility Study). To ensure uptake and benefit among the poorest, it is crucial to engage them throughout the entire process: from needs assessment; product design, market development and replication, as potential buyers as well as sellers.20

### Agro-climate information services

**CGAIE (2014) identified eight key lessons learned in scaling up climate services for farmers, based on 18 case studies in Asia and Sub-Saharan Africa:**

- **Rural climate services are enabled and sustained by institutional arrangements and investments in capacity at all levels of government.**
- **The private sector can play a role in the development and delivery of climate information to farmers.**
- **Climate services must be delivered at a local scale to be relevant for farm decision-making.**
- **A seamless suite of forecast, advisory and early warning products, with a range of lead times, enables farmers to integrate knowledge into their farming practices.**

- Giving farmers an effective voice in the design, production and evaluation of climate services increases uptake, legitimacy, and sustainability.
- Integration of meteorological information with local indigenous knowledge may foster trust, local relevance and ownership.
- Face-to-face dialogue between farmers and service providers is an effective way to communicate historic and present-day weather patterns.
- Information and Communication Technologies, in combination with other communication channels, offer expanded opportunities for climate services delivery.

#### 301. Viet Nam

- Aside from television, short-term weather forecasts are also available for free via smartphone applications or the internet, often with animated maps.
- With smartphone usage increasing, including among youth from poor households, mobile phone-based weather information is becoming more popular.
- Mobile and fixed weather stations as integral part of pro-poor agro-climate information services are a must. Experience from North-Central Vietnam shows that farmers who have access to these can be a valuable addition for farmers next to investing in improving the GoV's forecasting system B1.

#### 302. Viet Nam

- In addition, farmers and local authorities have also been successfully engaged in managing village-level low-cost automated weather stations (AWS) to collect data on temperature, rainfall, humidity, wind speed and direction.
- The AWS can be used for providing real-time information on temperature, rainfall, humidity, wind speed and direction at the same intervals as official observations. They help farmers see the difference between the farmer forecast, observations from the nearest official weather station and the actual temperature in the village.

#### Engagement with smallholder farmers

- Three main approaches have been used in Viet Nam of engaging farmers in project activities on agriculture, rural development and climate resilience, with the most successful approach being a group-based learning process called Farmer Field Schools (FFS). The FFS model is based on the following principles:
  - A combination of training-of-trainers (usually by the agricultural extension department), by external experts, and farmer-to-farmer training.
  - External experts are usually involved in the first two cycles targeting 'farmer champions', 'lead farmers' or leaders of farming groups; farmers-led on-site demonstration, testing and learning during the third cycle onwards. And ongoing mentoring support to continue the farmer peer-to-peer learning and sharing.
- The FFS model is based on a combination of training-of-trainers by external experts, local extension services and mass organizations, these latter services still operate based on a traditional top-down farmer education model of lectures, demonstrations and field visits. These services often offer better of-farm or on public land, and limited to no follow-up at the farmer level.

#### Mechanisms for delivering support to farmers

- A variety of options have been used in Viet Nam to deliver financial support to farmers to strengthen agricultural production and climate resilience. These are often combined with technical information and training and have specific conditions per their provision, ranging from aid-kind support to preferential pricing schemes (A1). These

# F1 Economic and Financial Analysis

## Financial analysis

307 The financial analysis has been carried out in accordance with the Guidelines for the Financial Analysis of Projects of the United Nations Development Programme. These guidelines clearly mandate that a financial analysis of proposed projects must be conducted to ensure that the project is financially viable, as this will ultimately result in real and quantifiable financial revenue generation (incremental earnings from the project) or a direct contribution to the GCF trust fund. The Financial Analysis Guidelines also state that the project must be able to generate sufficient funds to cover its own costs over its lifetime.

308 In the case of the proposed project, the analysis shows that the incremental earnings from the project are insufficient to cover the project’s costs and will not result in a positive Net Present Value (NPV) or a Positive Internal Rate of Return (FIRR) computation is carried out for the entire project. It can be seen from the analysis above that with the participation of GCF in the form of Grant, the proposed revenues financed with the grant are insufficient to cover its costs and will not result in a positive NPV or FIRR.

309 The economic analysis was carried out for the project as a whole by comparing with and without-project scenarios following the guidelines for the Economic Analysis of Projects under Global Environment Facility (GEF) (UNEP, 2010). The interventions are considered integrated and benefits should not be isolated given that they all contribute to the overall impact. In order to reflect this, the economic analysis was carried out on a project scenario which is an understanding of the impact of climate change on production regions. Given that the financial cost of the project are largely non-tradable goods and services, a standard conversion factor of 0.9 was used. The discount rate applied in the economic analysis was 12% per year, reflecting the opportunity costs associated with intervention between 15 and 25 years. It was assumed that after the useful life of each intervention, benefits become zero.

310 The economic benefits were derived based on avoided damages from drought and the incremental outputs generated by the project in the form of increased agricultural production. The period for which the benefits are collected (rainwater water is assumed to be 25 years with appropriate O&M cost factored in to ensure sustainability while the CRA and EFA are expected to have stream of benefits for 10 years). The ENPV and economic costs were calculated using a discount rate of 12% per year. The project scenario was assumed to generate a total ENPV of $34.9 million USD assessed at 10%.

## Financial analysis

311 The financial analysis shows that the incremental earnings from the project are $24.8 million which is $22.1 million in economic terms. Costs include construction, detailed design and supervision of the pcrs, CRA packages and farmer loans with setting up of access to markets and credit and maintenance (O&M) during the implementation period and during the lifespan of the pcrs included in the economic analysis.

## F.2 Technical Evaluation

### Identification and selection of technical solutions to mitigate climate change risks to smallholder agriculture in the five provinces of Zimbabwe (Section 3.4)

The identification process for the technical solutions was carried out as described under Section 3.1 above, and the Stakeholder Consultations Annexes X10-15 include: farmers; provincial and district authorities; MARDI, DARDII, NGOs and other stakeholders.

### Agricultural Social Safeguards (ESS) assessment

An Agricultural Social Safeguards (ESS) assessment (see Section F.3) has been rigorously carried out to confirm best design and implementation practices to promote climate-resilient agricultural practices and cropping systems that are appropriate for the Zimbabwean context.

#### Irrigation: The technologies to be applied to bring water from the WEDAP main infrastructure to farmers' plots has been vetted by engineering consultants from the ADB during preparation of the WEDAP project. These assessments have included a review of the design, construction and operation and maintenance (O&M) of irrigation systems and management who drafted the corresponding sub-assessment of the Feasibility Study. Water storage options already exist in the form of existing dams and water reservoirs. The technologies to be applied include furrow pond construction and renovation are well known and tested, including bioengineering of surrounding landscape and feeder canals. Other technologies that will be applied for irrigation purposes include drip irrigation systems, sprinklers, and other systems, none of which present particular technical difficulties in maintenance or use after the results of the pilot project have been evaluated with the successes to innovate the technology in response to changing weather and climate conditions.

#### Climate-resilient agriculture as climate continues to evolve: farmers will need to be able to make required adaptive changes. The key areas for adaptation in socio-economic context include: agro-ecosystem vulnerability and climate-resilient agricultural practices and cropping systems, together with use of improved seeds and other inputs. A number of tools have been developed by the project team including methodologies for identification or adaptation of potential solutions, as well as in the application of solutions already identified. These include: a participatory rural appraisal (PRA) tool; a climate-resilient agriculture training manual; a guide to climate-resilient agricultural practices and cropping systems; use of drought tolerant varieties, etc. These solutions, identified during the corresponding sub-assessment of the Feasibility Study, have been tested in pilot farms in the five provinces but have not been disseminated nor have farmers received training in their application at scale. The project will provide technical assistance to farmers through a voucher system and/or technical assistance with project support. Given the targeting of the project to the most vulnerable farmers (provinces of Manicaland, Matabeleland North, Masvingo, Mashonaland West and Midlands), specific solutions identified during DFS training through a voucher system based on the successful FAO and Action Aid approach will be provided. These include vouchers and unconditional cash transfers to more than 5,000 farmers in Gai La, Oak Dak and Luk Dang.

#### Climate information: the need for localized agro-climate information is essential to enable farmers to effectively utilize climate-resilient agricultural practices and cropping systems. The project will provide access to climate information at the target provinces consists of combining farmers' traditional knowledge with GVI climate information. This has been tested in pilot farms in Zimbabwe, Malawi and Tanzania. The approach used on the PCMSA model - has been tested in Zimbabwe, Burkina Faso, Ghana, Mali, Nigeria, Senegal, Malawi and Tanzania. The project will provide access to climate information through a combination of the Climate Adaptation and Food Security (CAFS), International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) and the Rockefeller Foundation. It has been determined to be a viable and effective alternative to non-participatory weather and

### F.3 Environmental, Social Assessment, including Gender Considerations

#### 319. A Social and Environmental Screening assessment was undertaken to identify and address any potential social and environmental risks that could arise from project activities.
Based on the findings of this assessment, an Environmental Management Plan (EMP) has been developed.

#### 320. The overall social and environmental risk category for this project is moderate. Specific project risks are listed in Section B below, together with appropriate mitigation measures.

The environmental impacts associated with the project are considered acceptable due to the nature of the interventions proposed. These interventions can provide long-term improvements to the lives of communities in the target areas. The project will also promote inclusion, particularly of women and vulnerable groups.

#### 321. Key considerations in minimising environmental and social impacts during the project are outlined in the ESFM.
These include social inclusion and participation, settlement and erosion control, and health and safety for workers and communities.


## Environment

The project target areas, primarily rain-fed lands, are subject to significant land degradation as a result of erosion due to intense rainfall events that are being exacerbated by climate change. Reducing direct impact of rainfall on soils will be managed through cropping systems that maintain soil cover for extended periods. Cropping systems will include intercropping and relay cropping with legumes and other crops that can reduce the erosive impact and runoff velocities on the soil surface. Once on the soil surface, the flow of water across rain-fed lands will be reduced by the use of contour bunds, terraces, and other land management practices such as grassland establishment and storage, grass-lined basins, gabions, gutters, etc.

#### 322. Conversion from bare/ground cover will reduce erosion, both wind and water, which will benefit waterways.
Through reduced runoff, there will be a positive sequence of adopting more climate-resilient agricultural practices, soil organic matter will increase, resulting in greater water holding capacity, increased carbon storage and improved biodiversity.

#### 323. Environmental benefits will also accrue from diversification of production and irrigation: diversified farming systems will result in better land use through nutrient cycling, enhanced carbon sequestration and improved aquifer recharge.
Diversified cropping systems also produce ancillary benefits such as increased diversity and abundance of insect populations, including pollinators.

#### 324. Benefits will include increased food security, improved weather information, improved management of water resources, reduced soil erosion, improved livelihoods for smallholder farmers through crop selection and marketing, improved access to markets.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 83 OF 102

### Physical impacts will be primarily associated with construction and installation of equipment such as ponds, pipes, pumps, etc.

#### Project interventions will be undertaken in areas of Central Vietnam where communities are particularly vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. These communities are largely rural and often have constrained livelihood opportunities.
- The project targets the poor and near-poor; in the target areas 10% of the population are from extremely poor households.
- Socio-economic inequality in climate-vulnerable communities will be reduced through post-harvest farmers gaining access to markets, improved water resources management, increased agricultural productivity, and income increases. With increases in income, poorer farm families will be able to raise their quality of life through increased purchases of food and other goods.

#### Participation in farmer groups will assist in building and maintaining social cohesion through shared ponds, better cooperative linkages to market, peer-to-peer learning and knowledge sharing.
- By implementing Farmer Field School (FFS) training, the project aims to increase the capacity of smallholder farmers to be able to develop mentoring relationships and partnerships with more successful farmers and strengthen their ability to manage risks.

#### Financial risks to farmers will be reduced by their increased capacities to manage climate risk, invest in enhancing and maintaining the climate-resilience of their productive assets, and generate sufficient revenues from commercialization of their produce. The project will also provide financial support for smallholder farmers enabling farmers to develop small farm enterprises and then to invest the ensuing profits in inputs to maintain or improve their livelihoods.

#### The project will also improve the capacity of government and communities to plan, design and manage rural infrastructure that is climate resilient. Guiding documents such as manuals, checklists, and standards will be presented for use by local authorities and communities.

### Furthermore, the project makes provisions for a complaint register along with a two-tiered Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) in accordance with the UNDP’s Gender Equality Policy and the World Bank Group Safeguards Policies. The Grievance Redress Mechanism established goals and objectives along with a process to ensure that complaints are addressed in good faith throughout the process and more importantly, that it will be arbitrary in nature in trying to achieve mutually acceptable resolutions for all parties. The Grievance Redress Mechanism also provides for the covering of costs associated with the resolution of grievances. The project will provide training on how to file a complaint to the complaints to the attention of MARD and UNDP. Finally, the Grievance Redress Mechanism allows individuals and/or communities to lodge their complaints directly with the UNDP or MARD in case they feel that the Office of Anticorruption and Integrity within the UNDP should have any concerns as to corruption, unethical behaviour or where they believe their complaint or grievance has not been adequately addressed.

### Gender Mainstreaming and Implementation

340. The project has been designed with attention paid to gender and social inclusion. The gender analysis undertaken at the design project phase, acts as an entry point for gender mainstreaming throughout implementation, and builds on existing gender equality policies and strategies where they exist. These include those that have been implemented, and national standards where available. The project design takes into consideration a number of key gender issues including: women’s access to land; women’s participation in decision-making processes at all levels of the gender division of labour; women's access to and control over environmental resources; and identification of gender-based vulnerabilities.

341. Based on the Gender Assessment, the project implementation proposes a number of actions to strengthen gender equity from the outset. These include:

- Ensure that women are adequately represented on decision making boards and committees, including the PMU.
- This will also provide positive role models for young women.
- Develop specific strategies to include and target female and ethnic minority farmers for interventions to ensure greater participation in project activities.
- Enhance capacities of both male and female farmers to understand climate change information and use this to inform their farming practices.
- Build capacities of both male and female farmers in climate resilient agriculture production, taking into account women’s daily routines and promoting both genders participation in agricultural decision making
- Build capacity of male and females farmers in farming as a business and value addition for irrigation schemes as well as improving access to markets. This will include training on financial management, record keeping and marketing skills e.g. ensure that at least 25% of FFS facilitators are women and that at least 20% are ethnic minorities.
- Advocacy and awareness should be adjusted to most effectively reflect gender-specific differences. As women and girls have different needs in relation to climate change adaptation, it is important to engage with community improvements to better serve their needs which can increase the adaptive capacity of their communities.
- Address the fact that women often have major roles in producing food, and will therefore be key partners in the co-development of climate and farm practices information messaging so that they are also reached effectively.
- Inclusion of all stakeholders involved in the project to develop awareness raising/training aimed at drawing attention to the importance of access to climate information, improved irrigation and farming practices and gender equality.

342. During project implementation qualitative assessments will be conducted on the gender-specific benefits that can be directly associated with the project. This will be incorporated into the annual Project Implementation Report, Mid-term Report, and Terminal Evaluation.

344. The project will be implemented following the National Implementation Modality (NIM) following UNDP guidelines available at [link]. UNDP will assess the national capacities of the Implementing Partner and Responsible Parties by undertaking an evaluation of capacity following the Framework for Cash Transfers to Implementing Partner to this document. This assessment will be conducted in accordance with the UNDP’s National Implementation Modality followed the UNDP financial rules and regulations noted above and applicable audit guidelines and policies.

345. The NIM Guidelines are a formal part of UNDP's policies and procedures, as set out in the UNDP Programme and Operations Handbook (POH) and the UNDP Financial Rules and Regulations. All project documents prepared by UNDP and are fully compliant with UNDP’s procurement and financial management rules and regulations.

346. As the National Executing Entity (Implementing Partner), MARD is in charge of implementing the project in compliance with the NIM Guidelines, UNDP policies and procedures as set out in the UNDP POH and UNDP Financial Rules and Regulations. These include relevant requirements on fiduciary, procurement, environmental and social safeguards, and other performance standards. The project will be implemented under the terms and conditions of the Agreement signed by UNDP and GCF in 1978, together with a UNDP project document before implementation Prior to signature of the project documents.

347. As delegated by MARD to coordinate the project, CPO needs to have undergone a Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfer (HACT) assessment by UNDP as well as GCF requirements. During implementation, UNDP will provide oversight and monitoring of the project through regular visits, site visits, and other means. The HACT assessment is based on the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) already signed between UNDP and GCF, and project confirmation to be granted by GCF. CPO will also need to ensure that all partners are in compliance with their roles and responsibilities as outlined in the project document and participate in Project Steering Committee meetings, quarterly progress and annual implementation reviews, and audits of financial statements.

348. The Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfer (HACT) framework consists of four processes: 1) macro assessment; 2) micro-assessment; 3) monitoring and evaluation; and 4) audit. Macro-assessment is conducted by UNDP auditors. Micro-Assessment activities include planning, period on-site reviews (spot checks), programmatic monitoring, scheduled audits and special audits. During micro-assessment, there can be weaknesses identified for which actors are required to take corrective action. The macro-assessment will be conducted at least once a year. Monitoring of the implementation of assurance activities will have to remain higher and modalities of engaging with that implementing partner will have to be defined in the project document.

349. The project will be audited in accordance with UNDP policies and procedures on audits, informed by and/or together with GCF requirements. UNDP’s internal audit function is responsible for conducting financial and compliance audits of the auditors. UNDP scheduled audits are performed during the programme cycle as per UNDP assuranceauditplan (AAP). The project document will specify the frequency of these audits. The AAP is used to determine whether the funds transferred to the partners were used for the appropriate purpose and in accordance with the work plan. A financial audit can consist of a financial audit or an internal control audit.

350. At central level, UNDP will conduct an annual audit (financial audit) of the project document (including department at central level) and Responsible Parties, less any agreed cost recovery amount. Under UNDP’s national implementation modality, the National Audit Office (NAO) is responsible for conducting financial audits of Implementing Partners and Responsible Parties for the implementation of agreed and approved project activities, in accordance with UNDP standard audit procedures. The NAO will also conduct an annual internal control audit of the project document based on expenditure as a financial report on quarterly basis to UNDP. Any additional requirements will be in accordance with the AAP as and when it is agreed.

351. A cost recovery plan (which will be further discussed and revised prior to UNDP Project Document signature) is annexed in Annex XIII.

# G.1 Risk Assessment Summary

## 352. Effective project implementation faces technical, operational, financial, social and environmental risks (please see section 3 for ESS assessment). Manageable risks exist in relation to design, construction and operation of irrigation infrastructure. The project will be implemented in a rural context where the majority of households live in rural areas that may be difficult to access. Effective and efficient operation of new irrigation equipment and associated water storage systems is dependent on the availability of trained personnel. Farmers who are illiterate or who experience communication difficulties in Vietnamese or suffer critical deficits in formal education, Institutional limitations such as lack of technical capacity at project implementation level, and poor coordination among project stakeholders may experience communication difficulties in Vietnamese or suffer critical deficits in formal education. Institutional limitations such as lack of technical capacity at project implementation level, and poor coordination among project stakeholders may experience communication difficulties in Vietnamese or suffer critical deficits in formal education.

## 353. Incomplete preparation by communities to integrate into the project can hamper effective participation in project undertakings as well as subsequent adoption of the technologies, practices, cropping systems, and information provided through the Farmer Field School. The project will work closely with farmers to build technical and financial capacity for operations and maintenance of irrigation systems, water storage systems and water efficient technologies, including the development of a farmer field school. This will be done in close collaboration with local government authorities.

## 354. Extreme weather events such as droughts, severe heatwaves or widespread flooding – may harm project investments, impacting project implementation and affecting production of project results. Finally, there are risks associated with financial management, access to credit and markets, etc., which could affect smaller commercial farmers who have limited resources.

## 355. This project proposes a number of measures to mitigate these risks. The project will invest in local level awareness raising activities through Farmer Field Schools and other means, as well as at the commune, district and provincial government authorities to ensure suitable design and operation of irrigation infrastructure, water efficient technologies, and associated water storage systems. The project will also provide intensive training in Farmer Field Schools to plan and manage irrigation, cultivation and commercialization of climate-resilient crops. This will be done in close collaboration with local NGOs and government extension services. The project will work closely with farmers to build technical and financial capacity for operations and maintenance of irrigation systems, water storage systems and water efficient technologies, including the development of a farmer field school.

## 356. Any risk assessment should be seen as an ongoing process that is subject to change in response to new information or changes in circumstances. The project will also work closely with farmers to build technical and financial capacity for operations and maintenance of irrigation systems, water storage systems and water efficient technologies, including the development of a farmer field school.

# G2. Risk Factors and Mitigation Measures

## Selected Risk Factor 1

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Farmers may be reluctant to adopt climate-smart agricultural practices due to perceived risk associated with application of new techniques, technologies, inputs. | Technical and operational | Medium (5-10% of project value) | Low |

### Mitigation Measure(s)

357. Participatory vulnerability assessment and prioritization methods used in Farmer Field Schools will highlight farmer-identified needs and priorities, as well as traditional knowledge of their agro-ecosystems.

## Selected Risk Factor 2

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Champion farmers could capture the benefits of partnerships with the private sector or otherwise neglect mentoring and capacity building of neighboring farmers. | Social and environmental | Medium (5-10% of project value) | Low |

### Mitigation Measure(s)

358. Champion farmers will be selected by their peers from among the most respected and successful farmers in each participating village at a pre-determined time during the project. Training and mentoring by champion farmers will be supervised/informed by PRIU and DARD staff, and commune farmers will be encouraged to provide feedback on what they are learning and doing. M&E visits by project and institutional staff will be conducted regularly with emphasis on the success of the champion farmer model. With this framework, this risk remains at Medium.

## Selected Risk Factor 3

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Poor and near-poor farmers may perceive credit as too risky, thus reducing the likelihood of their possessing it. | Financial | Medium (5-10% of project value) | Medium |

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 88 OF 102

### Mitigation Measure(s)

359. Poorer-poor farmers may be more risk averse than their more well-off counterparts given uncertainty regarding production methods, markets, weather and other factors. This project will teach farmers to identify and understand the risks they face in order to make informed decisions about how best to manage these risks, increase both productivity and climate resilience. Farmers will build their capacities in Farmer Field Schools to apply agricultural practices and cropping systems that are both productive and climate-resilient, as part of FFS champion farmer groups. These farmers will also be supported by a range of other activities including field visits, peer-receiving institutional and NGO support. Through their participation in FFS and multi-stakeholder platforms, farmers will gain access to information on market opportunities, financial services, inputs, extension, marketing of climate-resilient commodities, and other factors, together with buyers, creditors, institutions and NGOs. The project will also provide training for private sector entrepreneurs in risk management and marketing risk. The participation productivity of agri-climate advisories will deliver a high degree of confidence in the information provided to farmers on how best to manage their risks. By improving access to irrigation water accessibility through irrigation and storage, the project will reduce the risk to farmers of increasing rainfall variability and extreme events. Given this overall strategy of reducing risk, this risk is considered as Medium.

### Selected Risk Factor 4

Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring
---|---|---|---
Private sector entrepreneurs perceive risks to participating on multi-stakeholder innovation platforms in terms of potential insufficient or negative cost-benefit and cooperation is low. | Financial | Medium (5-10% of project value) | Medium

### Mitigation Measure(s)

360. Private sector entrepreneurs are expected to look favorably on participation on multi-stakeholder platforms given their economic interests tied to commodity production and commercialization processes. It is expected that the private sector will be interested in increased production of climate-resilient agricultural products for the potential market opportunities, as well as improved access to inputs, extension services, credit facilities, production and commercialization, cooperation among entrepreneurs is critical. At the same time by reducing the risks to producers and processors, the project will provide a strong incentive for private sector participation. Producers can be relatively certain of sufficient volumes and quality of produce, which will strengthen their interest in providing inputs and other services. By strengthening the role of multi-stakeholder platforms, entrepreneurs can enter into various partnerships or contractual relationships with producers and producer’s organizations. The project will invite a wide variety of private sector entities to each provision of risk mitigation measures. Given this overall strategy of reducing risk, this risk is Medium.

### Selected Risk Factor 5

Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring
---|---|---|---
WESDAM operations are poorly run resulting in insufficient water at critical times of the production cycle. | Technical and operational | High (>20% of project value) | Low

**Page 93**

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 89 OF 102

### Mitigation Measure(s)

**361. As WEIDAP will provide access to farmers of a fundamental resource vital to one of the country's most significant economic sectors, the Government of Vietnam will prioritize the fully effective operations and maintenance of WEIDAP infrastructure. The risk associated with this is considered Low due to the planned capacity development of the institutions responsible for WEIDAP operations and maintenance. Given the importance of WEIDAP to the national economy, the risk to poorer-poor farmers is negligible or poor performance in areas charged with WEIDAP infrastructure operations and maintenance. As such, this risk is considered to be Low.**

#### Selected Risk Factor 6
- **Description:** Poorer-poor farmers may assess the time needed to establish fully functional irrigation systems (3–4 years) to be too long and therefore unaffordable economically given alternative subsistence land uses with annual crops (e.g., maize, rice).
- **Risk category:** Technical and operational
- **Level of impact:** Medium (5.1–20% of project value)
- **Probability of risk occurring:** Low

### Mitigation Measure(s)

**362. In establishing plantation agroforestry systems, there is a time gap between tree seedlings to grow annual subsistence crops. Project staff, champion farmers and cooperating institutions and organizations will assist farmers in overcoming this challenge by providing technical support and training on production techniques, inputs for their production system, thereby reducing their costs and financial risk over the first two years of establishment. Given that agroforestry systems are established with a mix of tree species and annual crop varieties as well as providing input supply, it is expected that farmers will be able to establish agroforestry systems on their plots. This risk is considered to be Low.**

#### Selected Risk Factor 7
- **Description:** Ethnic minority and women farmers may feel that project activities are not culturally sensitive or responsive to their specific needs in terms of language, cultural factors and gender norms, thus affecting their participation.
- **Risk category:** Social and environmental
- **Level of impact:** Medium (5.1–20% of project value)
- **Probability of risk occurring:** Medium

### Mitigation Measure(s)

**363. Project implementing parties will receive training and awareness raising regarding ethnic minority and women farmers' rights, services and cultural practices to ensure their full and respectful participation in the project. Participation by these two populations will be closely monitored, and grievance redress mechanisms to address complaints will be established. Given existing awareness of potential conflicts and sensitivities, as well as mitigation measures to avoid them in this project, this risk is rated as Medium.**

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 80 OF 102

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Delays in co-financing fund flows under the WEDAP project resulting in slow progress that may impact the GCF project. | Financial | High (>20% of project value) | Low |

## Mitigation Measure(s)

364 Given the importance of WEDAP to the national economy, the government of Vietnam will prioritize the execution of the modernization of the project as fully effective, including operations and maintenance. Further, the legally binding ADB concessional loan agreements have been fully negotiated and signed. As such, UNDP will continue to monitor and report on co-financing realized during project implementation in compliance with GCF reporting requirements.

## Other Information

365 Over the long-term, climate change is expected to continue to evolve with increasing climate variability and extreme events as likely periodic outcomes. This project is aimed at adapting farmers' agro-ecosystems to climatic changes by promoting the adoption of appropriate technologies and practices for sustainable adaptive measures. By empowering poorer-poor farms with the required knowledge, information and methodologies, they will be better able to adapt their farming systems.

366 Markets for agroforestry commodities are reasonably stable, but given the time required for establishment and initiation of production, rapid response to sudden changes in the market for a specific commodity is difficult. Market risk can therefore be mitigated by providing financial support to project implementing parties, as well as others, to avoid market risk and take advantage of economic opportunities.

# RESULTS MONITORING AND REPORTING

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 87 OF 102

### 3.5 Enhance supplementary irrigation systems (not included in Activity 3.4) and water harvesting to enhance climate variability and drought

This activity will augment water storage capacities in rain-fed communities in the eight target regions by implementing a package of interventions, including construction of household or community constructed ponds, installation of small-scale water harvesting systems, and establishment of DWM protocols and funding mechanisms.

#### 3.5.1 Construct and upgrade 1200 climate-resilient ponds

- Upgrade existing ponds
- Construct 484 new ponds
- Install 163 pond-management groups for O&M

#### 3.5.2 Establish 975 new pond management groups for O&M, including agreements on potential funding mechanisms

### 3.6 Increase water harvesting capacities to apply climate-resilient and efficient practices that maximize water availability in mountainous water systems using coping strategies with drought

This activity will enhance water availability for smallholder farmers through the adoption of adaptive practices and technologies that maximize water use efficiency and reduce evaporation, support poor rural households at the community level by adopting climate-smart agricultural techniques and technologies. This will include the installation of rainwater harvesting systems, climate-resilient low-cost technologies such as drip irrigation, micro-irrigation, and other efficient water-use technologies in mountainous areas, as well as their operation and maintenance.

The project will train government extension workers and community-based organizations to support farmers in the design, operations, and maintenance of rainwater harvesting systems. Farmers and trained farmer schools will be supported with training on climate-resilient low-cost technologies for water harvesting and water management to enhance soil moisture retention, reduce evaporation, and improve water availability.

#### Activity 2.1: Train 30 DAWM staff and district-level coordinators in rainwater harvesting systems

- Conduct 900 FTTs and train 21,258 poor rural households
- Install 400 small-scale water harvesting systems for KALI pool/heap floor smallholders

#### Activity 2.2: Train 30 DAWM staff on water efficiency systems for KALI pool/heap floor smallholders

- Conduct performance-based training for 150 farmers and other interested stakeholders
- Install 600 small-scale water harvesting systems for KALI pool/heap floor smallholders

#### Activity 2.4: Train another farmer school in rainwater harvesting systems for KALI pool/heap floor smallholders

### 3.7 Investment in inputs and services to scale up climate-resilient agricultural practices and policies (lead management)

This activity will focus on training farmers with the skills and capacities, as well as continued support through the provision of improved choices that optimize the increase in productivity and income for smallholder farmers.

Through Farmer Field School, farmer groups, and other community-based organizations, target regions will learn simple methodologies to enhance their climate-resilient agricultural practices.

### 2.3 Co-development and use of livelihoods-oriented climate-resilient agro-climate advisory services to enhance smallholder farmers' access to modern agricultural production
This activity will develop and convey agro-climatic information through a local technical advisor linked to local conditions, with the aim of enhancing farmer access to modern agricultural production. This will be achieved by:
- Providing training on climate-resilient agricultural practices that are adapted to the specific climatic conditions of their farms.
- Providing information about the best times for planting and harvesting crops.

The project will support farmers and GIZ staff in developing a better understanding of traditional knowledge and interpreting down-scaled climate data, including climate and weather data. These will be disseminated through conventional and digital channels.

### 2.3.1 Train 50 technicians and interpreters on agro-climatic information and interpreting down-scaled climate data for the development of a training plan (being taken over by GIZ after the first year).
- 50 technicians and 240 interpreters will be trained in the use of climate advisories to 139,436 households.

### 2.3 Co-development through the provision of Climate Flowing (CF) personal and group-based advisory services with smallholder farmers and local technical advisors
- Provide technical advisory services for 50 participants over four years.
- Provide training for 30 PAs and 56 interpreters per year (150 participants per four years).

#### Project-level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken in compliance with the UNDP POPP and the UNDP Evaluation Policy. The project will establish a system of continuous monitoring and reporting results via the Project Manager.
The Project Manager will develop annual work plans to ensure the efficient implementation of the project activities. The Project Manager is responsible for ensuring that all project activities are implemented during implementation, including the development of the Monitoring & Evaluation (M&E) plan, so that the appropriate data can be collected in a timely manner and analyzed on an ongoing basis.
The UNDP Country Office will support the Project Manager as needed, including through annual supervision missions. The UNDP Country Office is responsible for complying with UNDP project-level M&E requirements as stipulated by the POPP.
