# Secretariat’s assessment of FP125

## Proposal name: Strengthening the resilience of smallholder agriculture to climate change-induced water insecurity in the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast regions of Vietnam

### Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

### Country/ies: Viet Nam

### Project/programme size: Medium

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The project supports the poorest farmers in vulnerable regions in implementing climate-resilient agricultural practices. |  |
| GCF funding will increase impacts and results beyond those achieved by the Asian Development Bank funding by connecting vulnerable poor and near-poor farmers to main trade routes. |  |
| The project contains a solid operations and maintenance plan for water supply systems and infrastructures with financial commitment from the district government and beneficiary communities. |  |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions set out in the annex hereof and addition of third “List of proposed conditions and recommendations”.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

1. Viet Nam's central highlands and south-central coast are two of the most climate-vulnerable regions of the country in terms of hydro-meteorological hazards. The regions have been experiencing more frequent and severe droughts, leading to changes in precipitation patterns.
2. Climate change-induced water insecurity is affecting smallholder agriculture in the region, which is a major livelihood for many people. Smallholder farmers are dependent on agriculture and water resources for their livelihoods. They also constitute Viet Nam's largest percentage of poor households. The majority of the population in these regions depends on crops grown for market, rice, maize, and cassava for local consumption, especially by the poorest. Both are important for food security and nutrition.


---
**Page 112**
---

1. The project aims to empower vulnerable smallholders in the two regions to manage increasing climate risks to agricultural production by securing water provision, supporting farmers to adopt climate-resilient agriculture, and strengthening access to agro-climate information services.
2. The project will directly benefit around 222,412 people in five provinces through last-mile connections to the irrigation mainline and water storage to boost productivity on rain-fed lands. They will also benefit from increased access to location-specific agro-climate information for agricultural planning and decision-making, as well as improved market access and greater access to markets. An additional 335,252 people will indirectly benefit through climate information services.
3. The project is structured into two outputs:
   (a) Enhanced water security for agricultural production for vulnerable smallholder farmers in the face of climate-induced rainfall variability and droughts; and
   (b) Increased resilience of smallholder farmer livelihoods through climate-resilient agriculture practices and improved access to markets.
4. The project requests USD 302.7 million in GCF grants, which is 19 per cent of the total project cost. USD 99.6 million in co-financing of senior loans will be provided by the Asian Development Bank (ADB) on behalf of the Government of Japan and the Government of Viet Nam, including from the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development (MARD) and the Department of Agriculture and Rural Development (DAR). The total project cost is USD 156.3 million.
5. **2.2 Component-by-component analysis**
   * Output 1: Enhanced water security for agricultural production for vulnerable smallholder farmers in the face of climate-induced rainfall variability and droughts (total cost: USD 138.1 million; GCF cost: USD 15.6 million or 11.3 per cent)
     - Objective 1 will address the high uncertainty of smallholder farmers through establishing large-scale irrigation infrastructure and last-mile connections to poor and near-poor farmer lands. This will be achieved by constructing water storage facilities, such as dams, weirs, ponds, and dugouts, and increase on-farm productivity with water-efficient technologies, including sprinklers and drip irrigation.
     - The proposal delineates that GCF financing will cover the incremental cost of reaching additional smallholder farmers to link their plots to the main irrigation systems. It also demonstrates that the GCF funding will enlarge the project impacts and beneficiaries by complementing existing infrastructure in the region. A total of 1,025 ha of land for rice production will be irrigated under this project, which is expected to bring significant benefits to farmers and society at large.
     - The proponent presented a detailed hydrological study which assesses the water balance for each site project. The irrigation system will be sourced by existing dams and reservoirs, as well as new ones constructed in the project area. This will result in positive balances of water with results ranging from 85–90 per cent reliability within the projected scenarios under a changing climate. The study also includes proposed crop varieties under irrigation such as rice, maize, soybean, sugarcane, banana, custard apple, cashew, vegetables, avocados, durian and others. These are assessed to be appropriate in the context of droughts and rainfall variability resulting in wetter seasons.


---
**Page 113**
---

10. The first strategy is clearly analyzed and delineated in the proposal. It provides an estimated cost for operations and maintenance (O&M) for 10 years after project completion. Sustainability is ensured through a solid O&M plan, including the commitment from district authorities to provide necessary resources. The irrigation system will be regularly reviewed and monitored to ensure that the large-scale irrigation system is adequately maintained. It also implies that farmers will receive the necessary trainings for the farmers to properly operate and maintain the irrigation systems and equipment.

**Output 2: Increased resilience of smallholder farmers livelihoods through climate-resilient agriculture and access to climate information, finance and markets (total cost: USD 14.6 million; GCF cost share: 80 percent)**

11. With output 1 ensuring reliable water resources to smallholder farmers, output 2 focuses on delivering climate-resilient agriculture to the same beneficiaries. It will be implemented through a voucher system that provides financial incentives for smallholder farmers to attain 900 farmer field schools (FFS) and adopt climate-resilient agriculture. At the same time, it will provide access to credit for all participating farmers in each province to link the farmers to market and provide access to credit by bringing together all actors in the agricultural value chain. The voucher system is supported with localized agro-climate advisories to enhance their agricultural production.

12. A proposed performance-based voucher system with climate-resilient agriculture packages is recommended for this project. Smallholder farmers who participate in the FFS, upon successful completion of the trainings, will be provided with vouchers to purchase pre-determined technologies or inputs from pre-determined sellers. The voucher system will be implemented through a series of steps that include the operational steps to implement the voucher system which was already designed and implemented successfully by the AE in other provinces. This voucher system has been used for 862,500 farmers, which make up 4 per cent of the total direct beneficiaries of this project. Given that the voucher system is a proven model, the Agricultural Advisory Entity (AE) should explore the design of a larger financial incentive system to ensure replication and transform the agricultural system.

13. The proposed GCF can enable dialogues and knowledge exchange among various stakeholders along the agricultural value chain. While this can be a good first step to bring in stakeholders, it is important for the project to draw up concrete action plans and strategies through which these dialogues will be translated into concrete actions that will lead to development.

14. The output contains standard practices in generating and disseminating documented agro-climatic forecasts and advisories. This has merit as means of reaching indirect beneficiaries at scale, but the AE should make sure that the methods and techniques are fully in line and harmonized with the overall climate information services at the national scale.

**Component 3: Project management (total cost: USD 2.9 million; GCF cost: USD 1.4 million or 80 per cent)**

15. The last component is the project management cost for the implementation of the project. The GCF portion of the project management cost is around 4.8 percent of the total requested GCF funding, and it is compliant with the GCF policy on fees.


---
**Page 114**
---

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium-High

- Viet Nam experienced increases in temperature by 0.62°C in 1958–2014 and changes in precipitation patterns, with a 15–20 per cent increase in the wet season and a 10 per cent decrease in the dry season.
- In the central highlands and the semi-arid central zone, inter-seasonal differences are more pronounced than seasonal ones. The impact of climate change is felt most strongly during the dry season, requiring adequate water management and storage plans.

The proposal aims to reach a sizeable proportion of the population, with 223,412 beneficiaries in the central highlands and semi-arid central zone (SACZ) through various interventions, including access to reliable water resources through irrigation and water harvesting systems. These interventions will also improve food security, nutrition, livelihoods, and opportunities to access markets through GPs. In addition, 335,252 people will indirectly benefit by receiving climate-smart agriculture and agro-climate advisories to improve the resilience of their agricultural livelihoods.

The proposal is expected to promote water-saving technologies such as sprinkler and drip irrigation and small-scale rainwater harvesting ponds that focus on reducing water losses and increasing water use efficiency. The KFBP will provide co-financing for these interventions, but it does not include locked-in and long-lived climate-vulnerable infrastructure.

The proposed targets poor and near-poor smallholder farmers, as measured by the multi-dimensional poverty index (MDPI) in the central highlands of Viet Nam. These beneficiaries are those who will be unable to cover the cost of connecting to the ADB-financed main irrigation systems or other water sources that are not climate-smart.

The project's irrigation infrastructure mainly concerns water distribution channels, overflow dams and reservoirs that will be connected to the mainline irrigation systems. A large amount of water is lost in these areas due to poor maintenance and lack of investment in their agricultural fields; pumping will therefore not be needed in most cases. In fields where pumping is required, electricity from the national grid will be utilized.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium/High

The project has a good potential for upscaling and replication to other regions of the country through the GPs. The proposal demonstrates replicable potential for scaling interventions, supported by MARD and DARD staff, farmers and other relevant stakeholders whose capacity and knowledge are expected to increase as a result of GCP-funded activities.

The proposal also presents a number of innovative approaches that could serve as a paradigm shift were they presented to implement such upscaling and replication activities. The proposal demonstrates good potential for knowledge and learning by introducing the GPs in the SACZ, which will provide an enabling environment for access to credit and market development through the GPs, but the scope and extent to which the actual market transactions are supported depend on the participation of the private sector in the agricultural value chains.


---
**Page 115**
---

28\. The proposal provides a solid post-project O&M plan with an estimated budget. Significant cost is expected to be covered by beneficiary communities. The AO should ensure that established infrastructure and assets are properly operated and maintained after project completion by enabling full government and community ownership of the assets.

### 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

25\. The proposal sounds justiﬁcation for the project’s potential to produce positive environmental impacts. The proposed climate-resilient agricultural production packages include generally well-recognized practices that have positive environmental impacts, such as intercropping and crop rotation. No harmful environmental impacts are associated with the selected technologies and practices. Environmental impacts of the project will be carefully monitored to avoid any negative environmental externalities.

26\. The proposed climate-resilient seeds will provide more reliable income for farmers, and ecosystem services provided by trees will increase. Project social beneﬁts are also assessed to be high, as diversiﬁed agricultural production with climate information and better water management practices will improve the livelihoods of project communities and farmers are well prepared in the face of long-term climate change impacts.

27\. Gender considerations have been taken into account in the project design with an aim to mainstream a women-centred approach in agricultural development. The proposal emphasizes the prioritization of women’s access to resources, services, and decision-making power. A detailed gender assessment and action plan was developed and submitted as an annex.

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

28\. The vulnerability of the country and target beneﬁciaries and their adaptation needs are explained throughout the proposal, although it could beneﬁt from more detail on the scale and intensity of climate change impacts in the region. It is acknowledged that women and ethnic minorities are affected by climate change. Small-scale farmers (up to one hectare) and high dependency households are particularly vulnerable, which is highly affected by variable variability and increasing temperatures. Approximately 40-64 per cent of people rely on agriculture and water resources for their livelihoods.

29\. Using ﬁnancial estimates, the proposal describes the needs of the recipient with regard to implementing the project. The estimated cost of US$135 million is signiﬁcantly higher than shortfalls in the national budget to cover its estimated cost. Although Viet Nam is not a least developed country (LDC), it is still classiﬁed as a developing country and therefore, funding and strategies are commended. The target beneﬁciaries are classiﬁed by the ofﬁcial government deﬁnition as poor or near-poor, and their ﬁnancial capacity is deemed to be too weak to access credit.

### 3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

30\. The proposal clearly shows that the project would contribute to Viet Nam’s nationally determined contribution under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change and the country’s various national development and climate change plans and strategies. It could have a signiﬁcant impact in terms of reducing greenhouse gas emissions, as well as extent the project would contribute to Viet Nam’s nationally-determined contribution.

3. As the executing entity (EE), MARD is the main coordinating body in the country for agriculture and rural development. Its annual budget in 2018 was USD 900 million and it worked with the AE on many projects. It has a proven track record in managing similarized interventions.

4. The project was designed based on rigorous consultations with relevant stakeholders, including the GCF national designated authority. A stakeholder engagement plan was submitted as an annex to the funding proposal. The proposal contributes to the MARD restructuring strategy by providing a new approach for climate change adaptation that can enhance responses and combine them with agro-forestry and crop diversification methods.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: High

5. The proposed financial structure is justified in a reasonable manner, considering the income status and vulnerability of target beneficiaries. The GCF grants will target smallholder farmers and other vulnerable groups such as women, indigenous minorities, who are unable to pay for connections to their main WEDMAP infrastructures.

6. The proposal demonstrates high cost-effectiveness with an economic internal rate of return of 10% over the project lifetime and a net present value (NPV) of USD 25 million over the 25-year project lifetime. The calculation was based on avoided damages from droughts, floods, and other climate-related events generated through climate-resilient agriculture and the FFS.

7. Financial viability is demonstrated through the financial analysis, which shows a net present value (NPV) of USD 10 million over the 25-year project lifetime at an interest rate of 8.9 per cent. In spite of the result, the proposal justifies the need for high concessionality based on the budget constraints of the Government of Viet Nam to finance climate change adaptation.

### 4.2 Gender policy

The proposed country gender assessment and a project-level gender action plan, and therefore meets the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy and Gender Action Plan.

#### 4.2.1 Gender assessment conducted presents the national legal and policy framework for addressing gender equality and discrimination in Vietnam, including decrees that have been issued to address gender equality and discrimination in various sectors. The Ministry of Health (MOH), the Ministry of Labor and Social Affairs (DOLASA) and the People’s Committee (PC) are responsible for mainstreaming gender. In addition, the Vietnam Women’s Union (VWU) protects the legal rights of women and promotes gender equality. However, at the sub-national level in various places put in place there is a general lack of capacity to implement the relevant policies and laws. The implementation of the policy requires coordination between sectors such as the DOLASA, VWU and PC mandated to mainstream gender have limited capacity to fulfill their responsibilities for the promotion and protection of women’s rights and interests.

The objective of the gender assessment is to identify and implement interventions that will ensure equal access to and control over resources needed to implement climate resilient agricultural production in both women and men in the face of climate change induced rainfall variability, increased temperatures and extreme weather events. The gender assessment includes a review of the baseline survey of the WEDAP Stakeholder engagement through focus group discussions (FGDs) with key stakeholders and community members to identify issues. The discussion with communities indicated that there are similarities in concerns between men and women regarding access to resources for agricultural production, which have additional concerns and questions regarding their need for capacity development, access to micro-credit facilities, and the availability of information on climate change and early warning systems. Women, during the consultation, also indicated the need for improvements in access to economic activities, for peer to peer learning opportunities and technical assistance.

The gender assessment also identified that women are more vulnerable than men as they are more likely to be responsible for childcare and household chores while men are more likely to engage in wage labor with men due to male migration to other provinces in search of work. Women also play a primary role in decision-making for household use, and declining local availability increases the distance travelled to collect water.

In the context of agriculture, the gender assessment discusses the perceptions and stereotypes that exist regarding women’s roles within agricultural production systems and on agricultural tasks linked to the stereotypical assumptions that women are weaker and should be engaged in household work while men are seen as stronger and can engage in agriculture work. Therefore, women have limited access to land ownership and management rights at both the family and community level and typically decision making concerning the use of agricultural

### Fiduciary

UNDP will be the AE for this project. The role of the AE is to ensure compliance with donor requirements and rules pertaining to the use of programme resources. UNDP will also conduct quality assurance roles to carry out objective and independent project oversight and monitoring functions. UNDP will perform monitoring and reporting throughout the reporting period in accordance with the terms of reference, as well as the fiduciary agreement.

The MARD is the RE (GCF terminology)/Implementing Partner (UNDP terminology). The RE is responsible for implementing the project in compliance with UNDP rules and regulations, policies and procedures (including "National Implementation by the Government of UNDP Supported Projects" guidelines). The RE will be responsible for ensuring that the project is implemented effectively, including the monitoring and evaluation of project interventions, achieving project outcomes, and effectively using UNDP resources. These include fulfilling relevant requirements on fiduciary management, financial reporting, procurement, and adherence to international standards.

Funding will flow from the EE to the responsibilities parties (RRPs), which are directly accountable to MARD. RPs will led the implementation of the FFS for each province and the implementation of the respective subprojects in a subdistrict.

The RRPs for the RE will perform underperformance risk assessments, a micro-assessment of financial management and procurement capacity to fulfil requirements under the Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfers Framework and GCF requirements, as well

### Legal assessment

The AMA was signed with the AE on 5 August 2016 and became effective on 23 November 2016.

The AMA has provided a certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

The proposed project will be implemented in Vietnam, a country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF is not protected from criminal prosecution or civil litigation. The issue of whether GCF can be further assessed. The Secretariat submitted a revised draft of the privileges and immunities agreement to the Government of Viet Nam on 21 December 2016. However, no response has been received yet.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and Independent Integrity Unit have both expressed concerns that GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities for investigations, as appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by GCF are made only after GCF has been provided with the necessary privileges and immunities or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to:

(a) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and

(b) Completion of legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP125

## Proposal name: Strengthening the resilience of smallholder agriculture to climate change-induced water insecurity for Central Highlands and South Coast regions of Vietnam

### Accredited entity: United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

Project/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: High

The funding proposal considers a large-scale irrigation project, Water Efficiency Improvement in Poorly Affected Provinces (WEIAPP), financed through a loan from the Asian Development Bank (ADB) and resources from the Government of Viet Nam, and a complementary project to improve access to water for smallholder farmers. The irrigation system connections for poor/nearly poor smallholders, with a particular focus on ethnic minority and women farmers. Both irrigation schemes will be developed in the provinces of Dak Lak, Lam Dong, Kon Tum, Gia Lai, and Binh Dinh in the Central Highlands and South Coast Central regions of Viet Nam.

The project will:

1. **Enhance water resources for agricultural production** for vulnerable smallholder farmers in the face of climate change induced rainfall variability and droughts.
2. **Increase livelihoods of smallholders** by providing irrigation water to 7,000 ha and improving access to climate information, finance, and markets.

The project will also establish large-scale irrigation infrastructure (main pipeline systems connected to current reservoirs, upgraded canals and new weirs) to provide improved pumping power(s) to bring irrigation water to eight farming areas across the target regions. The WEIAPP project will also ensure the adoption of on-farm water management practices focused on improving efficiency.

4. Using GCF resources the project will establish last-mile connections (pipes, water shifting valves, small water storage structures and others) between the WEIAPP irrigation infrastructure and individual farmers' plots. This will be achieved through close collaboration with labour in co-designing, installing and maintaining their connecting systems. This support will be provided by a dedicated team of technicians and engineers who will also provide training on courses on climate-resilient farming (including water efficiency practices) conducted in the community.

5. The proposed project will also enhance supplementary irrigation for rain-fed smallholder farmers to cope with rainfall variability and drought through the construction and enhancement of existing supplementary water management systems, resulting in 13,159 climate-


---
**Page 125**
---

realized that will enable farmers to better maintain minimum irrigation water supply during climate change induced droughts. The project will enhance the productivity of water-strengthening smallholder capacities to apply on-farm water-efficient practices and technologies, and improve access to information and services for adaptation and drought.

6. To deliver the second output to the project will invest GCF grant resources with co-financing from national and provincial governments, in inputs and capacities to scale-up climate change adaptation strategies and technologies that are being piloted by smallholders through FFS and will ensure the establishment of market and credit linkages through the development of a value chain for water-efficient practices and technologies. Linked to the development of the climate innovation platforms, the project will assemble a technical advisory team with expertise in climate change services to provide tailored advisories for different cropping systems within the sub-provinces.

7. The project will benefit over 139,416 households as direct and indirect beneficiaries in the districts of Chitral, Buner, Abbottabad, Haripur, and Swabi, which together cover all five provinces of Central Vista Nam, or 10.1 per cent of the total population of the five provinces. From this number, 83,562 households will be directly benefited through improved water management and agricultural practices to cope with rainfall variability and drought; and (ii) all the direct and indirect beneficiaries, including 83,562 households in communities around the targeted municipalities, will benefit from the project’s efforts to provide climate information, market and credit advisories and services and widespread dissemination of knowledge on water-efficient practices and technologies. The project will ensure that very poor farmer households have obtained direct access to water to increase agricultural production, reducing their vulnerability to droughts and other adverse climatic conditions.

8. Through FFS and training the project will improve the capacity of 3,600 agricultural extension workers and personnel from the farmers’ and women’s unions to provide technical advice on climate change adaptation through better agricultural practices and resilient solutions in the selected municipalities.

9. Since the rationale of the project is to improve access to water for vulnerable smallholder households in the five provinces of Central Vista Nam in the face of climate changed induced rainfall variability and droughts, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) requested additional justifications from the proponents on several major technical rationales:

10. In response, the proponents claimed that the dry season rainfall is decreasing in Vizianagaram while the average annual rainfall has been increasing and that the trend will continue in the short, medium and long term. The long-term observational data sets are analyzed to identify the trends of the temporal and spatial patterns for major crops such as maize, rice, cassava, coffee, pepper and avocado.

11. It is found that “effective rainfall” (the rainfall used for analyzing the agriculturality relevance of rainfall) has been decreasing while the agriculturality of rainfall and the dependence on available rainfall is very high. Long-term data sets also show that effective rainfall in Vizianagaram is decreasing during the pre-monsoon season and post-monsoon seasons for the above mentioned major crops, which might have aggravated physiological stress and yield losses. Therefore, adjustments need to be made for irrigation in order to compensate for increasing droughts.

12. However, the extent of drought in relation to the change in effective rainfall over the season is not clear as there are no long-term data sets available on the major crops in different seasons in the two target areas. Even without such data, the independent TAP recommends that it would be useful to conduct a studies monitor to assess rising yield stress during critical growth periods for the major crops. The crop calendar and month-wide

# 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

## 1.2.1 Innovation

The project is presented as an adaptation solution to smallholder farmers focusing on ensuring access to enough water for agricultural production. Farmers in the WEIDAP project areas are required to invest their own resources to obtain last-mile connections from the surface canal or pipe irrigation systems to their plots as well as in storage systems. Since this presents a significant financial burden, especially for poor households, the project is focusing on addressing these challenges under a “climate lens.”

### 17. Large irrigation systems do not normally include small vulnerable farmers and ethnic minorities who are often landless or have very small plots of land. Smallholder farmers (with less than one hectare of land) and indigenous ethnic minorities who experienced droughts in the past will need to invest their own resources to obtain last-mile connections from surface canals or pipe irrigation systems to their plots as well as storage systems. Farmers will need to provide a kind contribution of labour and the project will provide guidance, manuals and mentoring.

### 18. Through FFS, farmers will be trained in resilience-enhancing crop diversification, shifting farmers away from traditional crops such as rice and coffee towards a more diversified mix of high-value crops that are better adapted to climate change. This will enhance soil fertility and organic matter, improving soil structure and limiting soil erosion.

### 19. For poor and particularly poor farmers, especially ethnic minorities and women, who have a risk perception with respect to borrowing money to implement CCA practices, a conditional


---
**Page 127**
---

performance-based voucher system will be integrated within the FFS to motivate the application of CRA packages. Vouchers will be given when the courses have been effectively taken by relevant farmers and will allow them to redeem the crops/seeds and specific agricultural inputs from local traders, cooperatives, or other private sector suppliers. This voucher system is a good incentive for farmers willing to shift to more effective CRA systems.

The project is also devoting some resources to link farmers to markets and to financial schemes, establishing climate innovation platforms, bringing together all actors of a particular value chain in one place, and creating an independent TAP (Technical Advisory Panel). The independent TAP believes that in order to deliver real transformative changes to ensure efficient implementation of the project, it is necessary to have a more flexible approach with shorter rates, larger payment periods and guarantees with relevant micro-crediting institutions. Moreover, there will be an agreement with agribusinesses already benefiting from the WEIP/PMMP project to support them in their climate adaptation efforts.

23. In terms of agricultural information systems, the proposal presents several related programmes that are part of a larger project approved by the Board in 2016, namely "Improving the resilience of vulnerable coastal communities to climate change-related impacts in Viet Nam", which includes a component to enhance the capacity of local authorities and farmers to use ICT for climate-smart agriculture applications in 28 coastal provinces of Viet Nam, including the south-central coastal region. The proposal also presents a separate programme that will support the development of different climate information system projects in the country, or a clear description of the proposed activities and their linkages so as to ensure coherence with other parts of this activity in the overall proposal.

24. The theory of change explains the purpose of the project and addresses the core issues involving irrigation infrastructure. It will still be only marginal, as rich or not-so-poor farmers have already been irrigating their lands in the dry season in the target regions. The creation of a new irrigation system is therefore unlikely to solve this problem. What is perhaps new is that it involves CRA and institutional approaches along with irrigation to offer a holistic safeguard to current agricultural practices.

12.2 Potential for knowledge and learning

As per the project document, the last-mile connection to large-scale irrigation infrastructure in Viet Nam has been achieved by connecting one million hectares in other areas in Viet Nam where similar conditions exist. However, replication will depend on the willingness of the Vietnamese government to invest in the development of small-scale irrigation infrastructure and the willingness to connect vulnerable communities, rather than large-scale agricultural projects that are often associated with high costs and limited benefits.

26. The knowledge and institutional capacity to support small farmers could be the foundation for delivering knowledge-sharing schemes to relevant provinces by the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development (MARD) in order to ensure better dissemination of agricultural practices to vulnerable communities at large.

The project will construct water storage facilities, which is a common practice in Viet Nam or other countries. The project proposes to build 102 new water storage facilities with a potential for an additional 6,683 household and communal ponds in other areas of Viet Nam. This requires the construction of 54 km of irrigation canals.

The project will train 150 farmers at the district and provincial level under the provincial departments of agriculture and rural development (DARs) and farmers' union trainers. These facilitators will train 1,800 lead farmers in the five target provinces. Two lead


---
**Page 128**
---

farmers to, in turn, train approximately 25 farmers in 900 FFS. This methodology has an expected replication potential to reach an additional 194,317 poor and nearly poor households in the five project provinces; and an additional 2,845,325 poor and nearly poor households nationwide. The proposed scale-up of the FFS methodology will be evaluated by MARD and mainstreamed for future use throughout Viet Nam.

According to the proposal, the agroclimate services could be shared with 32 districts (in addition to the five project provinces). This will be done through a combination of digital and print media as well as through mobile text messaging, reaching a total of 46 districts; this will allow for the dissemination of agroclimate information in all 58 districts in the five target provinces in central Viet Nam. However, these services will need to be better defined to ensure effective uptake by the end users to create climate-resilient landscapes.

### Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

As an export-oriented country, Viet Nam has supported significant efforts and large-scale agricultural development projects with limited involvement of smallholder farmers. The project could create an enabling environment to enhance the capacity of MARD and DRRM to ensure climate-resilient growth and sustainable livelihoods for smallholder farmers.

Maintaining water systems and information services will require a strong commitment by the national government, the provincial governments and the farmers. Raising agricultural and water-related awareness among the farmers is essential in order to make them more aware of their rights and obligations as well as the conditions that ensure this project could be scaled with national resources and maintained for future generations. The project will also need the conditions to ensure that this project could be scaled with national resources and maintained for future generations.

Viet Nam has seen some of its water irrigation infrastructure deteriorate in the past. To ensure an effective use of the irrigation investments, farmers will need to prove that through efficient management they can generate enough income from their crops to pay for the maintenance of water systems in the near future. This will have a strong connection to the farmer’s ability to access credit and other financial services.

The project gives different examples of climate information projects delivered with the support of the international community, including a GCF project that will need to be better integrated into this project.

### Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

The project will contribute to the agricultural restructuring plan (ARP), which aims to drive a market-led and consumer-driven agriculture sector where the role of the Government shifts from producer to facilitator. The ARP also includes a reference to the linkage with the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment (MoNRE), which aims at improving the management and development of water resources, as well as the prevention and mitigation of adverse impacts by water use in order to make effective linkages to conservation/vegetation programmes directly ensuring environmental sustainability. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the balance between agricultural production and conservation/regeneration efforts; the project could end up providing a more balanced approach to land management.

The project will contribute to fulfilling the Paris Agreement targets, especially in supporting vulnerable communities to adapt to climate change and to ensure food security and sustainable livelihoods. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the Law of Gender Equality, promoting broader participation and a benefit-sharing mechanism in the project.

10. However, as stated in the country ownership section, most of the policies in the country have been designed up to 2020. Viet Nam needs to have new policies with a vision of change and mainstream climate change as a short-, medium- and long-term priority for the country.

### 1.2.5 Scalability and replicability

Scalability and replicability will largely depend on the willingness of the Vietnamese Government to adopt the innovative financing mechanisms proposed but also in supporting smallholders in adapting to climate change with in-country resources. Lessons learned could be scaled and mainstreamed into MARD’s existing policy, agricultural practices for vulnerable communities at the provincial and district level. The effectiveness of this methodology will be evaluated, and lessons learned codified for analysis and for mainstreaming best practices into MARD’s policies.

10. The potential to maintain the climate innovation platforms will depend on the willingness of DARDs to convene relevant stakeholders, including private sector actors, after project completion. This will also depend on the sustainability of the financial resources of the project, including profitability and sustainability, and the willingness of the diverse actors to maintain equitable and effective production systems and value chains.

### 1.3 Sustainable development potential

#### Scale: Medium

#### Environmental co-benefits

10. During the last decades, Viet Nam has increased its revenues from the agriculture sector to the detriment of environmental management. More output results from more inputs at increasing environmental costs.

10. In response to the insistence of the independent TAP regarding whether the project will accelerate or not with the commercialization foreseen in the proposal, the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) stated that the risk is considered very low enough to be ignored. The UNDP also states that the project will not be successful if the forest owner is required to sign an agreement with local authorities to maintain/protect plant species as a condition for receiving compensation payments when there is a payment for ecosystem services schemes. However, the project makes no reference to these clauses or to the need to ensure the protection of water basins and the means to do so.

10. The project will contribute to the conservation of biodiversity in Viet Nam through ecosystem services for effective and sustainable agricultural systems. Moreover, the project does not provide any information on good agricultural practices, including the use of pesticides and fertilizers to avoid further environmental degradation.

10. The proponents explained that a grievance redress mechanism will be set up by the project (C) to address complaints from local communities regarding land resources and other related issues brought forward by project beneficiaries and community members. However, this mechanism is not described in the proposal and the role of MoRE is not evident in the management structure.

10. Furthermore, the project addresses the challenges of the smallholders with GCF resources. The project will contribute to the conservation of biodiversity through a balance between production and conservation efforts. Since the two projects are presented as connected efforts, there is an urgent need to understand the regeneration and conservation challenge of the overall ecosystem in order to ensure that the benefits from the project are maximized while water is produced.

# Economic co-benefits

* The benefit of ensuring water provision will in turn improve the sustainability of the agriculture sector by diversifying crops, intensifying productivity and reducing risk of crop failure while providing benefits to the most vulnerable communities.
* Improved and diversified agricultural production systems will ensure food security and will provide farmers with more income opportunities. This will help them achieve a better quality of life for the communities. There are also new possibilities to create on-farm employment opportunities, which is particularly important in rural areas.

However, if Viet Nam is willing to take loans to deliver irrigation systems for large-scale farmers in order to continue to compete in the world market, the approach to involve smallholder farmers will need to be different from that of the large-scale farmers. The approach to investments, possibly through a differentiated water tariff system that allows cross subsidies between users, would be more fair for farmers. Economic and financial incentives should gradually bridge inequalities and inefficiencies.

## Social co-benefits

* The project is designed to enable the participation of poor/nearly poor farmers to identify adaptive strategies and manage climate risks with the assistance of water for irrigation.
* The FVS approach will provide a platform for smallholder farmers, especially vulnerable households who will in turn provide information and technical skills to ensure better farming techniques that will result in increased productivity, labour and monetary income to improve the livelihoods of communities.

* The products or products that are tailored to specific needs, including using minimal water less farm inputs, will be promoted. This will equally empower communities to participate in the project, linking them with possible markets and stronger agricultural organizations that can help them in their own value chains and efforts.

## Gender-sensitive development

* The project presents an assessment of the current situation of women in Viet Nam and the major policies to ensure the elimination of all forms of discrimination against women in line with the UN Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women. These include the National Strategy on Gender Equality for 2013-2020 and the National Program on Gender Equality for 2016–2020, which aims to transfer public awareness and knowledge about gender equality in Viet Nam.
* The project also assesses the current capacity of institutions and sectors to mainstream climate gender priorities.

Women are more likely than men to be involved in small farms since men increasingly migrate to urban areas in search of wage labour. During water stress periods women spend more time and energy sourcing water for domestic and irrigation purposes, which can lead to a lack of time for other activities such as education. Women have represented in community decision-making levels and have less access to relevant extension, market information and credit services. This is particularly true for women from ethnic minorities and other marginalized groups in terms of education and literacy.

* The project presents an action plan (to) [i] ensure women’s access to training, extension, marketing and credit services; [ii] provide women with the necessary skills to meet their needs; and [iii] ensure equal ownership between men and women and management of shared water resources.

As women become more empowered, they could use agricultural profits to advocate for their community's needs and rights.
### Project Objectives

Overall, the sustainable development potential is rated medium because it presents weaknesses in terms of environmental considerations and social equity linkages.

## Needs of the recipient Scale: Medium-High

### Vulnerability of the country and vulnerable groups

According to the funding proposal, over the past 25 years, Viet Nam's agriculture sector has made immense progress. Steady advances in smallholder rice productivity and intensification have led to a significant increase in food production, poverty reduction, national food security and social advancement. Viet Nam ranks among the top five global exporters in products as diverse as rubber, coffee, cashews, rice and pepper.

However, Viet Nam is highly vulnerable to climate change due to its location and water productivity and has seen its agricultural productivity decline in recent years. There is a need to improve the resilience of agriculture against climate change impacts. Furthermore, increased agricultural productivity has been achieved at the detriment of the environment.
- Agricultural productivity has further declined owing to the consequences of climate change on soil fertility and water availability. The country's vulnerability to climate change is strongly affected by the Asian Monsoon and overlapping modes of climate variability, including the Modoki El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) and the Indian Ocean Dipole (IOD), which together make it difficult to observe clear trends in precipitation, aridity and drought.
- Viet Nam is already impacted by more irregular and intense climate variability and change and more extreme weather events. The country's agricultural sector has limited capacity to adapt; the country is consistently classified as under "very high risk" or "extreme risk".

### Central Highlands region

The Central Highlands region has suffered from heavy rainfall-induced floods, storms, landslides and droughts. Droughts are becoming more severe and are impacting a larger area than before. The frequency of extreme weather events has increased significantly over the last 30 years compared to previous decades.
- For the entire Central Coast, the most frequent disaster events have been floods, drought, heatwaves and strong winds. Annual temperature has slightly increased and annual rainfall has increased significantly. However, in future projected scenarios, droughts in general will occur less often as rainfall will increase during the dry season; but droughts will become more severe.

The World Risk Index ranks Viet Nam as 10th most at risk of the world under the category of "very high risk", well ahead of other countries such as Bangladesh (25), Pakistan (34) and India (68). The World Risk Index is a joint initiative by the United Nations Environment Programme, the United Nations Development Programme, and the United Nations Human Security. Available at www.WorldRiskReport.org. The Climate Change Vulnerability Index ranks Viet Nam 10th most vulnerable in the world under the category of "very high risk". Available at e-magpi.com/en/about/news/239.html.

Figure: World Risk Index (UNEP, UNDP, UNEP). Nguyen TP. 2014. Mapping Droughts over the Central Highland of Vietnam in 65 Bike Years Using LandSat Imagery.

## Absence of alternative sources of financing

According to the World Bank overview, Viet Nam’s development over the past 30 years has been remarkable. Economic and political reforms have spurred rapid economic growth, transforming Vietnam from a low-income country into an upper-middle-income country between 2002 and 2018, more than 45 million people were lifted out of poverty. Poverty rate fell to 9% in 2017 compared with 36% in 1993. Gross domestic product (GDP) per capita increased by 2.5 times, standing at over US$2,500 in 2018.

The overview predicts that in the medium term, Viet Nam’s economic outlook is positive and the country will continue to be one of the fastest-growing economies in Asia, remaining robust at around 6.5–7% per cent in 2020 and 2021 and the external balance remains under control and should continue to be financed by strong foreign direct investment inflows, which reached


---
**Page 133**
---

almost USD 13 billion in 2018 – accounting for almost 24 per cent of total investment in the economy.

### Viet Nam's current population of 97 million is expected to increase to 120 million before moderating around 2058. Feeding a growing population and maintaining agricultural exports without degrading the environment and natural assets, and thus adding to the climate threats, will be a challenge.
- **Viet Nam’s irrigation expansion has been driven by rapid agricultural growth**, which has absorbed the majority of the sector's budget. Between 2009 and 2012 public expenditure on irrigation increased from 65 to 70 per cent of total agricultural expenditures. This is expected to continue, with a further increase in spending planned for 2013–2014. This increase is a concern if it indicates the crowding out of public resources for other priority areas such as climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction (DRR) or the expansion of irrigation under ARP. It is recommended that some public spending be reallocated away from irrigation to other areas.
- The focus on diversification and improve water productivity is sorely needed as water productivity is low compared with other countries* and crop diversity will support climate change adaptation by reducing vulnerability to pests, diseases, and extreme weather events.
- Viet Nam is taking a loan from ADB and matching resources to develop the WEIDAP irrigation system. According to the project proposal, between 2012 and 2016, Viet Nam’s debt burden has increased significantly due to the large number of infrastructure projects that have been imposed stricter loan limits, approving loans only for large infrastructure projects.
- However, it is not clear to the independent TAP*why the Government of Viet Nam is not able to provide a more detailed explanation on how the project will contribute to the needs for the vulnerable community around the WEIDAP project as part of the overall loan, as this will represent only a small percentage of the project investment.

## 1.5 Country ownership

### Scale: High

#### Alignment with national climate strategy

The National Climate Change Strategy for 2021–2030 recognises Viet Nam as one of the countries most vulnerable to climate change and outlines a large number of priority actions, including upgrading hydrometeorological forecasting and early warning systems, modernising farming practices, improving water management, increasing agricultural productivity, developing climate-resilient crops with sustainable use and management of water resources and increasing forest cover. In 2018, MARD issued an updated action plan on addressing climate change in the agriculture sector. The plan identifies four main areas for action under three broad major sections: strengthening the policy and institutional framework and capacities; adaptation and mitigation actions to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and increase the mitigation of other sectors within the agriculture sector.

Also in 2018, the Government of Viet Nam approved a specific implementation plan to make progress towards commitments towards the goals of the Paris Agreement, acknowledging adaptation challenges such as inefficient institutions with weak capacity at the lowest level where most climate change impacts are felt. The plan includes actions on financing and early


---
**Page 134**
---

warning terms in terms of timeliness and reliability: a focus on disaster response rather than prevention; too much investment in structural measures with limited attention to non-structural measures; and an underdeveloped risk insurance market.

* Viet Nam has also developed (i) A National Strategy on Natural Disaster Prevention, Response and Mitigation for the period 2016–2030, (ii) a National Growth Strategy and Action Plan approved in 2014 with some actions oriented to reduce greenhouse gas emissions; and (iii) a BRIEF on climate change adaptation in Viet Nam. However, these documents are not accompanied by actual implementation projects and activities.

75. It is worth noticing that even though Viet Nam started climate change programmes sometime ago, the country has no national strategy that will end in 2030. It is therefore important to develop a new climate change strategy for the coming decades.

* Moreover, the projected increase of temperature will lead to an increased need to ensure water for agricultural production but does not demonstrate evident connections to ensure water supply through programmes to conserve river basins or restoration and conservation programmes at large.


### Capacity of accredited entities and executing entities to deliver

5.2.2 MARIN is the executing entity for the project. MARIN oversees the coordination of a variety of national policy and institutional frameworks for the agriculture and rural development sectors, including the National Rural Development Strategy 2016–2030, which focuses on ensuring food security for the vulnerable population and has an important budget allocation within the country's overall budget.

The World Bank is being the primary investor and service provider to be the facilitating of investments and services provided by others.

* MARIN and UNDP are currently collaborating in the implementation of the ongoing GCF Enhancing Coastal Resilience project as well as UN-REDD Viet Nam – Phase II Programme. For this reason, MARIN has been designated as the lead executing entity for the proposed 2016–2020 National Implementation Modality, which considers managerial, technical and administrative and financial management capacities.

* In addition, the Central Project Office for Water Resources (CPO) will be delegated to coordinate the project. CPO will serve as focal point with donors to implement irrigation development projects in the Mekong Delta region. The CPO has experience in implementing large-scale irrigation projects, including the WEIP project and the proposed last-mile connection project.

* CPO has wide experience managing foreign-funded projects and has successfully completed several large-scale irrigation projects in the Mekong Delta region, including the WEIP Project Agency projects in the irrigation sector. CPO already works closely with ministries, including the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development (MARD) as well as with provinces, including at the commune level, specifically through the WEIP project. MARIN, through CPO, will also tap into ongoing partnerships with development partners (e.g. Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ), Asian Development Bank, World Bank, ADB), civil society organizations, international non-governmental organizations and other stakeholders to mobilize and catalyze resources, maximizing the impact of combined resources.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

#### Scale: Medium-High
1.6 Cost-effectiveness and efficiency


---
**Page 136**

## The proposed project to complement the WEIDAP project is requesting total GCF financing of USD 30,205,367 million to implement all project activities.

The total amount for the WEIDAP project is USD 124,226.08 million over the six-year project cycle. The breakdown of the funding from the GCF is as follows: (i) a GCF grant of USD 95.37 million; (ii) an ADB grant of USD 10.05 million; and (iii) government co-financing of USD 24,829,936.

## From the GCF budget, around 50 per cent will be invested in contractual services to deliver construction/provision of the following assets:

- A total of 4,765 last-mile connections to the WEIDAP water infrastructure spread across five provinces (Quang Binh, Quang Tri, Thua Thien Hue, Khanh Hoa and Ninh Thuan) using demographic and socioeconomic analysis;
- Construction of 100 shared ponds and 183 new shared ponds with bioengineering solutions, in addition to upgrading 484 existing ponds;
- Providing 6,821 households with CCA packages; and
- A total of 55 learning sites, including on seeds, seedlings, fertilizer, highly efficient irrigation systems, etc.

## The other 50 per cent will be invested in project personnel and in developing knowledge and development platforms such as FPS, training a team, the trainer programme and delivering capacity-building workshops, as well as providing access to credit and microfinance services. In the view of the independent TAP this percentage seems overstated. Potential savings could be achieved by reducing the number of learning sites.

## The project estimates incremental earnings generated by the enhanced crop yields and income due to increased and more reliable water during the dry season. In a modest scenario, the financial benefits are estimated at USD 105 million per year in terms of agricultural production generation. With the GCF grant, the project achieves financial viability with an acceptable capital recovery period (CRP) of 23 years. The net present value (NPV) is estimated to be a net present value of USD 21,991,000.

## An economic analysis was carried out for the project by comparing with and without the project scenarios using the methodology developed for the Economic Analysis of Projects of the United Nations Development Programme (2005). The analysis shows a 25–40 per cent increase in agricultural production generation. In addition, the project is expected to generate income for the benefited households. Overall, the project is expected to generate a total economic net present value of USD 138 million.

## The operation and maintenance plan emphasize the strong commitment to maintain the assets by the Government of Viet Nam and the community, expecting 10 years of continued operations and maintenance after the end of the project cycle (2025). The estimated operation and maintenance costs have been estimated for a time span of 16 years (2020–2035). If the operation and maintenance plan is not followed, the assets will be transferred to the central and provincial governments and USD 4,642,792 (cash and in kind) by the beneficiary households.

## The independent TAP considers that the proposed financial structure is heavily dependent on government support. A more detailed financial assessment should be developed in the design stage of the assets, ensuring a real assessment of the willingness to maintain and maintenance of the individual and communal assets, refining the sustainability analysis of the project.

## On the other hand, the Government of Viet Nam should think of including last-mile connections in the national water infrastructure development plan. It is also recommended that arrangements for knowledge-sharing, capacity-building and extension services, in order to ensure resources to scale the model to more vulnerable communities in the country.

### Overall Remarks from the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

The independent TAP recommends that the Board approve the project subject to the following conditions:

(a) Prior to the second disbursement of the project, the accredited entity shall submit to the Secretariat a report, in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat, which shall contain:
- An estimate of the area cultivated for each major crop type per cropping season
- An assessment of the water requirements for irrigation and an estimate of the additional volume of water needed for irrigation in the selected farm areas;
- An analysis of the current programmes to conserve and restore water basins that are critical for irrigation, including a review of existing water harvesting and integrated irrigation systems; assessment of water availability based on agricultural land-use planning, soil conservation measures, and other relevant factors
- A description of planned construction and restoration activities in order to ensure continued supply of ecosystem services (water, microclimate and soils) favourable to the downstream communities and the overall sustainability of the proposed production system, including institutions, financial and operational responsibilities; and
- A detailed plan for monitoring and evaluation of project completion including an analysis of a possible water subsidy water tariff system and an assessment of the willingness to pay by the farmers for water related activities, as well as the potential impact on the value of other water assets.

(b) The independent TAP further recommends that the accredited entity, together with the Government of Viet Nam, develop updated agricultural and climate change strategies and plans beyond 2020, supporting the vision of change of the proposed project, presenting the overall pathway for achieving this vision.

1. Climate Innovation Platforms are a key innovation that bring together all stakeholders in the different value chains – farmers, input and technical assistance providers, traders, processors, and consumers – to develop and disseminate strategies for the development of the “industry” (from field to consumer) for the different products. These will include better-off farmers (“agribusinesses”) to nurture the development of smallholder agriculture. The platforms will link Farmer Field Schools with mixed socio-economic groups, poor/near-poor farmers, particularly women and youth, through training, capacity building, knowledge mentoring relationships and partnerships with more successful farmers and investors in order to improve their productivity.

2. A second innovation is the participatory development of agro-climate advisories (Participatory Scenario Planning) involving farmers, agro-climatological experts, and other stakeholders. This will involve the collection, analysis, and synthesis of traditional knowledge and information, and synthesizing them to produce actionable, localized agricultural advice for smallholder farmers in order to reduce climate risk.

3. Basin management through conservation, regeneration and management of forests is well established in Vietnam, and includes a large number of plans and programs detailed under section E5.1. of the Funding Proposal (Existence of a national climate change strategy and action plan). The implementation of REDD+ (REDD+ NAPAs and NAPs), including the current UN-REDD+ program in the Central Highlands, is well underway. However, there are concerns about the sustainability or renewal of these policies is under discussion. The Institute of Water Resources Planning (IWRP) operates within the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development to provide technical advice on water resources management, including regulating the use of water resources, as well as protecting and developing water resources in a sustainable manner. IWRP also provides technical assistance for water resources across Vietnam. IWRP will be directly engaged by the ADB under the WELAID project (Water Efficiency and Livelihood Improvement [surface and groundwater] and water allocation framework development).

UNDP's response to the condition prior to the 2nd disbursement proposed by the independent TAP:

- Key crops currently grown
- Total numbers of hectares that poor or near poor farmers are currently cultivating.
- Expected crop mix after application of CRA packages;
- Estimated number of hectares of resilient production that is expected to take place; and,
- Estimated increase in the crop water demand for different crops under climate change.

The CRA packages, distributed through the Farmer Field School, are intended to equip farmers with technical skills and to provide financial incentives for Y1 diversifying the crop.

mix to include more drought resistant crops and varieties (e.g. garlic); 2) introducing resilience-enhancing techniques (e.g. crop rotation); and 3) diversification of the system itself by integrating with perennials or perennials with other water-efficient perennials (e.g. coffee with avocados).

Before the second disbursement, tables will be presented with updated estimates of:

- The areas of each crop cultivated (estimate) in each cropping season within the areas served by the WEIDAP.
- The range of expected changes in ET and crop water requirements for each crop and cropping season
- The water requirements for each crop and cropping season under both current climate and future climate change
- The additional volume of water required for supplementary irrigation in the farmed areas

Note that at the time of the second disbursement, the CRA packages will not have been delivered; therefore, refined estimates of the new crop mix will be used for this purpose.

An analysis of the current programs to conserve and restore water basins that are providing water services to allow the development of the proposed irrigation systems, assessment of the potential impacts on these water basins (e.g. through land use planning actions) detailing land use planning actions as well as conservation and restoration activities in order to ensure continued supply of ecosystem services (water, microclimate and soil) downstream from the WEIDAP areas, including institutional, financial and operational responsibilities.

Please note that this analysis is part of a larger-scale program to be financed through the ADB loan (WEIDAP), and this analysis itself will also form part of UNDP's submission in October 2019. Purpose of this analysis is to understand water availability and demand for irrigation, including agricultural, domestic, industrial and environmental flow, and the complementary last-mile connections between these uses. Given that the WEIDAP areas are already served by GCF investment it is a small additional component to the wider WEIDAP programme; it cannot be expected to address the impact of large-scale WASH investments on irrigation water demands.

However, recognizing that monitoring of the water use throughout the lifetime of the project and beyond is desirable, the project will work with the Government and ADB to monitor the water use for irrigation at headwater for the WEIDAP command area. It will also attempt to monitor the water use in the farmed areas by crop type and season (i.e., the crops cultivated per cropping season) and their water requirements in the WEIDAP areas, the results of which will be presented before the second disbursement.

In addition, stacking of water conservation programmes within the catchment areas and an assessment of the impacts on demand (By crop) to understand water demand will be undertaken before the second disbursement.

- A detailed analysis of the sustainability of the project interventions after project completion including an analysis of a possible cross-subsidy water tariff system and an assessment of the willingness to pay to maintain and operate the water assets by the communities.

Of the US$464M contributions expected from beneficiary households towards O&M, over 16 years and assuming that all beneficiaries will be able to access the project interventions, this is estimated to range from US$10 to US$48 per household depending on the support they receive from the project. This includes an average annual contribution of US$25/year for water supply, US$7/year supplementary irrigation US$10/year; and on-farm water-saving technologies US$40/year). If a household has access to both piped connection and on-farm water-saving technologies, the annual O&M contributions will be US$67.

The average incremental increase in income from the project intervention was conservatively estimated at 14% of the farm income for beneficiary households or approximately US$530/year. Based on this, the financial obligations expected to the project beneficiaries for the O&M costs are between 2% (for 95% of the beneficiaries) and 6% (for 5% of beneficiaries) to 42% of farm incomes (for approximately 5% of the beneficiaries), which is expected to be a sustainable level of contribution towards the operation and maintenance necessary for the project. Moreover, please note that it is a standard practice in Viet Nam for communities to contribute up to 10% of their income to O&M costs. This point has been discussed during stakeholder consultations undertaken in the target communitie

That said nonetheless, we acknowledge the importance of monitoring the performance of community and local government co-financing for O&M requirements for ensuring sustainability of project interventions. The level of contribution from communities towards O&M mobilization will be monitored and presented in the AFIS. Moreover, in light of comments from ITAP, towards the end of the project implementation, a report on exit strategy will be prepared with recommendations for ensuring the sustainability of the project interventions.

# Executive summary

1. As part of the project design process, the gender assessment was conducted by UNDP to provide an overview of gender and climate change issues and present gender mainstreaming gaps that are addressed in this proposal. The assessment used different methods such as desk review of current policy documents and study reports; consultative meetings with different stakeholders at national, district and commune levels; and interviews with key informants who have been directly impacted by this project. This gender assessment provides inputs and baseline information to the design and implementation of the project's gender action plan (GAP) aimed at ensuring gender equality in climate change adaptation and mitigation activities in Viet Nam for the period 2014-2016, with priority given to prioritized gender issues in Viet Nam and in the target project provinces.

## Legal and policy framework

### Strengths of the legal and policy framework: Gender mainstreaming. The gender assessment showed that Viet Nam has a strong legal and policy framework to address gender equality and climate change response. This advantage can be seen through a series of legal and policy documents that have been developed in recent years, including laws, decrees, policies, strategies, guidelines, and climate change-related legal documents. However, the gender assessment also shows some gaps in implementation of gender equality principles in these legal and policy frameworks as well as in other related general and climate change-related legal documents. The primary issues is that the legal documents on gender have strongly confirmed that gender mainstreaming principles will be applied in the whole process of policy formulation and implementation at all levels, including national, provincial, district, commune level. However, there are some weaknesses in the capacity to guide on gender equality as well as limitations in the capacity to guide on gender mainstreaming in climate change response processes. The main reason for this is that the number of women working in this area is very low in identifying gender issues and taking action, as well as allocating resources to address gender issues when designing and implementing climate change programs. As a result, gender mainstreaming principles are not fully applied at all levels.

### Mechanisms to promote gender equality: Viet Nam has three strong mechanisms to address gender equality in the country. The first mechanism is the National Gender Equality Committee (NGEC). This state management agency on gender equity is the first strong mechanism in theory. According to the Law on Gender Equality, the Ministry of Labour, Invalids and Social Affairs (MOLISA) will be a state management agency for gender equality at national level. MOLISA has been established as an equal gender equity at provincial, district and commune levels. Both MOLISA and the People's Committee have their own gender equality committees to guide on gender equality in all activities of the organization. The weakness of this mechanism is a limited capacity of technical staff or leaders in provision of guidance and orientation on gender mainstreaming into practice. Due to the limited capacity of gender mainstreaming, it is difficult for MOLISA to provide sufficient guidance on gender mainstreaming to other technical agencies at the same level. The second mechanism is the Vietnam Women's Union (VWU). VWU has been established as a strong organization in Viet Nam and has significant power to influence how gender mainstreaming works. This also has an organizational structure from national level to commune level. This mechanism has also been established in each organization of VWU at all levels, including national, provincial, district, commune and village level. The mission and vision of the VWU is to protect the legitimacy of rights and benefits of women. However, due to limitation of gender mainstreaming capacity in VWU, it is difficult for VWU to provide sufficient guidance on gender mainstreaming into policy formulation and implementation processes in general and climate change in particular.
