### Poverty and social issues: Poverty and gender is differentiated by household structure and ethnic minority groups. Poverty is a heavy burden on households headed by women, poor households and ethnic minority households. Children and elderly people are more likely impacted by poverty. Women are disadvantaged in terms of access to resources and services. They have limited opportunities to take advantage of development opportunities, which results in relegating them to the roles of mother and housewife. In contrast, men dominate control over land and other valuable assets in the family and community. This is because women’s property rights are not recognized by law. Women’s access to land since registration has followed the old land law. This can lead to the denial of women's rights in cases of divorce or widowhood. Women also have limited access to credit, financial services, education and use of income. Limited asset possession reduces women’s access to credit and investment opportunities. Small-scale farmers, in particular women, face unequal access to knowledge, technology and markets. This is because women are often excluded from the decision-making process that determines where indigenous people live. The rural population in the VEDAP irrigation command area is the five largest proportion of poor households.

### 5. Employment and gender issues: Women are concentrated in fewer sectors and occupations than men. This highlights female workers' greater vulnerability in working conditions and pay, reflected, for example, by the fact that women earn less than men on average (World Bank, 2011). The World Bank also draws attention to the disproportionate time spent by women on unpaid work at home and in agriculture. Women are more likely to be employed in informal sectors or in precarious work given that systematic evidence on these activities is still sparse. Women's wages are now about 75% of men’s, according to the 2009 Labour Force Survey (LFS), not considering differences in education or job experience. The gender pay gap is even larger for women working in agriculture and rural work, given that systematic evidence on these activities is still sparse. Women's wages are now about 75% of men’s, according to the 2009 Labour Force Survey (LFS), not considering differences in education or job experience. The gender pay gap is even larger for women working in agriculture and rural work, given that systematic evidence on these activities is still sparse.

### Women are also more vulnerable jobs, for example, own-account work and unpaid family labour. They are more likely to be employed in informal sectors or in precarious work. In the agriculture sector, women make essential contributions to food production and to agricultural and rural economies, but it is impossible to verify empirically the share produced by women. Female farmers, for example, are often excluded from the decision-making process that determines where indigenous people live.

### Gender mainstreaming is a key component of the project areas in. In regard to the state management agency or gender equality (People’s Committee and DOLUSA), most staff and leaders assigned to do gender work from the state management agencies have been trained on gender equality concepts, gender analysis skills, and gender mainstreaming techniques (gender analysis skills) and to propose specific actions for gender mainstreaming into specific technical areas. The interests of women are not always taken into account in the development process. It is important to make sure that these two agencies can perform state management tasks effectively. In regard to provincial DARDs, the provincial government agency on agriculture, they must develop their own action plans on gender equality and gender mainstreaming. However, there is a significant gap between the knowledge gap in knowledge and skills of technical staff and leaders of DARD on gender concepts; and gender mainstreaming techniques. The results of training are not reflected in the development process because gender equality and gender mainstreaming are not realized effectively by the technical staff and leaders because of lack of knowledge and skills to do so. The Vietnam Women’s Union's staff is not currently able to maximize the role of women in agricultural production and change resilience.

### Key gender issues in the larger project community: Male and female farmers are aware of negative impacts of climate change on agricultural production and daily life, such as reductions in productivity and quality and lack of water leading to abandonment of land. There are limited

### 11. Migration and drought impact: Men have more opportunity to work outside the community while women are expected to stay at home to take care of children and do domestic work. Due to lack of job opportunities in the community, local people must find employment in other provinces (e.g., Lao Dong, Ho Chi Minh City). Women also migrate with their families for better livelihoods or to send remittances back to other provinces. In most of the project target communes, approximately 50-60% of the farmland could not be cultivated in the drought season. Currently, access to water for agricultural production is a serious issue for farmers and women.

### 12. In addition, drought more seriously impacts female farmers than male farmers. As mentioned above, women are responsible for household chores and children care while men migrate or work outside the community. Women have limited opportunities to participate in income-generating activities as they lack access to other provinces. Gender norms block women from the opportunities and resources to perform their roles effectively. At the livelihoods of women are completely dependent on agriculture, lack of water in the dry season makes it difficult for them to cultivate crops or livestock. As a result, women have limited stable income from agricultural activities. Due to men’s migration to other provinces, there is no support from or information about climate change adaptation and gender equality issues. Women face challenges such as lack of knowledge and information about climate change adaptation in general and adaptive solutions in agricultural production in particular make women more vulnerable than men. Women are limited to some agriculture-related activities due to their responsibilities for household chores and children care, especially during community events.

### 13. The gender profile also provided specific recommendations to address gender inequality issues in project communes by providing a gender action plan that focuses on some key solutions including:

#### Build capacity for gender equality mainstreaming for local stakeholders, with a focus on strengthening the participation of women and men in public consultations on project implementation and climate change resilience in particular. The project will provide an enabling environment to promote women’s participation in decision-making processes at all levels (national, provincial, district, commune) through the appointment of female participants in project management boards, interest groups, water users groups, farmer field school facilitators, etc.

#### Promote equal access to climate information: The project will design specific activities such as field-based training (using FPS approach) and regular group-discussion meetings that target both male and female farmers at all levels (national, provincial, district, commune). These activities will be implemented in the project areas. The project will also use diversified methods and gender-sensitive design and implementation of communication materials to promote rainwater harvesting/agricultural water and agricultural management. These opportunities provide a constructive platform for both men and women to maximize their experience and capacity to apply this information in daily practice as well as share this knowledge with others.

#### Promote equality in decision making processes: At community level, the project will design interventions that ensure equal participation of women and men in all aspects of agricultural production and investments. Special models for women will be considered to provide an enabling environment and opportunities for women to enhance their roles and contributions to implementation of climate change adaptation measures at household and community levels. At household level, the project will support men and women to implement water conservation models in agriculture production and access to markets. These models can be considered as means to promote open discussions between men and women in decision-making processes. The project will support

### Introduction

The project is designed aiming to empower vulnerable smallholders in the Central Highlands and South-Central Coast regions of Viet Nam. The focus is to empower women and ethnic minority farmers to cope with climate change. The project will help ethnic minority farmers to address climate risks of agricultural production, including water availability, adopting climate-resilience, and water-efficient agricultural cropping systems. The project also empowers women and ethnic minorities in accessing market information for risk management related to water and agricultural planning and management.

The project aims to achieve key outputs. The first output is to improve access to water for vulnerable smallholder farmers through the development of a water management plan that takes into account climate change, technology, and institutional capacity building. The second output is to strengthen capacities of smallholder farmers to apply climate and market information, technologies, and services in their agricultural production.

The gender assessment was conducted as part of the project design process to ensure that the project will address key gender issues in climate change response. The assessment not only reviewed current policy frameworks but also provided specific recommendations on how the project will promote gender equality in practice.

### Objectives of the gender assessment

The overall objective of the gender assessment was to identify key gender issues that are relevant to climate change policies and explore potential opportunities and actions needed to promote gender mainstreaming in climate change resilience activities in the project areas. The specific objectives of the gender assessment include:

1. Review current gender and climate change policy frameworks (both strengths and gaps) on gender equality and climate change resilience.
2. Assess current capacities of the relevant stakeholders and current gender mainstreaming practices in relation to climate change resilience.
3. Identify key gender issues in climate change resilience and actions needed to be done to facilitate gender equality in climate change resilience activities.
4. Provide recommendations on actions needed to promote gender equality in climate change resilience.

The Gender assessment is building on what has been proposed by the GCF-funded project titled "Improving the Resilience of vulnerable coastal communities to climate related impacts in Viet Nam".

# Annex XIII (c) – GENDER ASSESSMENT AND ACTION PLAN

## 3. Methodology

### 3.1 Desk review

The desk review was conducted to collect all available data and gender baseline information for project design. The desk review focused on gender and climate change policies, consulting study reports made by the World Bank, UNDP, and other organizations in the field of gender and climate change.

The desk review aims to provide an overview of gender and climate change policies as well as policy implementation gaps that have hindered the key gaps in implementation of gender policy in climate changes and agriculture practice.

### 3.2 Consultative meetings with stakeholders

The field visits were conducted to a number of target project communes in three provinces (Dak Lak, Dak Nong and Kon Tum) covering the five project provinces. The focus of field visits was to consult with local communities on the implementation of gender and climate change resilience activities and their current experience in mainstreaming gender into climate change resilience activities. One consultative meeting was conducted at each commune level with representatives from various government agencies such as People’s Committee (PC), Department of Agriculture and Rural Development (DARD); Department of Labour, Invalids and Social Affairs (DVSA), Department of Ethnic Minority (CEM), Women’s Union and Farmers’ Union. The same or similar meetings were also conducted at district level. In addition, consultative meetings were also conducted with participation of leaders and technical officers from key agencies at district and provincial levels.

28. These consultative meetings at provincial, district and commune level aimed to gain a better understanding of gaps in knowledge, attitude and practices related to climate change and gender mainstreaming. The meetings also aimed to identify the current experience and lessons learned on practice on gender mainstreaming in the process of planning, implementing and monitoring climate change resilience activities. In addition, these meetings were used as an opportunity for stakeholders to express their needs and concerns on building gender mainstreaming capacity in climate change response.

29. At provincial level, consultative meetings were conducted with representatives from UN agencies (e.g. IFAD, FAO, UN Women), government partners (e.g. MARA, MUSA, Women’s Union) and international organizations (e.g. CARE, Oxfam). The main objective of these meetings was to identify potential opportunities to collaborate and utilize current experience and good practice from those organizations in this project.

30. Consultative meetings at district level were more extensive and involved a wider range of people, from farmers (women and men) to government agencies (commune, district, province) to NGOs/CBOs. The main objective was to identify the gender gaps that exist within these institutions or the Women’s Union or as farmers and heads of households, gender-specific concerns were not necessarily or explicitly highlighted. It is noteworthy that many ethnic groups in Dak Nong, Dak Lak and Kon Tum have a strong tradition of women owning land and other productive assets such as power and property passed from mother to daughter. Men assume the main responsibilities to go out to work and earn money for their families while women are responsible for household chores and other outcomes, in essence mirroring the concerns of their fellow men farmers. As such, women farmers tended to

### 3.5 Focus Group Discussions

The focus group discussions (FGDs) were conducted with participation of male and female farmers, who will benefit from and will be impacted by the project. The FGDs were conducted in a representative number of target communes in Dak Lak, Ninh Thuan and Dak Nong provinces. At least one FGD was conducted per each target commune, and there was an average of eight people (four men and four women) participating in each group.

The objectives of the FGDs with farmers were to identify key gender inequality factors in division of labour and responsibilities within households, as well as barriers that prevent women from accessing finance in particular. The FGDs also identified gender needs and social factors that block women and men from accessing climate change information and implementing climate change resilience solutions in agriculture.

In addition, the FGDs also helped to provide a better understanding of the feasibility of gender actions to be taken by the project. This was done through discussions on the perceptions of how to address gender issues and meet the concerns of both male and female farmers when planning, implementing and monitoring climate change resilience activities in the target project communes.

## Key findings of the gender assessment

### 4.1 Policy framework on addressing gender inequality in Viet Nam

Viet Nam was one of the first countries to ratify the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) in 1980, which was subsequently ratified in 1982. The country also made huge progress in reforming its legal and policy framework to guarantee gender equality and nondiscrimination. A number of laws and policies have been enacted since the ratification of CEDAW that could be seen through the efforts of the Government of Viet Nam to establish a solid legislative framework for political, economic, social, cultural, educational and other spheres of life in all fields of life.

At the highest level of law, the principles of "non-discrimination" of race or sex", and "equal citizens and residents" have the same rights in every aspect of politics, economy, society and family" were included in the Constitution (which was revised in 2013). This provides a strong foundation to ensure that gender equality is guaranteed at all levels.

The Law on Gender Equality was adopted in 2006, which defines gender equality and gender-based discrimination. The law also sets out the principles of non-discrimination and equal rights for both sexes. Of this law also provides general instruction about gender mainstreaming into the legislative formulation process and the implementation of laws and policies to ensure the realization of greater gender equality in Viet Nam. The Law on Domestic Violence Prevention and Control, passed in 2007, is another significant piece of legislation acknowledging for the first time violence against women by partners as a punishable offence.

#### Document of Vietnam has also issued three decrees to facilitate implementation of the Law on Gender Equality:

36. The Government of Vietnam has also issued three decrees to facilitate implementation of the Law on Gender Equality.

37. Decree 79/2008/NĐ-CP was approved on 4 June 2008. It provides guidance on implementation of some articles of the Law on Gender Equality, specifically, the roles and responsibilities, and coordination mechanisms among various levels of government in order to ensure effective implementation of gender equality.

The Ministry of Labour Invalids and Social Affairs (MOLISA) was assigned as the leading agency for the implementation of the Decree. The MOLISA is responsible for developing and implementing national programs on gender equality; (2) evaluate the process of mainstreaming gender into policy formulation, development, and implementation at all levels of government, and all international regulations on gender equality.

Other ministries are responsible for reviewing, developing, and approving legislative documents, and providing direction for gender mainstreaming in their own sectors.

38. Following this Decree, the People's Committees at various levels are responsible for developing policies and programs to ensure implementation of the Law on Gender Equality within their domain. The People’s Committees are also responsible for mainstreaming gender issues into socio-economic development plans and into policy formulation at management level.

42. Decree 48/2009/NĐ-CP (19th May 2009) provides specific guidance and methodologies to ensure gender equality in the implementation of the Law on Gender Equality. These include gender mainstreaming in communications, education and training, and the management of public services.

The Decree also sets out a list of measures for ensuring the rights of women under-related workers in remote and under-developed areas. Decree 52/2009/NĐ-CP (10th June 2009) provides specific guidance on the implementation of the Law on Gender Equality, including the framework for use by the authorities for penalties in cases of violations.

40. Resolution 57/NQ-CP (1st December 2008), approved the Action Plan Resolution 1/2008/NĐ-CP that aims to ensure the effective implementation and enforcement of institutionalization recommendations. The Action Plan aims to raise awareness, to promote effective management of administrative agencies, to realize gender equality in all areas of life, including education, health care, and the professions. It also aims to meet the goals of national industrialization and modernization.

In the future, women will be able to participate more effectively in decision-making processes at both personal and cultural status. Furthermore improves the possibility of participating in social activities. They will be equal in terms of rights and opportunities with men in all areas of life.

41. National Strategy on Gender Equality for 2011 – 2020 (26th December 2010) emphasizes that gender equality is one of the key factors to improving the quality of life of individuals, families, and society. The general strategy on Gender Equality is to ensure equal opportunities between men and women in terms of opportunities, participation, and satisfaction in the fields of politics, economy, culture, education, health care, and social services.

The Strategy has set seven objectives1, in which there is one objective on improvement of state

# Annex XIII (c) – GENDER ASSESSMENT AND ACTION PLAN

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

### Management capacity on gender equality

The official seven objectives of the national strategy on gender equality also include the improvement of state management capacity on gender equality.

42. In addition, the National Program on Gender Equality for 2016 – 2020 was approved by Decision 124/QD-TTg dated 22nd July 2011. The Program objective is to fundamentally transform public environment and policies in order to ensure gender equality in all spheres of life, reduce the gap between men and women, promote women in high-risk areas of inequality, and contribute to successful implementation of the National Strategy on Gender Equality for 2016 – 2020. The Program also aims at promoting gender equality and advancement of women. The Circular 191/2009/TT-BTC was approved by the Ministry of Finance in October 2009 to regulate the implementation, supervision, inspection, evaluation, assessment, management, and use of budget for gender equality and advancement of women. It also provides government agencies with guidelines on the preparation, budgeting, fund allocations, and payment procedures for activities promoting gender equality.

43. In addition to the enactment of these laws that specifically concern women’s rights, notable efforts are being made in other areas of law. For example, the 2015 amendment to the Labour Code, which came into effect in May 2013, added new provisions on non-discrimination and women's labour rights such as: prohibition of sexual harassment; exemption of maternity leave to six months; equal pay for work of similar value; equal opportunities for promotion and career advancement; and equal legal value. Gender has been also mainstreamed in Civil Law, Labour Law, Penal Code, Law on Marriage and Family, Law on Women’s Rights, Law on Children, Law on Education, Law on Culture, Law on Education, Vocational Training Law, Law on Public Healthcare, Law on Complaints and Democratic Participation, Law on Social Security, Law on National Defence, Law on Civil Service, as well as other legal documents such as Prime Minister decrees and decisions, and ministerial circulates. The Regulations on the Implementation of the Constitution for Socialist Democracy and promoted the participation of men and women in the community’s political life.

## 4.2 Gender mainstreaming in climate change resilience policies

### Government of Viet Nam has also developed a strong legal framework and frameworks to respond to climate change

The Government of Viet Nam has also developed a strong legal framework and frameworks to respond to climate change. The Decision No.13/2015/QD-TTg dated 9th September 2015 by the Communist Party is a high-level directive on climate change. It outlines aims to strengthen the active roles of relevant agencies in responding to climate change, including: environmental protection and management; natural resources management and environmental protection; it is the basis and premise for planning directions and policies for socio-economic development, ensuring national defense, security and social security. Climate change is a key issue that must be addressed by all levels.

### 2013 Law on Natural Disaster Prevention and Control

The 2013 Law on Natural Disaster Prevention and Control reinforces gender equity as one of seven core principles of disaster prevention and control. The document provides clear guidance on how to implement the law, including the roles and responsibilities of relevant agencies in this work. It prescribes the activities of natural disaster prevention and control, the rights and obligations of individuals and organizations, and the role of local authorities in natural disaster prevention and control, and state management and available resources to ensure achievement of the stated objectives.

The Strategic Orientation for Sustainable Development in Vietnam (National Agenda 21) was approved by Decision No.30/2015/QD-TTg dated 9th September 2015. The document sets out the following categories which include eradication of extreme poverty, narrowing the gap between rural and urban areas, women and

### National Target Program in Response to Climate Change (NTP-RC)

In 2007, the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment (MONRE) was assigned, in coordination with other ministries, to formulate a national target program for response to climate change. The NTP-RC was approved by Decision No. 135/QĐ-TTg dated December 2012 by Decision 1183/QĐ-TTG (30th August 2012). The goal of NTP-RC is to implement the National Strategy on Climate Change (NSCC) and the National Action Plan for Climate Change (NAPCC), in order to adapt to climate change, greenhouse gas emission reduction, low-carbon economy, and active participation of Vietnam in international cooperation on the global climate system. NTP-RC identifies six specific objectives:

* Update the assessment of climate change in Vietnam, including sea level rise impact assessment of climate change on specific fields and locations; identify solutions for adaptation.
* Develop database system on climate change and sea level rise in order to provide inputs for the planning of socio-economic development;
* Update the vulnerability assessment related to climate change;
* Build capacity for organizations and institutions, and policy on adaptation and mitigation of greenhouse gas emissions;
* Strengthen international cooperation on climate change;
* Raise awareness among people on climate change.

### The NTP-RC highlights the importance of gender equality in sustainable development. It stresses the need to conduct vulnerability assessments at sectoral, regional, and community levels, as potential climate change impacts on women can hinder the achievement of the MDGs.

#### National Strategy 2011 - 2020 for Climate Change Response

The NSCC was approved under Decision 2319/QĐ-TTg dated 5 October 2011. The Strategy identifies key tasks to support government agencies and ministries and local authorities:

* To respond to climate change and rising sea levels;
* To ensure food and water security, sustainable development, biodiversity, greenhouse gas emissions reduction, and adaptation to climate change;
* To develop community models to respond to climate change;
* To enhance international cooperation on climate change; and
* To allocate budget resources on climate change response.

#### National Action Plan for Climate Change Response 2012 - 2020

The NAPCC was approved under Decision 1474/QĐ-TTg dated 5th October 2012. The Action Plan identifies key tasks of government and other agencies.

### 52. The Law on Gender Equality (2006) does not mention gender mainstreaming in climate change directly. However, it does provide an overall framework that requires gender mainstreaming in all policy development and implementation processes. This is reflected in the law's provisions on women's rights in legal documents including defining gender issues and measures, forecasting impacts of the regulations on women, and ensuring equal access to resources for men and women.

### 53. In addition, state agencies have the responsibility to mainstream gender in policy formulation, and prepare gender equality action plans. The National Gender Equality Committee is responsible for monitoring the agency in charge of gender mainstreaming to appraise the gender equality in policy development.

### Research shows that climate change impacts affect women's lives in many ways. Impacts including increased water scarcity, reduced agricultural productivity, and loss of livelihoods are felt by women more than men. Women have less access to, and control over the resources that they depend upon for food and income. In addition, women are more vulnerable to natural disasters because they often live closer to areas where disasters occur.

### Some key recommendations made by international development partners include:

- Elimination of gender stereotypes;
- Participation in decision-making and gender analysis;
- Quotas on women's leadership and participation in decision-making;
- Establishment of a National Women's Union;
- Gendered MAE and reporting;
- Increased funding for women;
- Expert networks including male allies;
- Mainstreaming gender equality into climate change dialogues and reviews;
- Advocacy and action at the regional and international level.

### 4.3 Gender policy implementation mechanisms

In Viet Nam, there are three structural mechanisms with a specific mission to address gender equality and advancement of women:

1. The first mechanism is the state management agency on gender equality and women's advancement. This is the Department for Gender Equality in MOUSA. It is a key body responsible for formulating policies and strategies related to gender equality. It was established in early 2008 to advise the Minister on carrying out state management activities, including the formulation of policies and strategies related to gender equality and the role and responsibilities of the Division of Gender Equality vis-à-vis the agency as a whole. There is a gap in

### Capacity Assessment of the State Management Agencies and Other Concerned Agencies to Implement the Gender Equality Law of Vietnam

#### 55. The Law on Gender Equality also confirmed that the People's Committees are the state management agencies responsible for gender equality at all levels.

The state management roles rely completely on consultation and technical support provided by DOLISA and the Committee for Women’s Affairs (CWA) to ensure that the law is implemented in a manner consistent with experience and evidence of good practice. The People’s Committee have been facing many difficulties and challenges, including:

- **56. According to the Law on Gender Equality (2014), DOLISA at provincial level is also a state management agency on gender equality.** DOLISA has established organizational structures at provincial and district levels that are responsible for implementing gender equality policies and laws. At the provincial level, the chair of the provincial People’s Committee and the vice-chair of the Viet Nam Women’s Union act as the focal points for gender equality work. At the district level, the chairperson of the district People’s Committee and the president of NCFAW are assigned in part-time positions. These persons have as their primary tasks to provide recommendations to leaders of agencies in implementing gender policies. They also provide consultation to other agencies on gender equality issues.

- **57. The second structural mechanism to promote advancement of women is the Committee for Women’s Affairs (CWA).** At national level, CWA acts as an inter-sectoral body that counsels Viet Nam’s Prime Minister at (national) level and People’s Committees at provincial and district levels on gender equality issues. At local level, CWA also plays a role in building economic empowerment. At national level, NCFAW also supports research and coordinates interdisciplinary activities to promote women’s development.

- **58. The third structural mechanism is the president of NCFAW, with vice-chairs from the Viet Nam Women’s Union and the Vice Minister of MOLISA.** At provincial level, the chair of the provincial People’s Committee and the president of NCFAW are responsible for gender equality work in relevant agencies such as DOLISA, Women’s Union, Agriculture and Rural Development division, etc. Since the members of these committees are not full-time officials, they have limited time to focus on promotion of gender equality within their organizations. The quality of gender work will depend very much upon their commitment and showing willingness to promote gender within their organization.

- **59. The Vietnam Women’s Union (VNU) is a member of the CWA at national level with the mission to protect legitimate rights and benefits of women.** The VNU has its own organizational structure, established in 1946 as a non-governmental organization. Its main role in relation to gender equality is to conduct dissemination of gender policies and develop capacity on gender equality. This organization also provides training for members and other stakeholders on gender equality and providing feedback on the implementation of gender policy. Among other mass organizations, VNU is known as an important partner in promoting gender equality at all levels. At provincial level, they are often invited for technical support to other organizations to provide training courses on gender equality. However, their capacity remains limited due to lack of resources and time spent on gender work focusing in specific areas. In addition, the VNU is a representative member of many committees and joint mechanisms with the mission to promote the voice and interests of women and girls. However, their

### 4.4 Poverty reduction and Gender

60. Viet Nam reached lower middle-income country status in 2010 and poverty has been declining continuously from 58% of the population below the poverty line in 1993 to 8.4% in 2014. However, poverty is unevenly distributed across regions and among different socio-economic groups and rural areas. According to VLSIS 2014, 3% of the urban population lives below the national poverty line, compared with 57% of the rural population. The poor are concentrated in remote mountainous areas where inequality and benefits of growth have spread unevenly across Viet Nam; increasing income inequality between regions and among socio-economic groups has been observed over time. Women and ethnic minorities are considerably underrepresented in middle-income groups, whereas the Mekong River Delta is overrepresented in the poor-group. The North West and Central Highlands [1] are two regions where most of the poor live.

61. The most recent survey data that is publicly available for Viet Nam’s Multidimensional Poverty Index (MPI) is from 2014. According to this, 38% of Vietnamese households are multidimensionally poor; they are “multidimensionally poor” with an additional 4.3% live near multidimensionality line (3.930 thousand people). The MPI for the country as a whole is 52%, which means that about half of Viet Nam’s population lives in poverty, and the share of the population living in multidimensional poverty is 39.9%. The MPI, which is the share of the population that is multi-dimensionally poor (adjusted for severity), has increased from 40% in 2010 to 52% in 2014 (UNDP, 2016). Although tens of millions of Vietnamese households have incomes above the official poverty line, they are still considered multidimensionally poor because their household members suffer under a multi-dimensional definition (Delfos, 2017).

62. According to the World Bank’s Gini index which measures economic inequality in Viet Nam has increased from 35.7 to 38.7 over the 20-year period from 1992 to 2012. However, these figures may underestimate the true extent of inequality because income and consumption data on the assets or expenditures of the rich are under-reported and under-captured in household surveys; therefore, emphasis should be placed on other measures such as the Gini coefficient for wealth. The choice of measurements may provide different pictures of inequality in Viet Nam.

63. The report by the World Bank (2015) on gender equality in Viet Nam pointed out that the relation between gender and household poverty is not clear because poverty conditions are measured at household level, which means that women’s poverty is often masked or hidden behind different household structures, such as between the households headed by women and the households headed by men of the ethnic minority households and Kinh households. In addition, the report also expressed that there was a lack of data on the gendered division of labor in Viet Nam. Women are more than children and elderly people are more likely than exclusively working age adults to live in relatively poor households. The report also pointed out that women’s poverty is often masked or hidden behind their role as caregivers, which is clearly related to dependency ratio [1]. Women are also more likely to be poor, particularly if they are older and/or have a disability. There was no evidence of a gendered division of labor in Viet Nam; there were no differences in other gender indicators pertaining to health or investment in children’s education.

64. The report by the World Bank (2015) on gender equality in Viet Nam also pointed out that discrimination against women in various aspects of economic and social life is still of concern. Women are disadvantaged compared with men in terms of access to resources, opportunities for education and capacity development and in many aspects of life as a result of gender norms that relegate them

The role of mother and household. Today, women remain disadvantaged compared to men, despite the fact that there is a legal framework which supports gender equality. Men dominate control over land and other valuable assets; most land tenure certificates are issued under the name of a male household head. Women have limited access to credit for agricultural production and face a lack of women's rights in cases of divorce or inheritance. Men typically make the decisions about household resources, including food security and nutrition. Women also face discrimination when it comes to access to credit and investment opportunities. Small-scale farmers, in particular women, face unequal access to land, inputs, extension services, credit, markets, and technology.

Women contribute significantly to food production and to agricultural and rural economies, but it is impossible to verify empirically the share produced by women. Female farmers, for example, play a key role in producing staple food products, but are frequently unrecognized as economic actors, both at household level and in value chains.


## 4.5 Health and waterborne disease

The improvement in health indicators for women has been remarkable, but the problems of HIV/AIDS and gender-based violence are still significant with women in Viet Nam continuing to be victims of these two major public health challenges. The prevalence rate of HIV among women is 0.6%, of married women have experience with at least one form of physical violence. The rate of women ever experiencing sexual violence was 12%. Women also face unequal access to health services.

Disadvantaged groups face unequal burdens in the health financing system. In 2015, the total planned budget for health care spending was US$384 million (VND7 trillion), an increase from VND69 billion in 2011 to VND17/1 billion (55.8%). Total health expenditures as a percentage of GDP increased from 4.9% in 2011 to 5.4% in 2015. The share of out-of-pocket payments for total health budget rose from 8.6% in 2011 to 9.4% in 2015. However, as much as 90% of the health budget is reserved for routine services and preventive care, which are provided free at the point of service. These have made little progress in improving efficiency, particularly regarding the cost of medical services. Veterans and people with disabilities also face significant financial barriers to accessing health care due to high out-of-pocket payments.

The value of out-of-pocket increased from 43% in 2012 to 48% of total health expenditure. The share of out-of-pocket payment is higher among poor households (including many households [notably women, the rural poor and ethnic minorities] at significant risk of improvement. This rate has been increasing since 2012. In Viet Nam, the Ministry of Health (MoH) has recognized that high out of pocket levels have led to catastrophic health expenditure in many countries, in Viet Nam, the rates of catastrophic health expenditure (an out-of-pocket payment for health care, which exceeds 40% of a household's total income or assets) increased from 12.5% during 1992–2002, especially among disadvantaged populations such as the poor people, with low educational attainment and ethnic minorities.

According to Minh Hoang Van and Phuong Nguyen Th (2013), 583,724 Vietnamese households were partially or completely unable to pay for health care in 2012. The share of catastrophic health expenditure is due to the absence of evidence-based policy development, effective health administration (including the lack of a national health insurance system) and weak social protection. Health insurance coverage has increased over time, up to 65% in 2012 and 75% in 2015. However, there are still significant gaps between urban and rural areas, which leads inevitably to inequities in access to health care and reliance on out-of-pocket payments.

### Employment and gender issues

#### 4.6 Employment and gender issues

The 2011 Viet Nam Country Gender Assessment notes that the structure of the Vietnamese economy has shifted from agriculture to industry and services, with a corresponding increase in female labour market integration. However, the report highlights persistent gender segmentation of the labour market, with women concentrated in lower-paying jobs and in less secure employment relationships. Women are also more vulnerable to working conditions and pay, reflected for example in women's much higher share of informal workers.

#### 4.7 Political Participation and Decision Making

In political leadership, participation is another important area where gender differences emerge. Even though representation of women in the National Assembly is high by regional standards. There are signs that women's representation has been declining since 2016. The report also notes that women's representation in some areas for example the 2011-2016 National Assembly term, got slightly lower from 27.7% to 2005/2007 to 24.4% at 2011 and 26.90% in term 2016 - 2021.

### Land rights

#### 4.8 Land rights

In Viet Nam, land is the property of the "tenure people" and is allocated or leased to the State for organizations, households or individuals for use terms ranging from 20 years (for agricultural land) to 50 years (for forest land) or indefinitely (residential land). At household level, according to the regulations of the Land Law 2013, the land use rights will be granted to both wife and husband, and both

However, the Viet Nam Household Living Standards Survey 2014 showed that nationwide, one in four households are administered/managed/headed by women (equal to 26.54%), compared to 73.46% of households headed by men. In addition, the survey also found that only about 8% of land use rights were granted to the heads of households, with the majority being men.

### 4.9 Gender-based violence

#### 4.9.1 Definition and prevalence
Gender-based violence (GBV) is a complex problem with roots in attitudes and behaviors deeply engrained in culture that are difficult to change. GBV refers to a wider set of issues than domestic violence and can take many forms, including sexual abuse, physical abuse, emotional abuse, economic abuse, and neglect.

#### 4.9.2 Prevalence
According to the national survey on domestic violence against women done by ILO in 2010, 58% of married women have experienced with at least one form of physical violence from their partner during the last 12 months. The most common forms are slapping and kicking (37%), followed by four forms of gender-based violence from their partner during the last 12 months. The rate of women experiencing emotional abuse was also high, with 54% reporting that they had experienced emotional abuse (see table below).

#### 4.9.3 Risk factors
The sex ratio at birth imbalances are a strong indicator of sex selective abortions, whereby female fetuses are selectively terminated in order to have sons. The sex ratio at birth is currently significantly imbalanced, with 1:112 to make every 100 females born in Vietnam (ILO, 2010). Cumulative gender differences in sex ratio at birth between different ethnic groups, with the Khmer minority demonstrating a sex ratio of 135 males per 100 females. The sex ratio at birth imbalance was higher among better-educated women, with an imbalance of 1:138 to make every 100 females born in Vietnam (ILO, 2010). This is consistent with the findings from other studies on sex ratios.

#### 4.9.4 Regional differences
The regional differences were also noted, with the Red River Delta in northern Viet Nam demonstrating the sharpest imbalance of 1:138 to make every 100 females born (ILO, 2010).

Human trafficking is less common with a few large-scale studies and fragmented data from government sources. Between 2005 and 2009, almost 6,000 women and children were identified as

### Victims of trafficking in Viet Nam: Trafficking in men, however, remains largely unknown.

Further difficulties in estimating the prevalence of human trafficking in Viet Nam are caused by the lack of recognition of self-identified and self-rescued victims of trafficking. Viet Nam’s National Steering Committee on Combating Human Trafficking (NSCCT) estimates that 10% of all Vietnamese trafficking victims were repatriated by foreign governments or NGOs, while 120 victims were self-identified and rescued in 2013. The NSCCT also estimated the number of Vietnamese women victims of trafficking with 950 perpetrators and 1,080 men, women, boy and girl victims and identified (Ministry of Public Security, 2014). However, these numbers are not considered to be a complete picture by any. In 2012, the Supreme People’s Court stated that it had prosecuted 496 defendants in relation to human trafficking cases, but only 35% were Vietnamese nationals (Ministry of Public Security, 2014). Women go to Viet Nam but only represent a minority of cases given the vast majority have not been identified by authorities.

### 8. Preference for sons leads to men outnumbering women and an increased ‘pull factor’ to supply external demand for sex work. The preference for sons in Viet Nam is one of the most common causes cited as a preference for sons favours men, is one of the most common destinations from Viet Nam for women seeking employment or marriage. Women are also more likely than men to be involved in sex work put women at increased risk of being trafficked for sex work to other parts of Viet Nam or other countries. Women trafficked for marriage are also vulnerable to domestic violence because they are separated from their families and communities, and are often unable to speak the local language due to seeking help and with little or no economic independence. Gender inequalities perpetuate both ‘push’ (economic) and ‘pull’ factors (social and cultural) that drive human trafficking in Viet Nam.

### 4.10 Ethnic minority issues

#### 81. Viet Nam has 54 ethnic groups in which, Kinh is the majority group, accounting for 85.9% of the total population. The remaining 14.1% of ethnic minority groups make up 14.60%. The total population of ethnic minority people is 13.92 million, with 57.2 million males (equal to 50.21%) and 8.66 million females (equal to 49.79%). The largest ethnic minorities are the Kinh (50.21%), followed by the Viet (17.96%), Thai (1.72%), Mường (1.39%), Khmer (1.29%), Mong (1.25%) and Hmong (1.24%). The largest ethnic minorities are concentrated in the north, central highlands, and south of the country. There are 80 ethnic groups living in Viet Nam, including: Ngái (806); La (73); Pu Pheo (736); Ro Măm (453); Bru (906); and O Du (406). Ethnic minorities live mostly in hamlets and villages of 1.433 communes nationwide, etc.

#### 82. The Kinh majority group accounts for approximately 85% of the population. Kinh tend to live in delta areas and have a higher living standard than other ethnic minorities. Hoa (Chinese) are also a better off group also living in delta areas. Thus, Hoa are often grouped together with Kinh and may face ethnic discrimination. The remaining 14.1% of ethnic minority groups make up the remaining ethnic diversity groups. Ethnic minorities make up less than 15% of the country’s population, but account for up to 70% of the entire poor population.

#### 83. According to the 2014 survey conducted by MOLISA, the incidence of poverty among ethnic minority households is significantly higher (69%) compared with Kinh households (45%). Ethnic minority children are facing higher poverty risks (about 62–78%) than Kinh or Hoa children (24–28%). In 2014, for households headed by an ethnic minority in Vietnam, the probability of being in the bottom quintile

### Ethnic Minorities and Gender Inequality

Paradoxically, the largest gender poverty gap is to be found among the better off Kinh/Cham minority ethnic groups. This suggests that the relationship between ethnicity and gender inequality can be complex and may be manifested in a different extent across various cultures and circumstances (World Bank 2011)[3].

## Access to Health Services

### Lower Access to Health Services Among Ethnic Minorities

Lower access to health services among ethnic minorities is endemic, caused by factors such as an increasing reliance on out-of-pocket spending, a bureaucratic health system, ethnic discrimination, and internal features of minority groups (such as patriarchy, religion, and workfare). Women in ethnic minority households are more likely than women from majority-ethnicity families to miss prenatal care. Studies show that increasing accessibility to quality service for other minorities is not enough; it must be accompanied by increased awareness, opportunity costs, language barriers, mistrust, rituals, culture, taboos, and network habits[4].

## Employment and Ethnic Minorities

### Employment of Ethnic Minorities in Agriculture

86% of ethnic minority people employed in agriculture is very high at 81.4%, twice as high as the national average (39%). The majority of ethnic minorities are concentrated in groups that are employed in agriculture. Women of 50 ethnic minority groups work more in the agricultural sector than men, and this is a reflection of their traditional role in the family. The employment structure of ethnic minorities is mostly linked to agriculture (cultivation, husbandry) and forestry. In agriculture/forestry production, ethnic minority people still apply traditional production techniques[5].

### Traditional Farming Practices

Traditional farming practices are taught to children in early years. When they reach adolescence, they can participate in the family farm work. The proportion of ethnic minorities who have never worked on the land is very high compared to that of the Kinh ethnic group.

### Employment and Ethnic Minorities in Forestry

The share of ethnic minority workers employed in forestry is 10% lower than the national average (28%). This base reached 9.7%, roughly one third the national average (23.4%) or the percentage of Kinh (36.3%). The share of ethnic minorities who have never worked on the land is very high compared to that of ethnic minority men (10.5%) and far below the share of Kinh women employed in industry (21.7%).

### Employment and Ethnic Minorities in Industry

In terms of employment, ethnic minorities are underrepresented in industrial activities among ethnic minority workers. The educational and technical qualifications of employed ethnic minority workers aged 30 years or older were lower than those of majority-ethnicity groups. Only 45% of ethnic minority workers aged 15 and older who underwent technical training nationwide was 19.0%, which is 3.5 times higher for Kinh women (68%) and men (72%). This proportion of ethnic minorities undergoing professional technical training (5.72%) is not significantly different from that of ethnic

### 4.14 Political participation

Currently, there are 923,599 party members who are ethnic minorities in communes in ethnic minority areas nationwide, accounting for 36.1% of total party members of the respective provinces. ## 5.3 Current gender mainstreaming capacity

103. According to the Law on Gender Equality, the People’s Committee is the state agency for gender equality. The People’s Committee has its own organizational structure at three levels (Provincial, District and Commune) with a clear division of responsibilities. The provincial level is responsible for the implementation of gender policies and focusing on the implementation of the Law on gender equality and the National Action Plan on gender equality 2016–2020. In addition to this responsibility, it has also developed the provincial plans of action on gender equality for the five project provinces (Ninh Thuan, Dak Lak, Kon Tum, Gia Lai and Ben Tre). The district level is responsible for implementing their own plans of action based on requests and guidance from the province level for district level. The same process in the commune level. In addition to this responsibility, the communes have developed their plans of action based on requests and guidance from the district level.

104. The Plan of Action on Gender Equality developed by the People’s Committee of Ninh Thuan aimed at achieving five goals: (1) increase the proportion of women in leadership positions; (2) reduce gender gaps in labour, economic, and employment, and strengthen access of rural women to market and employment; (3) improve quality of the female labour force and enhance their capacity for self-employment and entrepreneurship; (4) reduce gender inequality in access to health care services; (5) ensure equal gender rights in access to information and culture activities. The Plan also identified specific solutions and assigned responsibilities at provincial, district and commune levels.

105. Ninh Thuan People’s Committee has issued the provincial action plan on gender equality for the period 2016–2020, under Decision 1059/ND-UBND, dated 4 May 2016 and Plan 733/NH-UBND dated 8 September 2016. The provincial action plan was submitted to the People’s Committee of Ninh Thuan for approval on the day to implement the national action plan on gender equality for the period 2016–2020, 20 May 2016. The provincial action plan is a general guideline and will be followed by each district over the tasks for all relevant agencies at provincial level. Under the direction of this decision all districts of the province will develop their own plans on gender equality. The districts and communes will select and identify the same objectives and target indicators.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

106. The Khmer Huu People's Committee has issued Decision B35/2Q-LEND, dated 3 April 2017, to implement the national action plan on gender equality for the period 2017 – 2020. This decision also identified seven specific objectives, targets and indicators on gender equality that need to be achieved within this period.

107. The Binh Thuan People's Committee has issued Decision 159/34Q-LEND, dated 27 March 2017, and Decree No. 68/2017/QD-BTC, dated 2 April 2017, to implement the national action plan on gender equality. This plan of action also highlighted seven objectives and the roles and responsibilities of all relevant agencies.

108. At the same time, DARD is an agency whose mission is to support the People's Committees to implement the plans for gender equality at the provincial level. This agency has its organizational structure at three levels: provincial, district, and commune. The provincial directorate is the body who is responsible for all activities there. At provincial level, DARDs have limited capacity to provide consultation services on gender equality issues in relation to agricultural extension activities at local levels. This effort is limited to development of general guidelines on gender policy implementation. It also awareness-rasing-activities on gender equality for relevant agencies from provincial, district and commune levels.

109. Most staff and leaders assigned to do gender work have been trained on gender equality concepts. However, even so, many still lack skills to identify gender issues (gender analysis skills) in specific professional areas. This is especially true at the provincial level where there are few women. There is a high need to fill this gap to make sure that these two agencies can perform state management tasks efficiently.

## 5.4 Gender mainstreaming capacity of DARD

110. The Department of Agriculture and Rural Development (DARD) has a specific mission to promote agricultural sustainability and productivity at provincial and district levels. This agency represents the state authorities in providing technical assistance for agricultural extension activities, including helping to identify suitable crops and livestock for each location by considering climate change adaptation and market demand. However, DARDs have not been able to provide adequate support to farmers who are reluctant to invest in agricultural production. Existing climate change adaptive solutions are largely spontaneous, without explicit guidance or orientation from specialized agencies like DARD.

111. According to the results of the gender assessment on gender equality and advancement of women within the organization. While the results of the gender assessment show that the action plan on gender equality and advancement of this agency is available, there is a lack of capacity at provincial level for DARDs to implement it effectively. This is especially true among staff and leaders of this organization at all levels. Most of the technical staff and leaders from DARD at provincial level have not been trained in gender analysis skills.

112. DARD lacks skills to identify gender inequality issues when designing and implementing agriculture extension activities. They also do not have the abilities to identify and consider the differences between men and women in terms of access to resources, knowledge, technology, and other services. The DARDs also do not have experience to identify difference between men and women in access to and control over agricultural resources. This is because they are not trained on gender analysis skills and not recognize men and women as impacted differently by social factors. Therefore, it is essential to strengthen the capacity of technical staff at provincial level for DARD to be able to identify the difference between men and women in needs and concerns associated with agricultural production. It is therefore necessary that this agency should have a training program on gender analysis skills need for building gender analysis and mainstreaming capacities for the technical staff if this agency.

# Annex XIII (c) – GENDER ASSESSMENT AND ACTION PLAN

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

113. Under the DARD, there is one specific technical division, which is responsible for agriculture extension activities (Centre for Agriculture Extension Office). Most of the leaders and technical staff from this division are men or women with little or no formal education in gender issues. As a result, the specific knowledge and experience of women, who are target groups of agriculture promotion activities, have not been considered when planning and implementing activities. Without gender sensitivity, most of the technical staff do not know how to identify gender equality issues in agricultural extension activities and maximize knowledge and experience of women and men nor do they know how to consider the differences between male and female farmers in their work. This is a major challenge for success. In these agencies, leaders and staff members need to deepen their knowledge of gender concepts and gender mainstreaming.

114. In summary, the gender equality has been known by DARD's leaders since the Law on Gender Equality came into effect in 2007, but there still is a gap in gender knowledge and skills among technical staff at all levels. Most of the technical staff are men or women with little formal education in gender equality in agriculture work, and they do not have skills to identify gender issues and how to address these issues while planning and implementing agricultural extension activities. During the gender mainstreaming training, it is important to guarantee raising on gender equality and skills building on gender analysis and gender mainstreaming.

### 5.5 Gender mainstreaming capacity of the Women Union

#### 115. The Viet Nam Women's Union (VWU) is a mass organization which is structured in four organizational levels: national, provincial, district, and commune. Its main role is to protect the legitimate rights and interests of women. At provincial and district levels, the gender assessment and action plan has been implemented since 2013. The Women's Union at the five project provinces have better knowledge and understanding of gender concepts in comparison with other agencies. However, the capacity for gender mainstreaming among the staff of these project provinces still lacks skills to identify gender equality issues in agriculture activities, and they also do not have sufficient experience in conducting communication and awareness raising activities regarding professional activities. Due to the lack of information and experience related to climate change adaptation and mitigation, they are not able to maximize their role in promoting gender equality in agriculture activities at the moment.

#### 116. In addition, the provincial Women's Union has experience in implementing livelihood models for women farmers with different levels of education and skills. The Women's Union at Ninh Thuan province Women's Union has experience in implementing livelihood models to cope with adapt to climate change effects. Ninh Thuan Women's Union also has experience in mainstreaming gender into livelihood models, which is a good example that can be replicated by other provinces. However, this experience in conducting communication and awareness raising activities on these issues. They still lack skills for promoting women's participation in climate change adaptation, especially related to agricultural production activities.

#### 117. The Provincial Women's Union has experience in mainstreaming small-scale savings and credit models into livelihoods that play an important role in coordinating with local commercial and policy banks to enable female members to access loans to invest in agricultural production activities. Most of the projects only provide loans and do not have any technical support for borrowers to use the loans effectively.

118. Due to resource limitations, the Women's Union lacks access to secure funding to access loans. They furthermore lack experience in providing training to women on additional skills such as knowledge and experience in using loans and a fear of taking risks. Consequently, many women do not dare to

119. The mission of the provincial Women’s Union is to protect the rights and benefits of women. The Women’s Union aims to address women’s issues rather than focus on gender issues (both of women and men) in relation to specific roles and responsibilities of men. 120. Despite the fact that VWU is the standing member of the committee for the advancement of women at all levels (provincial, district and commune) they have a weak voice in local Government decision-making procedures.

### 5.6 Knowledge and experience of women and men in climate change

**121. Most of the men and women interviewed during the gender assessment in the three provinces shared that they are aware of the negative effects of climate change related to their agricultural production and daily life. In these interviews, drought was mentioned quite a lot. Dry spells usually occur between April and June and can last for several months. These dry spells reduce water availability and lead to abandoned production land.**

**122. While both men and women are aware of negative impacts of climate change on their agricultural production and daily life, there is little knowledge and women to overcome the scarcity of water resources, especially during the dry season. The most popular actions done by men and women include irrigation using wells, rainwater harvesting, and construction of small-scale water conservation systems. There are a few households with better economic conditions who have applied these systems. This survey does not show any pilot or near poor households applying these systems in the project areas.**

**123. Television is the most popular instrument that has helped local people to access public information on climate change and adaptation strategies. The majority of women interviewed in the project provinces showed that most interviewed women and men in the project areas have access to weather related radio programmes. However, there are few households who receive information about production activities, such as irrigating, fertilizing or drying agricultural products. Many had heard the term “climate change” but did not know what it meant. The majority of interviewed women and men also receive information on water-saving models through television, but it is difficult for them to apply these models because of high cost. Additionally, these models are only suitable for farmers with compact production land.**

**124. The results of the in-depth interviews with men and women also showed that there are two main reasons for farmers not applying water-saving models in practice, including (i) the main sources of water are rainwater and wells which are not sufficient to meet their needs; and (ii) the cost of the water saving model is quite high while this mode is only suitable for some kinds of crops (e.g. vegetables). The majority of interviewed women and men also showed that they have limited knowledge and understanding of water-saving solutions for farmers. Women often have less access to information on climate change and water-saving models than men because women are more likely to be busy with household activities.**

### 5.7 Gender labour division in agricultural activities

**125. There is a hidden gender norm that affects the division of labour between men and women in most agricultural households, especially in the project area where crops include coffee and pepper. Both men and women are involved in these crops, but they divide the tasks based on**


---
**Page 173**
---

# Annex XIII (c) – GENDER ASSESSMENT AND ACTION PLAN

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL


rule that men automatically take on the heavier work and women the lighter tasks. To explain this division, local people do not conceive the issue of "who does what" in the family. For them, it is simply that each person should do one thing and whoever does something better from their experience should continue to do so. This division of labour is also a consequence of men being physically stronger and more suited for housework rather than for job opportunities outside the village, while men being stronger are also more likely to be involved in wage work. Women, on the other hand, are incapable of recognizing the inequality in the current division of labour in agriculture-related activities.


In coffee-growing areas, men take up most of the work, especially the heavier tasks. Women are also involved in some of these tasks but they do not have a share in decision-making regarding planting and drying while men focus more on planting, pruning, digging drainage around the roots; watering and grinding activities. (Table 5: Gender Division of Labour in coffee growing)


126. The division of labour between men and women in pepper growing is quite similar to coffee growing. Men tend to take heavier tasks while women take lighter ones. Both men and women are involved in most of the tasks but there is a clear division of labour with men taking care of planting, weeding so as making pillars for pepper plants, spraying pesticides and watering while women take tasks such as clearing grass, fertilizing, pruning and drying. Men and women have an equal share in harvesting and selling product.

## 128. In agricultural production in the project target areas, water pumping and irrigation, in general, are considered as heavy work. Thus, it has been done by men so far. Women are reluctant to take on this work because they have other responsibilities such as cooking, cleaning, taking care of children, etc., while men are responsible for irrigating crops within their family and men also manage irrigation in the community. In the family, women are also involved in watering crops, but this task is considered as heavy work and women do not like to take on it. Women who live in rural areas and have no land or livestock and are not involved in buying or preparing the equipment, digging wells or ponds, watering crops, etc.


## 129. Irrigation-related work, including such as planning, building, and maintaining are led by men. This is one of the reasons why women do not participate in irrigation projects. As a result, women have limited knowledge and experience in managing and coordinating water resources for agriculture. Therefore, their role in decision-making and contribution of women in this work are minimal.


### 5.8 Community participation in social groups


## 130. The result of baseline survey of the WEIDAP project showed that 77% of respondents belong to Farmers Associations (FA). This survey also found a slight difference between social groups participating in irrigation projects and those not participating. For example, women are less involved in the area. Religious groups and Women's Unions are other organizations where farmers participate with at 18.6 and 20%, respectively. The baseline survey showed that only 3% of respondents belong to the water users' groups, cooperatives, interest groups, agricultural extension groups or savings groups.


## 131. The baseline survey also showed that Farmer Associations and Women's Unions are organizations where most female-headed households participate. Indeed, if the participation strategy of the project is well-designed, it will be possible to increase women's participation in irrigation projects. By doing so, local communities could gain significant social capital, in their capacity to work together to manage this important resource, which they could then apply addressing other social and developmental problems.

### Participation of local people, especially women in community meetings is limited.
Both men and women participate in community meetings on a regular basis, but women are more prevalent in minority areas. Men are predominant in community meetings both in terms of quantity and quality of participation. Women are less likely to participate when husbands are present or when they are burdened with housework. Women only participate in community meetings when husbands are busy. When participating, women tend to be more involved in discussions about household issues than serious concerns. However, women are less likely to influence the decision-making process on water management and other related matters.

### The Women's and Farmers Unions are two agencies who have established and maintained farmer groups that provide a space for local people to learn and share specific information.
The Women's Union has organized 100 women into 25 groups. These groups are mainly focused on environmental protection; voluntary saving associations and credit groups. The Farmers Unions have organized 37 farmers into 14 groups. These groups are mainly focused on agricultural production with high potential for strengthening popular participation. However, currently, no farmer group has yet been established for economic assistance. Each commune has at least one cooperative group providing service to agriculture production for farmers, but they still lack skills to facilitate local farmer access to markets.

### 5.9 Access to water for production
There are two main sources of water that have been used by farmers for agriculture production activities: primary purpose from reservoirs/irrigation canals as the major source of almost all water use in irrigated production. A secondary source comprises a farmer's own shallow well/pump/bore hole in addition to rainwater harvesting. In some areas, especially in Dak Lak and Dak Nong, local people are forced to invest in extensive piping systems to bring the water to the fields. If the land is located at high altitude, farmers have to pump water from rivers or canals into their fields. The project preparation had heard about the water conservation systems, but there are very few models in practice due to lack of awareness and also because of the pressure from water shortages; so they do not yet consider water conservation models to be a priority.

## In regards to irrigation practice, it can be seen that over 60% of farmers in the project areas in the South West and Central Vietnam regions use flood/ing/hose irrigation method. The most common method applied by indigenous ethnic minority households is flood/ing/hose irrigation with 53.6%, followed by overhead sprinkler irrigation at 17.4%. Overhead sprinkler irrigation was commonly used in Dak Nong province (50% in Cu Ju subproject) and 71% in Dak Mil subproject. In comparison, the most common method applied for micro sprinkler irrigation is the most suitable method for coffee. Another reason can be the cost of equipment. It is notable that 22.2% of indigenous-headed households stated that they apply overhead sprinkler and micro sprinkler irrigation methods.

## Decision making issues

140. Men make most of the important decisions within the family. Women may be consulted, but men make the final decision on how to use money to follow the guidance and direction of men. Women’s roles are often limited to domestic tasks and childcare. Women guard household money, but the final decision of how to use this money is made by men.

### 5.11 Household Liabilities

The figures from the baseline survey of the WEDAP project show that the proportion of surveyed households with a current outstanding debt or liabilities was 75%. Indebtedness of poor households was less common than it is in non-poor households (80% compared to 73%). There was no significant difference between male-headed and female-headed households. The same applies for male-headed households. However, there was disparity in indebtedness among subproject areas (see table S4A.19).

# Annex XIII (c) – GENDER ASSESSMENT AND ACTION PLAN

## Access to climate information

147. The baseline survey also showed that approximately 85% of households borrowed money for agricultural activities. Borrowing for agricultural production purposes is a common reason in all project communities, with the proportion being highest among those who had borrowed money (90%), and lowest among those who had borrowed money (63%). Among those who had borrowed money, 100%, and 97% respectively who had borrowed money cited agricultural activities as their main reason.

### Access to information through television

5.12 Access to climate information through television is the most popular means: In-depth interviews with women and men in the communities show that television is the most popular means of accessing public information, including climate information. Both men and women access weather forecast information from television. They also watch television for other purposes such as entertainment, listening, fertilizing or arranging other daily tasks. They also access some market information through this channel.

### Access to information through seed or fertilizer companies

149. There is a number of means of providing information to local people, which is technical training conducted by seed or fertilizer companies. They sometimes conduct technical training on agricultural production techniques, though the limits are restricted to introduction of new seeds and fertilizers. Very few farmers have access to such training.

### Access to information through the Agriculture Extension Centre – GARDI

150. Every year, the Agriculture Extension Centre – GARDI - also provides some short training courses for local farmers. They provide them the knowledge and skills for planting and caring for coffee and peppers. But these training courses are often limited in numbers of participants due to budgetary constraints.

### Gender and migration

5.12 Migration and gender analysis

151. The gender analysis shows that women have more opportunities to work outside the community, whereas men stay at home to take care of children and implement domestic tasks. Due to the lack of job opportunities in the commune, local people are forced to look for work in other provinces (e.g. Lam Dong, Ho Chi Minh). Among those migrating, male migrants are the majority.

### Gender assessment

153. The gender assessment in the target project communities also showed that drought is an underlying factor driving people to migrate to other provinces. In most target project communes, approximately 50-60% of farmland could not be cultivated due to lack of water. As a result, local farmers are forced to migrate. A common solution by local farmers is to pump water from nearby rivers or lakes. There is no easy solution for the remaining farmland located far from a water source.

153. The gender assessment in the target project communes also showed that drought more seriously affects women than men. As mentioned above, women are expected to stay at home to take care of children, while men easily move to work in other provinces. This


---
